# VeinScript — every sample, full source

> Generated by `tools/gen-video-guide.js`. The companion to `VIDEO-GUIDE.md`: that file is the lesson plan, this one is the complete code of every sample in `samples/`, verbatim, each with what it teaches. When the two disagree about syntax, **this file is right** — it is the code the compiler accepts.

Not included:
- `samples/LANGUAGE-TOUR.vein` — a hand-made digest of the older samples. Its code is in the samples themselves, and some of its comments are stale (they still teach `use` and bare-name events).
- `samples/web_app/shards/Reference.vein` — the `/docs` route of web_app, generated by tools/gen-reference.js from the reference HTML. It is 2,579 lines of documentation text, not teaching code.

---

# Core language

## `samples/demo.vein`

**Demo: the IOP core in one file** — A compact tour of the core syntax: a shared $Health shape whose hp folds by sum, an @Damaged event, an SF, and a Drain shard that drains every #Enemy each tick, emits damage by chance, and marks the dead in settled. It never spawns anything, so it compiles and runs but prints nothing.  
Teaches: bundle / publicator / shared · shape with a folds sum field · event with an included $Shape field and a var member · SF (shard function that emits) · shard schedules: each tick and settled · target query over shape + mark · chance N% · mark (deferred structural change) · sigils $ # @  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: No run command in the header. `veinc run samples/demo.vein --ticks 3` exits 0 with no output (empty world). The output field holds an excerpt of `veinc ir samples/demo.vein` (the Shard Drain subtree), which matches the on-screen excerpt; the full IR is 33 lines. SF hurt is declared but never called (the header says so on purpose). docs/EXAMPLE-PIPELINE.md traces this file's emit through AST, HIR, IR and the backend.

Real output of ``:
```text
`--- Shard Drain
     |--- EachTick
     |    `--- Target $Health #Enemy as self
     |         |--- Assign -= self.Health.hp
     |         |    `--- Int 1
     |         `--- Chance 30%
     |              `--- Emit @Damaged
     |                   |--- Arg amount = Int 5
     |                   `--- Arg victim = Ref self
     `--- Settled
          `--- Target $Health #Enemy as self
               `--- If
                    |--- Cond = Binary <=
                    |    |--- Path self.Health.hp
                    |    `--- Int 0
                    `--- Then
                         `--- Mark self #Dead
```

```vein
// demo.vein — an Identity Oriented Programming sample. Exercises the hard lexer cases
// ($ @ # sigils, 30% percent, virtual newline terminators) and the IOP core:
// shape/shard/target/each tick/settled/emit/mark, folds, and if/else.
// Traced end-to-end in docs/EXAMPLE-PIPELINE.md.

bundle Demo {
    need "Vein.Core"

    publicator DemoCore {
        shared("Hit points and mana for any living identity.")
        shape $Health {
            hp: int folds sum      // many shards may drain hp in one tick; sum the deltas
            mp: int
        }

        shared("Damage applied after mitigation.")
        event @Damaged {
            amount: int
            victim: Entity
            $Health.hp = 0             // pull one field off $Health (defaulted, so optional at emit)
            var note: string = "hit"   // a `var` member — same `=`/required rule as a field
        }
    }

    // An SF never returns a value — it emits events (each auto-tagged with origin/source).
    // A single SF can emit many times, just like a shard. Declared here to show the syntax;
    // samples/functions.vein calls SFs and `fn`s for real.
    SF hurt(victim: Entity, amount: int) {
        emit @Damaged {
            amount: amount
            victim: victim
        }
    }

    shard Drain {
        // The SCHEDULE is outer, the entity `target` query is inner.
        each tick {
            target $Health #Enemy as self {
                self.Health.hp -= 1

                // Inline on purpose: docs/EXAMPLE-PIPELINE.md traces THIS emit through every stage
                // (AST → HIR → IR → backend). See samples/functions.vein for calling an SF.
                chance 30% {
                    emit @Damaged {
                        amount: 5
                        victim: self
                    }
                }
            }
        }

        // settled runs once after the tick's fold contributions reconcile.
        settled {
            target $Health #Enemy as self {
                if self.Health.hp <= 0 {
                    mark self #Dead
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/payload.vein`

**Event payloads: defaults and auto-fill** — Shows three ways a payload gets its values: a field default (@Hit amount = 1), auto-fill of an omitted field from the event being heard (a bridge re-emits @SyncedHit with no body), and plain dot access on the hear binding. The response reports where the event came from via the auto-tagged `from` origin.  
Run: `veinc render samples/payload.vein /`  
Teaches: event field defaults · emit with omitted fields (default or auto-fill from context) · hear ... as binding and plain field access · bridge as a First-Class object · event origin tagging (from.kind / from.name) · render pipeline: @Request in, @Response out  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: The `·` trace lines go to stderr; HTTP 200 and the body go to stdout. The run used MSYS_NO_PATHCONV=1. The file is in tools/check-backend.sh (payload.vein:2).

Real output of `veinc render samples/payload.vein /`:
```text
  · Shard.Source [VF:Shard:Source#1] emits @local.Demo.Hit
  · Bridge.Sync [VF:Bridge:Sync#2] emits @local.Demo.SyncedHit
  · Shard.Report [VF:Shard:Report#3] emits @Response
HTTP 200
amount 1 from Bridge.Sync
```

```vein
// payload.vein — emit payload ergonomics.
//  * event fields can have DEFAULTS (`amount: int = 1`) — optional at emit, override to change.
//  * omitted fields AUTO-FILL from the current context (the event being heard, then locals) by name.
//  * hear reads the payload with plain field access on the binding (no sigil): `s.amount`.
// Run:  veinc render samples/payload.vein /      (body shows the auto-filled amount)

bundle Demo {
    event @Request   { path: string }
    event @Hit       { amount: int = 1 }     // default 1 — omit it to accept the default
    event @SyncedHit { amount: int }         // no default — auto-filled from context on emit
    event @Response  { status: int, body: string }

    shard Source {
        hear @Request as req { emit @Hit }               // amount omitted -> default 1
    }

    bridge Sync {
        hear @Hit as h { emit @SyncedHit }               // amount auto-filled from h (current context)
    }

    shard Report {
        hear @SyncedHit as s {
            emit @Response { status: 200, body: "amount " + s.amount + " from " + s.from.kind + "." + s.from.name }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/events.vein`

**Audience: who may be heard** — Two shards emit @Damage on the same request: Combat carries $Session #Trusted and Sneaky carries nothing. The Auditor's `hear ... audience $Session #Trusted` lets Combat's event through and blocks Sneaky's, and the render trace logs the block.  
Run: `veinc render samples/events.vein /`  
Teaches: First-Class objects carrying shapes and marks (shard X $Shape #Mark) · audience barrier on hear · event origin (from.kind / from.name) · reading carries/audience in veinc ir  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 21  
Notes: Captured with MSYS_NO_PATHCONV=1. The `·` lines are stderr. Also ran `veinc ir samples/events.vein`, and it shows carries=[$Session #Trusted] on Combat and audience=[$Session #Trusted] on the Auditor's hear. In tools/check-backend.sh (events.vein:2).

Real output of `veinc render samples/events.vein /`:
```text
  · Shard.Combat [VF:Shard:Combat#1] emits @local.Demo.Damage
  · Shard.Sneaky [VF:Shard:Sneaky#2] emits @local.Demo.Damage
  · Shard.Auditor [VF:Shard:Auditor#3] emits @Response
  · Shard.Auditor BLOCKS @local.Demo.Damage (audience) from Shard.Sneaky
HTTP 200
audited trusted damage 5 from Shard.Combat
```

```vein
// events.vein — testing the `audience` barrier on `hear`.
// A First-Class object can CARRY shapes/marks: `shard Combat $Session #Trusted { ... }`.
// A `hear ... audience $Session #Trusted` only fires when the EMITTER carries ALL of them.
//
// Here two shards emit @Damage: Combat carries $Session #Trusted, Sneaky carries nothing.
// The Auditor's audience lets Combat's event through and BLOCKS Sneaky's.
// Run:  veinc render samples/events.vein /      (watch the log: one fires, one is blocked)
//       veinc ir     samples/events.vein        (see @carries and @audience)

bundle Demo {
    event @Request  { path: string }
    event @Damage   { amount: int }
    event @Response { status: int, body: string }

    // Carries the credentials the auditor requires.
    shard Combat $Session #Trusted {
        hear @Request as req { emit @Damage { amount: 5 } }
    }

    // Carries nothing — its @Damage should be blocked by the audience barrier.
    shard Sneaky {
        hear @Request as req { emit @Damage { amount: 99 } }
    }

    shard Auditor {
        hear @Damage as d audience $Session #Trusted {
            emit @Response { status: 200, body: "audited trusted damage " + d.amount + " from " + d.from.kind + "." + d.from.name }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/functions.vein`

**fn computes, SF emits** — Defines the two kinds of function, a value-returning `fn` (bonus, recursive fib) and an event-emitting `SF` (announce), then calls them locally and calls shared ones from other bundles through the qualified `*Vein.Math` / `*Vein.Console` paths.  
Run: `veinc run samples/functions.vein`  
Teaches: fn with -> return type and return · SF as a named emit sequence (no return) · recursion · publicator + shared("label") for export · cross-bundle calls via *Author.Bundle.Publicator.name · start @Boot as the entry event · bundle ... by author  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 5  
Notes: `bundle Functions by you` makes the author part of the bundle's name, so the shared fn is reachable as *you.Functions.Api.bonus(…). clamp prints `10`, not `10.0`.

Real output of `veinc run samples/functions.vein`:
```text
streak 3 bonus: 15
streak 99 bonus: 50
fib(10): 55
clamp(99, 0, 10) = 10
— all of the above came from four function calls
```

```vein
// samples/functions.vein — the two kinds of function, and calling them across a bundle boundary.
//     veinc run samples/functions.vein
//
// VeinScript splits functions by WHAT THEY PRODUCE:
//
//     SF   a shard function — EMITS events, returns nothing.   (`-> T` and `return` are errors)
//     fn   a function       — COMPUTES and returns a value.    (callable in any expression)
//
// A shard's behaviour is expressed by emitting, so an SF is a named emit sequence; `fn` is the reusable
// computation you can call inside a condition, a field value, or a `target` loop.
//
// Both are callable from ANOTHER bundle when declared `shared` inside a `publicator` — see the
// *Vein.Math / *Vein.Console calls below. `shared` is the only thing that crosses a bundle boundary.

bundle Functions by you {

    start @Boot { }
    event @Boot { }

    publicator Api {
        // A `fn` returns — so it can be used wherever a value can. This one is `shared`, which means
        // another bundle could call it as *you.Functions.Api.bonus(…).
        shared("Score bonus for a streak, capped at 50.")
        fn bonus(streak: int) return int {
            if streak <= 0 { return 0 }
            if streak > 10 { return 50 }
            return streak * 5
        }

        // An `SF` emits — it names an intent so callers don't hand-assemble the event every time.
        shared("Announce a score to the console.")
        SF announce(label: string, value: int) {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: label + ": " + value }
        }
    }

    // A local `fn` needs no `shared` — that keyword is about crossing bundles, not about being callable
    // here. `fn` may recurse.
    fn fib(n: int) return int {
        if n <= 1 { return n }
        return fib(n - 1) + fib(n - 2)
    }

    shard Scoreboard {
        hear @Boot as b {
            // 1. Call a local SF. It emits; it does not return, so it stands alone as a statement.
            announce("streak 3 bonus", bonus(3))
            announce("streak 99 bonus", bonus(99))

            // 2. Call a local fn in expression position — including recursively.
            announce("fib(10)", fib(10))

            // 3. Call a SHARED fn from another bundle. Resolved and imported at compile time, so this
            //    needs no link step: `clamp` is Vein.Math's, not ours.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "clamp(99, 0, 10) = " + *Vein.Math.Scalars.clamp(99.0, 0.0, 10.0) }

            // 4. Call a SHARED SF from another bundle. Same rule, but it emits instead of returning.
            *Vein.Console.Io.print("— all of the above came from four function calls")
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/loops.vein`

**Four loops, break and continue** — Runs VeinScript's four loops (while, repeat N as i, target over a list, target over identities) and cuts them short with break and continue: while uses both, a capped repeat uses break, and both target loops use continue. Players are brought in run once and reported in a separate each-tick schedule.  
Run: `veinc run samples/loops.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: while · repeat N as i · Index (loop counter) · target <list> as x · target $Shape #Mark as e (identity loop) · break / continue · var and reassignment · words() returns a list · builder with mark (identity template) + bring  
Difficulty 1/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 9  
Notes: The Report shard runs every tick, so with --ticks 2 the two score lines print twice. With --ticks 1 they still print once, so the comment 'This is the second tick' means the phase after run once, not frame 2. RULES.md 28a says words() is interpreter-only, but CSharpBackend.IsPrebuilt now lists words, and this file is in check-backend (loops.vein:2), so that rule text looks stale.

Real output of `veinc run samples/loops.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
while i=1
while i=3
while i=4
repeat r=0 Index=0
repeat r=1 Index=1
repeat r=2 Index=2
capped r=0
capped r=1
word 1 = alpha
word 2 = beta
word 3 = gamma
Ada scored 30
Grace scored 20
Ada scored 30
Grace scored 20
```

```vein
// loops.vein — the four loops, and the two words that cut them short.
//
//   veinc run samples/loops.vein --ticks 2
//
// IN tools/check-backend.sh, and that is the point of it existing.
//
// `break` and `continue` lexed, parsed, lowered to IrBreak/IrContinue and were emitted correctly by the
// C# backend — but the interpreter had no case for either and ran straight past them. The two runtimes
// disagreed about every program that used them, and nothing noticed, because NO SAMPLE USED THEM. The
// backend check diffs the interpreter against the compiled program on each file listed in it, so this
// sample is what makes that class of divergence impossible to reintroduce quietly.
//
// VeinScript has four loops and they are not interchangeable:
//
//   while <cond>          run until the condition goes false
//   repeat N as i         run a counted number of times
//   target $S #M as e     every identity carrying that shape and mark — the identity loop
//   target <list> as x    every element of a list
//
// `break` and `continue` mean the same thing in all four.

bundle Loops by you {

    shape $Score { who: string, points: int }
    mark #Player
    builder Player { $Score   mark #Player }

    shard Counting {
        run once {
            // WHILE, with both words. `continue` skips the rest of this pass; `break` leaves the loop.
            // Printed: 1, 3, 4 — 2 is skipped and 5 stops it before it prints.
            var i = 0
            while i < 10 {
                i = i + 1
                if i == 2 { continue }
                if i == 5 { break }
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "while i=" + i }
            }

            // REPEAT, counted. `as r` names the same number `Index` would give.
            repeat 3 as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "repeat r=" + r + " Index=" + Index }
            }

            // `break` in a counted loop still ends it early — the count is a limit, not a promise.
            repeat 10 as r {
                if r == 2 { break }
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "capped r=" + r }
            }

            // A loop over a LIST. Same statement, same two words.
            var seen = 0
            target words("alpha beta skip gamma") as w {
                if w == "skip" { continue }
                seen = seen + 1
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "word " + seen + " = " + w }
            }
        }
    }

    shard Make {
        run once {
            bring Player("Ada", 30)
            bring Player("Grace", 20)
            bring Player("Alan", 10)
        }
    }

    // A SEPARATE SCHEDULE. A `bring` is a structural change and structural changes commit at the phase
    // boundary (RULES 11/12b), so a `target` in the same block would query the world as it was before
    // the brings and find nothing. This is the second tick.
    shard Report {
        each tick {
            // `continue` inside an IDENTITY loop. Each entity gets its own activation, and skipping one
            // has to end that activation cleanly — a `continue` that left the store mid-activation
            // would corrupt every shard that ran after it in the frame.
            target $Score #Player as p {
                if p.Score.points < 15 { continue }
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: p.Score.who + " scored " + p.Score.points }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/enum_state.vein`

**Enum state machine with match** — A referee identity carries a $Match shape with an enum State { Kickoff, Play, Over }. Each tick advances the clock and the state by comparing enum cases, and a settled shard reads the state back with match, printing the phase every frame.  
Run: `veinc run samples/enum_state.vein --ticks 4`  
Teaches: enum declared inside a shape · enum case as a value (State.Kickoff) · comparing enum cases with == · match / when / else on an enum field · each tick vs settled · builder identity template + bring  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 10  
Notes: In tools/check-backend.sh (enum_state.vein:4). This file supersedes the 'enum case does not work as a value' paragraph in samples/match_orders.vein.

Real output of `veinc run samples/enum_state.vein --ticks 4`:
```text
t1 in play
t2 in play
t3 full time
t4 full time
```

```vein
// enum_state.vein — a state machine written with the feature that exists for writing one.
//
//   veinc run samples/enum_state.vein --ticks 4
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh, because an enum case is a VALUE now and the two runtimes carry values
// differently — the interpreter compares loosely-typed objects, the backend emits typed C#. A state
// machine that advances differently compiled is a game that ends at the wrong moment.
//
// THE BUG THIS PINS. `enum State { … }` inside a shape parsed, and `state: State` was accepted, but
// `State.Kickoff` in value position lowered as a field access on a name that resolves to nothing. So
// it evaluated to EMPTY, which meant:
//
//     m.state == State.Kickoff     // true
//     m.state == State.Over        // ALSO true — every case equalled every other
//     match m.state { when Kickoff … }   // matched nothing at all
//
// A referee written this way compiled, ran, and sat in no state. The workaround was to store the plain
// string "Kickoff" and match bare names — which works, and which meant the enum was decoration.
//
// THE RULE. A case evaluates to its own NAME, which is what the rest of the language already agreed a
// case is: `match` compares an arm against the subject's text, so one change makes a case equal to
// itself, unequal to its siblings, printable, and matchable — and a misspelt case is VS0242 at the
// spelling rather than a state nothing ever reaches.

bundle EnumState by you {

    shape $Match {
        enum State { Kickoff, Play, Over }
        state: State
        clock: int
    }
    mark #Match

    builder Referee { $Match   mark #Match }

    shard Boot { run once { bring Referee(State.Kickoff, 0) } }

    // The referee. Each frame it advances the clock and decides what state the match is in — and it
    // decides by COMPARING CASES, which is the thing that could not be written.
    shard Whistle {
        each tick {
            target $Match #Match as m {
                m.Match.clock = m.Match.clock + 1

                if m.Match.state == State.Kickoff { m.Match.state = State.Play }
                if m.Match.state == State.Play and m.Match.clock >= 3 { m.Match.state = State.Over }
            }
        }
    }

    // …and reading it back by `match`, which is the other half. An `else` arm would have caught every
    // frame before this worked, so its absence from the output is the assertion.
    shard Commentary {
        settled {
            target $Match #Match as m {
                match m.Match.state {
                    when Kickoff { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "t" + m.Match.clock + " awaiting kickoff" } }
                    when Play    { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "t" + m.Match.clock + " in play" } }
                    when Over    { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "t" + m.Match.clock + " full time" } }
                    else         { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "t" + m.Match.clock + " NO STATE" } }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/match_orders.vein`

**match per entity, with and without else** — Seeds four orders with string statuses, then three independent shards each run their own target loop over the same orders with a different match. Advance moves states forward and ignores unknown ones (no else), Report answers for every row (with else), and Ops prints warehouse actions for two states only.  
Run: `veinc run samples/match_orders.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: match on a string field · when arms match by name · else arm vs deliberately no else · several shards iterating the same entities · state advancing across ticks  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: STALE HEADER COMMENTS, do not quote on camera: (1) 'A REAL enum CASE DOES NOT WORK AS A VALUE TODAY' is contradicted by samples/enum_state.vein, which uses State.Kickoff as a value and is in check-backend. (2) 'INTERPRETER ONLY… match has no C# emission / [unsupported: IrMatch]' is contradicted by src/Vein.Compiler/Backends/CSharpBackend.cs (~line 823), which now emits IrMatch and whose comment says 'Emitted at all only now'. The file is still not listed in tools/check-backend.sh. Two header claims still hold: an arm name is not checked against anything (`when Paidd` passes `veinc check` and silently stops gadget advancing), and an undefined name as a bring argument still compiles clean and stores an empty value (`sprocket: status `). It overlaps enum_state.vein, which is the better match demo if only one is shown.

Real output of `veinc run samples/match_orders.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
widget: payment received
gadget: on the way
gizmo: on the way
sprocket: status OnHold
  [ops] pack widget
  [ops] manifest gadget
  [ops] manifest gizmo
widget: on the way
gadget: on the way
gizmo: on the way
sprocket: status OnHold
  [ops] manifest widget
  [ops] manifest gadget
  [ops] manifest gizmo
```

```vein
// match_orders.vein — `match` branching per-entity across SEVERAL `target` loops.
//
//   veinc run samples/match_orders.vein --ticks 2
//
// `match here()` is the well-known use — samples/console_roles.vein and console_broadcast.vein turn it
// into a role switch across several console windows, one program executing differently depending which
// window it is. This file is the OTHER axis: several ENTITIES in one `target` loop, each taking a
// different arm depending on a field it carries. Three independent shards each run their own `target
// $Order #Order as o { match o.Order.status { … } }` over the SAME four orders, and each shard reads a
// different thing out of the same match.
//
// AN ARM MATCHES BY NAME, and that name is compared against whatever the subject stringifies to
// (Interp.ExecMatch). A plain `status: string` field works exactly like the mark/enum patterns the docs
// show, provided the text stored in it matches an arm's spelling: `bring Order("Paid", …)` then
// `when Paid { … }` compares "Paid" to "Paid" and takes that arm. Nothing links the arm name to a
// declaration — `when Paid` compiles whether or not anything anywhere is called Paid.
//
// A REAL `enum` CASE DOES NOT WORK AS A VALUE TODAY, which is why this file uses plain strings instead
// of the `enum Status { Pending, Paid, Shipped }` LANGUAGE.md §3.4 describes. Measured, not assumed:
// `bring Order(Pending, …)`, `Status.Pending` and `O.Status.Pending` all store `""` — `status=[]`, never
// `status=[Pending]`. `enum` is real as a TYPE DECLARATION and as a `match` PATTERN (`when Pending`
// parses and compares by name whether or not an enum exists); the third leg, a case used as a VALUE,
// was never built. Two facts underneath it, both worth their own decision:
//
//   * `IrEnumRef` — the node that would carry one — is written into IR-SPEC.md §1.4 and exists NOWHERE
//     in src/. The same specified-but-unimplemented drift `ResolvedType` had before Semantics/Resolve.
//   * an unresolved name in an expression is silently null, and this is NOT enum-specific:
//     `bring O(TotallyUndefinedName, "x")` compiles clean and stores `""` too. That general hole is
//     what makes the enum one invisible — there is no diagnostic to notice either.
//
// INTERPRETER ONLY, and it degrades QUIETLY rather than loudly, which is the sharper reason this file is
// not in tools/check-backend.sh. `match` has no C# emission, so each block becomes a comment:
//
//     // [unsupported: IrMatch]
//
// The generated file then COMPILES and prints nothing where the interpreter prints four lines. A note is
// emitted per skipped statement, so the contract's Degrade Explicitly holds — but the failure to expect
// is silence, not a build error. Compare samples/json_roundtrip.vein, which genuinely does not compile:
// there the missing emission is a `fromJson` whose VALUE something needs, so the hole cannot be a
// comment.

bundle MatchOrders by you {

    shape $Order { status: string, item: string }
    mark #Order

    builder Order { $Order   mark #Order }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            bring Order("Pending", "widget")
            bring Order("Paid",    "gadget")
            bring Order("Shipped", "gizmo")

            // A status NO arm below names. Nothing is wrong with this order and nothing here rejects
            // it — it is what "on hold, awaiting a human" looks like, and it is the row that shows what
            // happens when a `match` finds no arm.
            bring Order("OnHold",  "sprocket")
        }
    }

    // ---- 1. match DRIVES STATE, not just a print ----------------------------------------------------
    //
    // One order at a time, in ITS OWN loop: `target` activates each entity separately, so one order's
    // `status = …` is that order's fold contribution and does not touch any other order's row
    // (docs/RULES.md 13). `each tick` so re-running with `--ticks 2` shows a second step — widget moves
    // Pending → Paid on frame 1 and Paid → Shipped on frame 2, which the two runs below make visible.
    //
    // `OnHold` matches NOTHING here and there is no `else` arm, so it is left exactly where it was —
    // silently, the same way samples/console_roles.vein's workers "match no arm and do nothing" for a
    // timer that is not theirs. That silence is what makes an `else`-less match the right tool for "act
    // only on the states I know how to advance, and leave everything else alone."
    //
    // Writing `else { }` here would run identically and read as a decision that was never made. The
    // absence IS the statement, and `shard Report` below shows the other half: an `else` when the job is
    // to answer for every row rather than to act on the ones you recognise.
    shard Advance {
        each tick {
            target $Order #Order as o {
                match o.Order.status {
                    when Pending { o.Order.status = "Paid" }
                    when Paid    { o.Order.status = "Shipped" }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- 2. the same four orders, a DIFFERENT loop, WITH `else` --------------------------------------
    //
    // A customer-facing line per order. `else` is what makes this block ANSWER FOR EVERY ROW: `OnHold`
    // has no `when` of its own and still prints something, because unlike Advance this shard's job is
    // "say something about every order," not "act on the ones I recognise."
    shard Report {
        settled {
            target $Order #Order as o {
                match o.Order.status {
                    when Pending { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: o.Order.item + ": waiting for payment" } }
                    when Paid    { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: o.Order.item + ": payment received" } }
                    when Shipped { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: o.Order.item + ": on the way" } }
                    else         { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: o.Order.item + ": status " + o.Order.status } }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- 3. a THIRD loop over the same orders, WITHOUT `else` ---------------------------------------
    //
    // Warehouse actions exist only for Paid and Shipped — there is nothing to pack or manifest for a
    // Pending or OnHold order, so no `else` is exactly right here. Run with `--ticks 2` and watch widget
    // leave this list on frame 1 ("pack") and come back changed on frame 2 ("manifest") as Advance moves
    // it along; sprocket (OnHold) never appears, on any frame, because nothing here names its status.
    shard Ops {
        settled {
            target $Order #Order as o {
                match o.Order.status {
                    when Paid    { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  [ops] pack " + o.Order.item } }
                    when Shipped { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  [ops] manifest " + o.Order.item } }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/sigil_names.vein`

**One name, four sigils** — Declares $Switch (shape), #Switch (mark), @Switch (event) and a Switch builder side by side, then brings two switches, emits @Switch for each in settled, hears it, and counts matches, proving the four same-named declarations stay distinct.  
Run: `veinc run samples/sigil_names.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: sigils are part of a name's meaning ($ shape, # mark, @ event, & builder) · builder with shape + mark (identity template) · bring · emit / hear chain · event queue ordering  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 12  
Notes: In tools/check-backend.sh (sigil_names.vein:2). The excerpt is lines 37-76 (to the end of Report); trim to 30 lines (37-66) if screen space is tight.

Real output of `veinc run samples/sigil_names.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
$Switch #Switch matched 2
@Switch says n=1
@Switch says n=0
$Switch #Switch matched 2
@Switch says n=1
@Switch says n=0
```

```vein
// sigil_names.vein — one name, four sigils, four different things.
//
//   veinc run samples/sigil_names.vein --ticks 2
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh, because the two runtimes answer this question with completely different
// machinery and both have got it wrong: the interpreter keeps name→type tables and the C# backend
// emits real types into one namespace. Only a diff proves they agree.
//
// THE RULE. A sigil is part of what a name MEANS, not decoration on it (RULES 14e, SYNTAX-DECISIONS
// D8). So a program may declare all four of these and they are four separate declarations:
//
//     $Switch     a shape   — the data an identity carries
//     #Switch     a mark    — a tag it wears
//     @Switch     an event  — a message about it
//      Switch     a builder — the template that makes one (`&` at the use site)
//
// This is not a curiosity. It is what lets a domain word stay one word: a switch has state, a switch
// can be tagged, flipping a switch is an occurrence, and something builds switches. Forcing four
// spellings — `$SwitchState`, `#SwitchTag`, `@SwitchFlipped`, `SwitchBuilder` — is the language making
// the author work around it.
//
// WHAT WENT WRONG, TWICE, AND WHY THEY LOOKED LIKE DIFFERENT BUGS:
//
//   * `AppLinker` keyed its type table by NAME ALONE, so a module's own Component and Tag matched each
//     other and one bundle was compared against itself: "'Switch' is declared differently in bundles
//     'Solo' and 'Solo'". Latent as a warning; a rejected build once VS0332 became an error.
//   * `Interp.Setup` filtered Tags out of its map for exactly this reason — and then let Components and
//     Messages share it. `shape $Switch` beside `event @Switch` threw a raw ArgumentException out of
//     ToDictionary and killed the interpreter before the program ran a line.
//
// Both were the same mistake in two places: a flat name→thing table for a language where the sigil is
// half the name. Marks need no table at all — they live in the store's own sets — and builders are
// gone by then, desugared into spawn/attach/mark.

bundle SigilNames by you {

    shape $Switch { on: int }
    mark  #Switch
    event @Switch { n: int }

    // The builder. Declared bare and reached as `&Switch` from another bundle; here `bring Switch(…)`
    // names it directly. It carries the shape AND the mark, so `Switch` in this one line refers to
    // three of the four declarations above and means something different each time.
    builder Switch { $Switch   mark #Switch }

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            bring Switch(1)
            bring Switch(0)
        }
    }

    // A `bring` commits at the phase boundary (RULES 11/12b), so the query is a frame later.
    shard Flip {
        settled {
            target $Switch #Switch as s {
                emit @Switch { n: s.Switch.on }
            }
        }
    }

    shard Listen {
        hear @Switch as e {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "@Switch says n=" + e.n }
        }
    }

    // Reading the shape and the mark back together, to show the query still tells them apart after
    // everything above has shared their name.
    shard Report {
        settled {
            var switches = 0
            target $Switch #Switch as s { switches = switches + 1 }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "$Switch #Switch matched " + switches }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/boot.vein`

**start: naming the boot event** — `start @NewGame { seed: 42, players: 2 }` replaces the default @Request boot event with a custom one and its payload. A Kernel shard hears it and answers with an @Response, and `--set` overrides a payload field from the command line.  
Run: `veinc render samples/boot.vein`  
Teaches: start @Event { payload } as entry point · declaring an event payload · hear ... as g and field access · veinc render --set field=value  
Difficulty 1/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Both the default and the --set run were captured. The `·` line is stderr. The '(with --set seed=99)' separator in the output field is mine; everything else is verbatim.

Real output of `veinc render samples/boot.vein`:
```text
  · Shard.Kernel [VF:Shard:Kernel#1] emits @Response
HTTP 200
new game seed=42 players=2

(with --set seed=99)
  · Shard.Kernel [VF:Shard:Kernel#1] emits @Response
HTTP 200
new game seed=99 players=2
```

```vein
// boot.vein — `start` names the boot event + payload, replacing the default @Request { path }.
// The payload is an emit-style body, so `?` fill-the-rest works here too (`start @NewGame ?`).
// Run:  veinc render samples/boot.vein
//       veinc render samples/boot.vein --set seed=99      (override a payload field at boot)

bundle Game {
    start @NewGame { seed: 42, players: 2 }

    event @NewGame  { seed: int, players: int }
    event @Response { status: int, body: string }

    shard Kernel {
        hear @NewGame as g {
            emit @Response { status: 200, body: "new game seed=" + g.seed + " players=" + g.players }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/boot_shared.vein`

**Same boot entry, two bundles** — Two bundles in one file (World and Ui, both `by studio`) each declare their own @Boot { seed, mode = "play" } and their own start, so each boots independently with its own payload. The header warns that the two @Boot events only share a word.  
Run: `veinc render samples/boot_shared.vein`  
Teaches: multiple bundles in one file · start per bundle · event field default (mode: string = "play") · --set overriding every bundle's boot payload · event ownership / name collisions  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: reference  
Notes: Ui never prints its seed, so `--set seed=7` is only visible on World's line. `--set mode=x` shows one override reaching both bundles ('world seed=42 mode=x', 'ui mode=x'). Do not demo the header's qualified fix (`start *studio.Core.@Boot { … }`) as working. In a test file with a `bundle Core by studio` owning @Boot it passes `veinc check` and renders, but the payload default is lost: it prints 'world seed=42 mode=' with an empty mode instead of 'play'. The '(with --set seed=7)' separator in the output field is not program output.

Real output of `veinc render samples/boot_shared.vein`:
```text
  · Shard.Sim [VF:Shard:Sim#1] emits @Response
HTTP 200
world seed=42 mode=play
  · Shard.Screen [VF:Shard:Screen#1] emits @Response
HTTP 200
ui mode=menu

(with --set seed=7)
  · Shard.Sim [VF:Shard:Sim#1] emits @Response
HTTP 200
world seed=7 mode=play
  · Shard.Screen [VF:Shard:Screen#1] emits @Response
HTTP 200
ui mode=menu
```

```vein
// boot_shared.vein — the "same entry everywhere" pattern: a shared @Boot event + payload shape.
// Each bundle has ONE entry (`start @Boot`) with the same payload, so every bundle boots the same way.
//
// CAVEAT: the two @Boot below are SEPARATE events that share a name — nothing forces their payloads to
// match, and a reader can't tell whose types they are. The collision-safe fix is to let ONE bundle own
// @Boot and reference it qualified: `start *studio.Core.@Boot { … }` (parses + `veinc symbols` validates
// it today; full cross-bundle type resolution at runtime lands with app link+run). This file keeps the
// naive duplicated form so it renders standalone.
//
// Run:  veinc render samples/boot_shared.vein
//       veinc render samples/boot_shared.vein --set seed=7     (override every bundle's boot seed)

bundle World by studio {
    event @Boot     { seed: int, mode: string = "play" }   // the shared entry shape
    event @Response { status: int, body: string }

    start @Boot { seed: 42 }                                // mode defaults to "play"

    shard Sim {
        hear @Boot as b { emit @Response { status: 200, body: "world seed=" + b.seed + " mode=" + b.mode } }
    }
}

bundle Ui by studio {
    event @Boot     { seed: int, mode: string = "play" }   // same entry shape as World
    event @Response { status: int, body: string }

    start @Boot { seed: 42, mode: "menu" }                 // same shape, different payload

    shard Screen {
        hear @Boot as b { emit @Response { status: 200, body: "ui mode=" + b.mode } }
    }
}
```

## `samples/bench_folds.vein`

**Benchmark: folds on the hot loop** — The measurement program behind tools/check-perf.sh: 1000 identities spawned by hand, each with a $Health whose hp folds by sum, and two shards (Drain -1, Rot -2) writing that same field every tick. It prints nothing by design.  
Teaches: spawn / attach / mark written out by hand (what a builder desugars to) · repeat N without as · folds sum reconciling two writers in one tick · one-line shards  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: reference  
Notes: The header has no veinc command; it is run through `bash tools/check-perf.sh`. `veinc run samples/bench_folds.vein --ticks 2` (the frame count check-backend uses) exited 0 with no output, as designed. The header calls it 'Not a teaching sample'.

```vein
// bench_folds.vein — the measurement program behind tools/check-perf.sh. Not a teaching sample.
//
// It exists to make ONE number reproducible: the cost of an ACTIVATION — one identity's pass through one
// `target` body in one frame. That is the axis a compiled backend exists to make cheap, and the axis the
// roadmap's "≈10×" claim is about.
//
// Shaped for measuring, which is why it looks unlike the other samples:
//
//   * it PRINTS NOTHING. A `@Print` per entity per frame is I/O, and I/O would dominate the number
//     and hide the thing being measured;
//   * two shards write the same `folds sum` field, so the fold path is on the hot loop rather than
//     beside it — reconciliation is a real per-activation cost, not an extra;
//   * the work per activation is arithmetic on the component, so what is timed is the runtime's
//     dispatch and storage, not a program doing something interesting.
//
// The harness runs it at two frame counts and takes the DIFFERENCE, which cancels process start, JIT and
// world construction — none of which are per-activation costs. Correctness is not this file's job:
// tools/check-backend.sh already diffs five programs covering these same constructs.
//
//   bash tools/check-perf.sh

bundle BenchFolds by you {

    shape $Health { hp: int folds sum }

    shard Spawn {
        run once {
            repeat 1000 {
                let e = spawn()
                attach $Health to e { hp: 1000000 }
                mark e #Mob
            }
        }
    }

    shard Drain { each tick { target $Health #Mob as self { self.Health.hp -= 1 } } }
    shard Rot   { each tick { target $Health #Mob as self { self.Health.hp -= 2 } } }
}
```

## `samples/clock_ticked.vein`

**Moving on the clock: @Ticked** — An Odometer identity is advanced by hearing the stdlib clock event `*Vein.Time.Clock.@Ticked` rather than by `each tick`. It records the event's frame number and accumulates the runtime's fixed delta, and a settled shard prints the elapsed time rounded with Vein.Math.  
Run: `veinc run samples/clock_ticked.vein --ticks 3`  
Teaches: need "Author.Bundle" · hearing a stdlib event by qualified path · @Ticked payload (frame, delta) · each tick vs a clock event · calling a shared stdlib fn (*Vein.Math.Round.roundTo) · float accumulation  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Lines 30-31 have odd formatting (a trailing space after `bring Odometer(0, 0.0)` and the closing `} }` on its own line). It compiles and runs fine. In tools/check-backend.sh (clock_ticked.vein:3). It uses *Vein.Math and *Vein.Console qualified without a `need` for them.

Real output of `veinc run samples/clock_ticked.vein --ticks 3`:
```text
frame 1 at 0.0167s
frame 2 at 0.0333s
frame 3 at 0.05s
```

```vein
// clock_ticked.vein — a game that moves on the CLOCK rather than on `each tick`.
//
//   veinc run samples/clock_ticked.vein --ticks 3
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh, because the two runtimes fire this event from two different places —
// `Interp.RunFrames` calls `FireTicked` directly, the C# backend wires `world.Ticked` to an emit of a
// generated payload type — and a game that moves on the clock moving differently compiled is not a
// difference anybody would spot in a screenshot.
//
// THE BUG THIS PINS. `@Ticked` used to be the WALL CLOCK's alone: a built exe with a real frame loop
// fired it, and `veinc run --ticks N` did not. So N frames ran, N `each tick` blocks ran, and every
// `hear @Ticked` ran zero times — a game booted under the compiler's own runner and did nothing, with
// no diagnostic, because nothing was wrong with it. Every game written against the clock was affected,
// which is most of them, and they were being tested through a host that fires the event instead —
// keeping the whole set out of the differential check that would have caught it.
//
// `each tick` and `@Ticked` are not alternatives. A tick block is THIS bundle's frame work; the event
// is a fact about the world that any bundle can hear, which is how a kit reacts to a frame in a game
// it was not written for.

bundle ClockTicked by you {

    need "Vein.Time"

    shape $Odometer { frames: int, seconds: float }
    mark #Odometer

    builder Odometer { $Odometer   mark #Odometer }

    shard Boot { run once { bring Odometer(0, 0.0) 
} }

    // Driven by the event, not by `each tick`. The delta is the runtime's, so the accumulated time is
    // the frame count times the step and a test can assert it exactly.
    shard Advance {
        hear *Vein.Time.Clock.@Ticked as t {
            target $Odometer #Odometer as o {
                o.Odometer.frames = t.frame
                o.Odometer.seconds = o.Odometer.seconds + t.delta
            }
        }
    }

    shard Report {
        settled {
            target $Odometer #Odometer as o {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: "frame " + o.Odometer.frames + " at " + *Vein.Math.Round.roundTo(o.Odometer.seconds, 4) + "s"
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/diagnostics.vein`

**No try/catch: failure is a message** — Shows what VeinScript has instead of try/catch: a Collector shard hears @DiagnosticRaised from anywhere in the program. Some diagnostics are raised on purpose with info/warn/error SFs, and one comes from a real runtime fault (`5 % 0`) that stops only its own block while the other shards carry on.  
Run: `veinc run samples/diagnostics.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: @DiagnosticRaised as the catch · severity levels 0/1/2 · *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.info/warn/error SFs · runtime faults become events; the block stops, the program lives · event queue ordering · failure events like @Failed / @Undelivered  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 7  
Notes: The header's 'Delete shard Collector below and the same six lines still appear' has the wrong count. With the Collector deleted (tested on a copy), FOUR diagnostics still print in the runtime's own format: `(info: 2 rows to check)`, `(warning: a row has an empty title)`, `(error: a row has rank -1, which is not a rank)` and `(error: Compute (once): Attempted to divide by zero.)`. They print inline, right after the line that raised them, instead of after the plain prints. The live edit works, but say 'four lines' and expect the order to change. The Unreachable shard hears *Vein.Net.Http.@Failed, which never fires here (no HTTP request), so it only illustrates the pattern. The row with an empty title and rank -1 is still brought and printed, because Validate reports but does not reject.

Real output of `veinc run samples/diagnostics.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
-- checking the rows --
-- dividing --
   5 % 2 = 1
   note  | 2 rows to check
   WARN  | a row has an empty title
   ERROR | a row has rank -1, which is not a rank
   ERROR | Compute (once): Attempted to divide by zero.
-- the program is still alive --
   row: [write the docs] rank 3
   row: [] rank -1
```

```vein
// diagnostics.vein — what VeinScript has instead of try/catch.
//
//   veinc run samples/diagnostics.vein --ticks 1
//
// THE FAILURE IS A MESSAGE. There is no block to encircle, and that is not an omission: `bring` and
// `emit` cannot fail at the point they are written. `bring` always spawns (an unknown builder is
// VS0203, at compile time); `emit` always succeeds, it just may reach nobody. Wrapping either would
// guard nothing.
//
// What CAN go wrong is a whole unit of work — a schedule block, one `hear` handler — and the language
// already had the shape for that before this file existed. Vein.Net answers a request that went wrong
// with `@Failed`, and a send that arrived nowhere with `@Undelivered`. The failure arrives as an event
// and you `hear` it, exactly like a success. So:
//
//     C                        VeinScript
//     try { … }                the shard body, unchanged
//     catch (e) { … }          hear *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.@DiagnosticRaised as d { … }
//
// The difference that matters: a `catch` belongs to the code that failed, and a `hear` belongs to
// whoever cares. One shard can collect every diagnostic in the program without the failing shards
// knowing it exists — which is what you want for a log, and impossible with try/catch.
//
// WHERE THE SFs GET CALLED. `*Vein.Diagnostics.Report.info/warn/error(…)` are for what YOUR code
// decides is worth reporting — a row that failed validation, a config that looked wrong. You call them.
// The RUNTIME raises the same event for a fault, so both land in one place and a collector written for
// one gets the other for free.
//
// A DIAGNOSTIC IS NEVER DROPPED. Every other unheard event vanishes — an emit is not addressed to
// anyone. This one prints to the console when no shard hears it, because a diagnostics library losing
// its own diagnostic is the one outcome it must not have. Delete shard Collector below and the same
// six lines still appear, in the runtime's own format.

bundle DiagnosticsDemo by you {

    shape $Row { title: string, rank: int }
    mark #Row

    builder Row { $Row   mark #Row }

    // ---- the collector: this is the `catch` ------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // Declared FIRST so it is reading from the moment anything runs. It belongs to no particular
    // failure, which is the point — every diagnostic in the program arrives here, whether some shard
    // chose to report it or the runtime had to.

    // READ THE OUTPUT ORDER, because it surprises people. The collected lines appear AFTER the plain
    // prints, not interleaved with them. Nothing is out of order: `@Print` is an event too, and this
    // handler answers a diagnostic by emitting ANOTHER event, which joins the back of the same queue.
    // A collector is one hop further from the console than a `@Print` written inline.
    shard Collector {
        hear *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.@DiagnosticRaised as d {
            // severity is an int, not an enum: 0 info / 1 warning / 2 error. stdlib/Diagnostics.vein
            // says why — a cross-bundle enum needs link-time support that does not exist yet.
            if d.severity == 0 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   note  | " + d.message } }
            if d.severity == 1 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   WARN  | " + d.message } }
            if d.severity == 2 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   ERROR | " + d.message } }
        }
    }

    // ---- 1. diagnostics you raise yourself -------------------------------------------------------

    shard Validate {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- checking the rows --" }

            bring Row("write the docs", 3)
            bring Row("", -1)

            // Nothing here FAILED. The program decided these rows are wrong, which is a judgement no
            // runtime can make for you — and exactly what the SFs are for.
            *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.info("2 rows to check")
            *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.warn("a row has an empty title")
            *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.error("a row has rank -1, which is not a rank")
        }
    }

    // ---- 2. a fault the runtime raises for you ---------------------------------------------------

    shard Compute {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- dividing --" }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   5 % 2 = " + (5 % 2) }

            // `% 0` genuinely throws, and this is the whole demonstration:
            //
            //   * the line below does not print — the BLOCK stops, not the statement. Resuming
            //     mid-block would leave the world in a state no source line describes.
            //   * the message names the shard AND the phase (`Compute (once)`), because "something
            //     threw" is useless and "which unit of work died" is not.
            //   * shard Carry_on below still runs. One broken unit does not take the program.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   5 % 0 = " + (5 % 0) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   THIS LINE NEVER PRINTS" }
        }
    }

    // `settled`, not `run once`, and the difference is load-bearing: the rows are brought in Validate's
    // `run once`, and a `bring` commits at the END of its phase (docs/RULES.md 12b). A `target` in
    // another `run once` block would run BEFORE that commit and print nothing at all — which looks
    // exactly like "the fault destroyed the rows" and is nothing of the kind. Written the wrong way
    // first, and the empty output is what caught it.
    shard Carry_on {
        settled {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- the program is still alive --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   row: [" + r.Row.title + "] rank " + r.Row.rank }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- 3. the failure events that came first ---------------------------------------------------
    //
    // @DiagnosticRaised is the general case. A subsystem that can fail in a way worth naming gets its
    // OWN event instead, because the name carries information a severity code cannot:
    //
    //     hear *Vein.Net.Http.@Failed     as f { … }   // no reply: bad host, refused, timed out
    //     hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Undelivered as u { … } // that console is gone
    //
    // Both are the same idea — the failure is a message — and neither needs a block around the call
    // that caused it. samples/db_supabase.vein hears @Failed for exactly this reason.

    shard Unreachable {
        hear *Vein.Net.Http.@Failed as f {
            *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.error("no answer from " + f.url + ": " + f.reason)
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/diagnostics_guard.vein`

**Guards: checking what arrived** — Rows arrive as @RowArrived events, including one built from JSON with a misspelt key, and a Guard shard on that boundary rejects rows with no title (error), keeps suspicious ranks with a warning, and reports a total (info). It demonstrates that a static check catches what you wrote while a guard catches what arrived.  
Run: `veinc run samples/diagnostics_guard.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: guard shard on a boundary event · choosing error vs warn vs info · missing values read as empty, not zero · fromJson and runtime-only field names · target ... by Shape.field ordering · if / } else { on one line · multi-line concatenation with trailing +  
Difficulty 4/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Interpreter-only because of fromJson, and not in check-backend. The output shows '4 rows arrived' while only three rows are kept, because the empty-title row was rejected. The ordering of kept rows comes from `by Row.rank`, where the empty rank sorts first.

Real output of `veinc run samples/diagnostics_guard.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
-- four rows arrive --
   WARN  | row "ship it" has rank [0] — sent as 0, or the field name was misspelled and never arrived at all
   ERROR | row rejected: a row with no title cannot be shown
   WARN  | row "paint it" has rank [] — sent as 0, or the field name was misspelled and never arrived at all
   kept: rank   paint it
   kept: rank 0  ship it
   kept: rank 2  write the docs
   note  | 4 rows arrived, and these are the ones that were kept
```

```vein
// diagnostics_guard.vein — validating what the language does not, and reporting it with info/warn/error.
//
//   veinc run samples/diagnostics_guard.vein --ticks 1
//
// READ THIS FIRST, because it is the reason the file exists.
//
// A fault raises @DiagnosticRaised and you can `hear` it — samples/diagnostics.vein shows that. A wrong
// ARGUMENT does not, because it does not fault. Here is the whole truth, measured against this tree:
//
//   bring Row("a", 1, 99, "x")     too many args    VS0204 error
//   bring Row("only-title")        too few args     VS0228 warning
//   bring Row(42, "words")         wrong types      VS0230 warning
//   add(1)  /  add(1, 2, 3)        call arity       VS0229 warning
//   emit @Order { quantity: 9 }    unknown field    VS0227 warning
//   emit @Order { item: "nuts" }   missing field    NOT reported — a missing field reads as empty, and
//                                                   that is load-bearing: @Fetch gained `headers` after
//                                                   programs were emitting it with three fields.
//
// All but the last are caught BEFORE the program runs, so `hear @DiagnosticRaised` is not the answer to
// a bad argument — the compiler is. There would be nothing to hear anyway: no exception is thrown and no
// event raised; the program just works with empty values it believes are real.
//
// WHAT A COMPILER CANNOT REACH is data. A row from a database, a JSON body, a form field — their shape
// is not in your source, so no check can look at it. That is what a GUARD is for, and it is the whole
// point of this file:
//
//     a static check catches what you WROTE.  a guard catches what ARRIVED.
//
// WHY A SHARD AND NOT AN `if` AT EVERY CALL SITE. The check lives in one place, on the boundary the data
// crosses, and every producer gets it without knowing. That is the same reason the collector in
// samples/diagnostics.vein belongs to nobody: a rule about the whole program should be written once.

bundle DiagnosticsGuard by you {

    shape $Row { title: string, rank: int }
    mark #Row

    builder Row { $Row   mark #Row }

    // The boundary. Rows arrive as events — from a database, a file, another program — and NOTHING
    // about this declaration forces a sender to fill it in correctly. That is what the guard is for.
    event @RowArrived { title: string, rank: int }

    // ---- the collector -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Collector {
        hear *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.@DiagnosticRaised as d {
            if d.severity == 0 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   note  | " + d.message } }
            if d.severity == 1 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   WARN  | " + d.message } }
            if d.severity == 2 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   ERROR | " + d.message } }
        }
    }

    // ---- rows arriving, correctly and otherwise ----------------------------------------------------

    shard Source {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- four rows arrive --" }

            emit @RowArrived { title: "write the docs", rank: 2 }   // fine
            emit @RowArrived { title: "ship it", rank: 0 }          // odd: rank 0 is not a rank
            emit @RowArrived { title: "", rank: 5 }                 // broken: no title

            // THE ONE THE COMPILER CANNOT SEE. The payload below is built from data that arrived as
            // JSON — a database row, a request body, a file — and the key is `rnk`, not `rank`. So
            // `row.rank` is nothing, and `rank` arrives EMPTY.
            //
            // Write that typo as a literal instead — `emit @RowArrived { title: "x", rnk: 9 }` — and
            // the compiler now stops you: VS0227, "@RowArrived has no field 'rnk'". That check did not
            // exist when this file was written, and adding it is what sharpened the lesson:
            //
            //     a static check catches what you WROTE.  a guard catches what ARRIVED.
            //
            // Neither replaces the other, and this line is the half no compiler can reach — the field
            // name is inside a string that only exists at runtime.
            //
            // A missing `int` arrives EMPTY rather than 0 — look at the output, which prints `rank []`
            // against `rank [0]`. `r.rank < 1` is true for both, which is why one test below catches
            // the typo and the honest zero together. Do not read that as "missing means zero": it does
            // not, and printing it shows the difference immediately.
            let row = fromJson("{\"title\":\"paint it\",\"rnk\":9}")
            emit @RowArrived { title: row.title, rank: row.rank }
        }
    }

    // ---- the guard: where info / warn / error get called --------------------------------------------
    //
    // Each severity earns its name, and the difference between them is what the program DOES next:
    //
    //   error — the row is rejected. Nothing is brought, and the program continues without it.
    //   warn  — the row is kept, but something about it is suspicious enough to say out loud.
    //   info  — nothing is wrong; this is the running total a reader wants at the end.

    shard Guard {
        hear @RowArrived as r {

            // A missing string arrives EMPTY, not null, so this one test covers both "sent nothing" and
            // "sent an empty title" — which are the same thing from here, and both unusable.
            if r.title == "" {
                *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.error("row rejected: a row with no title cannot be shown")
            } else {
                // Kept, but flagged. A rank of 0 is what a MISSING rank looks like — including the
                // `rnk:` typo above — so this one warning catches both the honest zero and the typo.
                if r.rank < 1 {
                    *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.warn(
                        "row \"" + r.title + "\" has rank [" + r.rank +
                        "] — sent as 0, or the field name was misspelled and never arrived at all")
                }

                // KEPT ANYWAY, and the output shows why that is a choice and not an oversight: the
                // "paint it" row is brought with an empty rank, sorts ahead of everything, and prints
                // `rank ` with a hole in it. A warning that changes nothing leaves the bad value in the
                // data. Moving this bring inside an `else` would make it an error instead — which is
                // the real decision `warn` vs `error` is asking you to make.
                bring Row(r.title, r.rank)
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- what survived ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // `settled`, because a `bring` inside a `hear` commits at the end of THAT event (docs/RULES.md 12b) —
    // a target loop in the guard itself would never see the row it had just brought.

    shard Report {
        settled {
            *Vein.Diagnostics.Report.info("4 rows arrived, and these are the ones that were kept")

            target $Row #Row by Row.rank as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: "   kept: rank " + r.Row.rank + "  " + r.Row.title
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

---

# Identities

## `samples/entities.vein`

**Folds: two shards, one hp** — Spawns three enemies with 5 hp. Two independent shards each subtract 1 hp every tick, and `folds sum` merges both deltas, so hp falls by exactly 2 per tick. A `settled` shard prints each enemy's hp and marks the dead.  
Run: `veinc render samples/entities.vein --ticks 3`  
Teaches: bundle · shape (component data) · folds sum (concurrent-write reconciliation) · shard · run once (world seeding) · spawn / attach / mark / unmark · each tick · target query with shape + mark (AND) · settled phase · Entity implicit binding · emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 1  
Notes: The header command (render) does not show the printed lines. The output above is from `veinc run ... --ticks 3`. Raw render output was 12 trace lines plus '(no @Response emitted for /)'. The bundle has no `by` author, unlike the other samples. `need "Vein.Core"` is declared but nothing in the file visibly uses it. The marks #Enemy and #Dead are not declared, so they are not checked (rule 16). This is the only sample in the group that prints Entity without an 'e' prefix ('enemy 1').

Real output of `veinc render samples/entities.vein --ticks 3`:
```text
enemy 1 hp 3
enemy 2 hp 3
enemy 3 hp 3
enemy 1 hp 1
enemy 2 hp 1
enemy 3 hp 1
enemy 1 hp -1
enemy 1 died
enemy 2 hp -1
enemy 2 died
enemy 3 hp -1
enemy 3 died
```

```vein
// entities.vein — the identity runtime actually RUNNING: spawn, attach, target, folds, settled.
//
// Two independent shards drain the same `hp` in one tick. `folds sum` is what makes that safe: each
// contributes its DELTA, so hp falls by exactly 2 per tick rather than one shard's write winning.
//
//   veinc render samples/entities.vein --ticks 3
//
// The clock is explicit (`--ticks N`) and `random` is seeded, so the run is reproducible.

bundle Entities {
    need "Vein.Core"

    shape $Health {
        hp: int folds sum      // many shards may drain hp in one tick; sum the deltas
    }

    shard Spawner {
        // `run once` builds the world before anything else happens.
        run once {
            repeat 3 {
                let e = spawn()
                attach $Health to e { hp: 5 }
                mark e #Enemy
            }
        }
    }

    shard Drain {
        each tick {
            target $Health #Enemy as self { self.Health.hp -= 1 }
        }
    }

    shard Poison {
        each tick {
            target $Health #Enemy as self { self.Health.hp -= 1 }
        }
    }

    shard Undertaker {
        // settled runs AFTER the tick's contributions reconcile — the only place a death check is
        // correct, because during the tick another shard may still be subtracting.
        settled {
            target $Health #Enemy as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "enemy " + Entity + " hp " + self.Health.hp }
                if self.Health.hp <= 0 {
                    mark self #Dead
                    unmark self #Enemy
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "enemy " + Entity + " died" }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_bind.vein`

**bring … as: naming what you built** — Node and Edge are identity templates. `bring Node("hub") as hub` binds the spawned entity to a name, so later edges can store it in an Entity-typed field. Two settled shards print the edges and the nodes.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_bind.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: identity template (builder with a mark member) · bring … as name · Entity as a first-class value and field type · declared marks · positional argument binding  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 13  
Notes: The 'e' in 'e3' comes from the string literal. Entity itself is just the number. The marks #Node and #Edge are declared, so this bundle's mark names are checked (rule 16).

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_bind.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
edge e3 -> e1 w=1
edge e4 -> e2 w=2
edge e5 -> e1 w=3
node e1 hub
node e2 leaf
edge e3 -> e1 w=1
edge e4 -> e2 w=2
edge e5 -> e1 w=3
node e1 hub
node e2 leaf
```

```vein
// entities_bind.vein — `bring … as name`, which hands back the identity a template just built.
//
// A `mark` member makes a builder an identity template (entities_template.vein). But `bring` is a
// STATEMENT, and the identity it spawns lives in a local the program never sees — so anything that had
// to REFER to what it built kept a hand-written spawn/attach/mark:
//
//     let hub = spawn()                    bring Node("hub") as hub
//     attach $Node to hub { name: "hub" }  bring Link("a", hub)
//     mark hub #Node
//
// `as` already means "bind this to a name" in `target … as self` and `hear … as e`, so `bring` keeps
// its place as a statement and simply names what it made.
//
// Two things it refuses. A fragment builder constructs nothing, so `as` has no identity to name — that
// is VS0221. And `bring 3 X(…) as n` would rebind on every iteration, leaving the name meaning only the
// last one, which is VS0222 rather than a silent surprise.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_bind.vein --ticks 2
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh          (the same program, emitted to C# and diffed against the above)

bundle EntitiesBind by you {

    shape $Node { name: string }
    shape $Edge { to: Entity, weight: int folds sum }

    mark #Node
    mark #Edge

    builder Node { $Node   mark #Node }
    builder Edge { $Edge   mark #Edge }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            // Each node is named, so the edges below can point at it. Nothing here spawns by hand.
            bring Node("hub")  as hub
            bring Node("leaf") as leaf

            // `hub` and `leaf` are ordinary Entity values — passable as arguments, storable in a shape.
            bring Edge(hub,  1)
            bring Edge(leaf, 2)
            bring Edge(hub,  3)
        }
    }

    // The edges are readable as data, which is the proof the binding held: each carries the entity the
    // template returned, not a zero or a copy.
    shard Report {
        settled {
            target $Edge #Edge as e {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "edge e" + Entity + " -> e" + e.Edge.to + " w=" + e.Edge.weight }
            }
        }
    }

    // And the nodes are still ordinary identities.
    shard Nodes {
        settled {
            target $Node #Node as n {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "node e" + Entity + " " + n.Node.name }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_multi.vein`

**Two shapes in one query: AND** — Seeds three #Unit identities: one with both $Health and $Shield, one with only $Health, one with only $Shield. `target $Health $Shield` visits only the one that carries both, and writes to both components in one activation are both folded.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_multi.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: a query over several shapes is an AND · folds on two components in one activation · control query to rule out an empty match · hand-written spawn/attach/mark  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: entities_template.vein shows the same AND semantics using builders, so this sample is optional if the template sample is shown. It is in check-backend.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_multi.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
both     e1 hp=9 sp=4
health   e1 hp=9
health   e2 hp=10
both     e1 hp=8 sp=2
health   e1 hp=8
health   e2 hp=10
```

```vein
// entities_multi.vein — a target query over TWO components, which is an AND.
//
// `target $Health $Shield #Unit as self { … }` visits only identities carrying BOTH shapes and the mark.
// The world below is built so that matters: one identity has both, one has only $Health, one only
// $Shield. A query that honoured just the first component would visit two of them and read a component
// the second does not carry.
//
// It also writes to both components inside one activation, so `folds` has to reconcile each of them —
// a backend that wrote back only the component it queried on would silently drop the other's deltas.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_multi.vein --ticks 2
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh          (the same program, emitted to C# and diffed against the above)

bundle EntitiesMulti by you {

    shape $Health { hp: int folds sum }
    shape $Shield { sp: int folds sum }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            // Carries both — the only identity the guarded query below should ever see.
            let a = spawn()
            attach $Health to a { hp: 10 }
            attach $Shield to a { sp: 6 }
            mark a #Unit

            // Health only.
            let b = spawn()
            attach $Health to b { hp: 10 }
            mark b #Unit

            // Shield only.
            let c = spawn()
            attach $Shield to c { sp: 6 }
            mark c #Unit
        }
    }

    // One activation, two components written. Both are `folds sum`, so both must be handed back.
    shard Wear {
        each tick {
            target $Health $Shield #Unit as self {
                self.Health.hp -= 1
                self.Shield.sp -= 2
            }
        }
    }

    shard Report {
        settled {
            // The AND: one line per tick, for the one identity carrying both.
            target $Health $Shield as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "both     e" + Entity + " hp=" + self.Health.hp + " sp=" + self.Shield.sp }
            }

            // The control: a single-component query still sees every identity carrying $Health, so the
            // one that lost only its shield is still here. Without this, "the AND works" and "the query
            // matches nothing" would look the same.
            target $Health as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "health   e" + Entity + " hp=" + self.Health.hp }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_nested.vein`

**Nested target: two bindings at once** — A query runs inside another query, and the inner body reads both the outer deck's title and the inner card's face. A second shard nests a plain list loop inside a query.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_nested.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: nested target loops · named bindings resolve by name (`as d`, `as c`) · iterating a collection with target … as · list literal · identity templates  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Most of the header comment is the history of a regression (roadmap item 5). The sample is in check-backend. The header says the broken interpreter printed "/ace". Given the code (title + " / " + face), it would really have been " / ace". The excerpt avoids the header.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_nested.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
trumps / ace
trumps / king
   trumps has hearts
   trumps has spades
```

```vein
// entities_nested.vein — a `target` inside a `target`, reading BOTH bindings.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_nested.vein --ticks 1
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh
//
// This is the regression guard for roadmap item 5, and it is in check-backend because the bug it guards
// was in BOTH runtimes and each got it wrong differently.
//
// WHAT WAS WRONG. `Lower` emitted a NAMELESS `IrSelfRef` for every target binding, so nothing in the IR
// recorded which loop a reference meant. The interpreter resolved it to `_targetBinds[^1]` — the
// innermost — so `d.Deck.title` written inside the inner loop silently read the CARD's row and came back
// empty. Not an error, not a crash: a plausible blank, indistinguishable from correct in a single loop,
// which is why it survived until a page tried to render a button's handler through a second query.
//
// The C# backend had the same hole plus two of its own: both loops emitted `foreach (var __e …)` and both
// declared `self_<Comp>`, so a nested query did not compile at all (CS0136, twice). It could not be run
// to see the wrong answer.
//
// WHAT FIXES IT is that `IrSelfRef` carries the binding's NAME, which `Lower` had all along — it tests
// the bind stack to decide a name IS a binding, then dropped the name on the floor. The interpreter
// resolves it out of the locals the loop already fills; the backend keys its entity variable and its
// component locals by binding, so `self_d_Deck` and `self_c_Card` cannot collide and cannot be confused.
//
// The output below is the whole test: every card line carries the deck's title. Before the fix the
// interpreter printed "/ace" — the slash with nothing in front of it.

bundle EntitiesNested by you {

    shape $Deck { title: string }
    mark #Deck

    builder Deck { $Deck   mark #Deck }

    shape $Card { face: string }
    mark #Card

    builder Card { $Card   mark #Card }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            bring Deck("trumps")
            bring Card("ace")
            bring Card("king")
        }
    }

    shard Show {
        settled {
            // TWO bindings live at once. `d` belongs to the outer loop and `c` to the inner, and the
            // body reads both — which is the entire point: nothing here is expressible while a binding
            // is anonymous.
            target $Deck #Deck as d {
                target $Card #Card as c {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: d.Deck.title + " / " + c.Card.face }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // The same nesting with a COLLECTION inside a query, because the two loop kinds bind by different
    // mechanisms — an entity id against a list element — and the fix has to hold for both.
    shard Suits {
        settled {
            target $Deck #Deck as d {
                let suits = ["hearts", "spades"]
                target suits as s {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + d.Deck.title + " has " + s }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_marks.vein`

**Marks: $Enemy vs #Enemy** — Declares the marks #Enemy, #Awake and #Spent, plus a shape that is also named $Enemy. Enemies lose 1 hp per tick. At 0 they swap to #Spent, and from the next tick a Ledger query on #Spent shows that the mark stayed on the identity. That needs --ticks 4 or more: the header's --ticks 3 run stops on the frame the enemies are spent, before the Ledger has anything to print.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_marks.vein --ticks 5`  
Teaches: marks as identity tags · declaring marks turns on name checking (VS0218) · shape and mark names are separate · several marks in one query (AND) · mark/unmark at runtime · deferred structural changes  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 11  
Notes: Run with --ticks 5, as the `run` field does, so the Ledger has something to print. From tick 4 on, each tick prints '  ledger: e1/e2/e3 is spent' and no more 'enemy' lines, because the enemies were unmarked #Enemy. The header's own --ticks 3 stops after '  e3 spent', before any ledger line.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_marks.vein --ticks 5`:
```text
enemy e1 hp=2
enemy e2 hp=2
enemy e3 hp=2
enemy e1 hp=1
enemy e2 hp=1
enemy e3 hp=1
enemy e1 hp=0
  e1 spent
enemy e2 hp=0
  e2 spent
enemy e3 hp=0
  e3 spent
  ledger: e1 is spent
  ledger: e2 is spent
  ledger: e3 is spent
  ledger: e1 is spent
  ledger: e2 is spent
  ledger: e3 is spent
```

```vein
// entities_marks.vein — a `#Mark` is an identity tag, and a shape may share its name.
//
// `$Enemy` and `#Enemy` are different things: different keyword, different sigil. One is the data an
// identity carries, the other is a tag it wears, and a program may legitimately use both names. This
// file does, on purpose — it is the case that decides how marks can be compiled at all.
//
// The C# backend emits marks as SECS identity tags (`IIdentityTag : IComponent`, a zero-data component):
//
//     mark e #Enemy              →   World.MarkAs<Marks.Enemy>(e)
//     unmark e #Enemy            →   World.UnmarkAs<Marks.Enemy>(e)
//     target $Enemy #Enemy       →   World.Query<Enemy, Marks.Enemy>()
//
// C# has no sigils, so `$Enemy` and `#Enemy` would both want the identifier `Enemy`. Tags are emitted
// NESTED in a `Marks` class, and a nested type cannot collide with a top-level one — so no mangling, and
// the generated code still reads like the source.
//
// Marks used to be strings in a dictionary beside SECS. As tags they are type-checked (a mistyped mark
```
