```vein
// stops compiling instead of matching nothing) and visible to the engine, which can ask
// `GetEntitiesByIdentity<Marks.Enemy>()` for them.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_marks.vein --ticks 3
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh          (the same program, emitted to C# and diffed against the above)

bundle EntitiesMarks by you {

    // Declared, so this bundle opts in to having its mark names checked: a `#Enmey` anywhere below
    // would now be VS0218 rather than a new mark that silently matches nothing. A bundle that declares
    // none is unchecked, which is why every other sample here is untouched.
    mark #Enemy
    mark #Awake
    mark #Spent

    shape $Enemy { hp: int folds sum }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            repeat 3 {
                let e = spawn()
                attach $Enemy to e { hp: 3 }
                mark e #Enemy          // the MARK — same name as the shape, different thing
                mark e #Awake
            }
        }
    }

    // Two marks on one query, so the AND across tags is exercised as well as the shape.
    shard Drain {
        each tick {
            target $Enemy #Enemy #Awake as self { self.Enemy.hp -= 1 }
        }
    }

    // Marks change every frame here, which is the case a string-keyed mark set made slowest: any
    // mark/unmark invalidates the query cache, so the next frame rescans. It is also the case that
    // proves `unmark` really removes the tag — a spent enemy must leave the Drain query.
    shard Reaper {
        settled {
            target $Enemy #Enemy as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "enemy e" + Entity + " hp=" + self.Enemy.hp }
                if self.Enemy.hp <= 0 {
                    mark self #Spent
                    unmark self #Awake
                    unmark self #Enemy
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  e" + Entity + " spent" }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // The tag it kept. Nothing else matches #Spent, so this is the check that a mark added in an earlier
    // frame is still there — tags persist on the identity, they are not per-frame state.
    shard Ledger {
        settled {
            target $Enemy #Spent as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  ledger: e" + Entity + " is spent" }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_detach.vein`

**attach and unattach at runtime** — Three mobs get $Health and a $Shield with no initialiser. Boost adds 2 and Wear subtracts 3 each tick. Once sp <= 0, Shatter unattaches the shield, after the tick's folds have been reconciled onto it.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_detach.vein --ticks 4`  
Teaches: attach with no initialiser (fields start at zero) · unattach … from · folds reconcile before a removal applies · deferred structural changes · query membership changing at runtime  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Ticks 2-4 are identical 'alive hp=8' blocks, so --ticks 2 is enough on camera. The sample is in check-backend.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_detach.vein --ticks 4`:
```text
shatter e1 sp=-1
shatter e2 sp=-1
shatter e3 sp=-1
  shielded e1 sp=-1
  shielded e2 sp=-1
  shielded e3 sp=-1
  alive    e1 hp=8
  alive    e2 hp=8
  alive    e3 hp=8
  alive    e1 hp=8
  alive    e2 hp=8
  alive    e3 hp=8
  alive    e1 hp=8
  alive    e2 hp=8
  alive    e3 hp=8
  alive    e1 hp=8
  alive    e2 hp=8
  alive    e3 hp=8
```

```vein
// entities_detach.vein — components ARRIVING and LEAVING while the world runs.
//
// Two structural changes the identity runtime has to get right, and both are deferred to the commit
// point so no unit in a phase can observe a half-changed world:
//
//   attach $Shield to e        — with no initialiser, so the fields take their declared zero
//   unattach $Shield from e    — the component goes, and the identity stops matching queries for it
//
// The ordering that matters is between a fold and a removal in the SAME phase: contributions reconcile
// FIRST, then the removal applies on top. So a shield written down to 0 and then dropped keeps its final
// value right up to the moment it disappears, rather than losing the writes or surviving them.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_detach.vein --ticks 4
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh          (the same program, emitted to C# and diffed against the above)

bundle EntitiesDetach by you {

    shape $Health { hp: int folds sum }
    shape $Shield { sp: int folds sum }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            repeat 3 {
                let e = spawn()
                attach $Health to e { hp: 9 }
                // No initialiser: `sp` starts at its zero, then Boost raises it below.
                attach $Shield to e
                mark e #Mob
            }
        }
    }

    shard Boost {
        each tick {
            target $Health $Shield #Mob as self { self.Shield.sp += 2 }
        }
    }

    shard Wear {
        each tick {
            target $Health $Shield #Mob as self {
                self.Health.hp -= 1
                self.Shield.sp -= 3
            }
        }
    }

    // Once the shield is spent it is removed — after this tick's folds have already reconciled onto it.
    shard Shatter {
        settled {
            target $Health $Shield #Mob as self {
                if self.Shield.sp <= 0 {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "shatter e" + Entity + " sp=" + self.Shield.sp }
                    unattach $Shield from self
                }
            }
        }
    }

    shard Report {
        settled {
            // Still shielded — shrinks as shields shatter, which is the removal being visible to a query.
            target $Shield #Mob as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  shielded e" + Entity + " sp=" + self.Shield.sp }
            }
            target $Health #Mob as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  alive    e" + Entity + " hp=" + self.Health.hp }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_template.vein`

**Identity templates: builder + mark** — Three builders over $Health and $Shield each carry `mark #Unit`, so `bring` spawns an identity and attaches the included shapes. `bring 2 Unit(4, 2)` makes two separate identities. The queries show that each template attached exactly the shapes it declares.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_template.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: builder as an identity template (the mark member) · $Shape include · positional arguments bound across includes · bring with a count · bring desugars to spawn + attach + mark  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 2  
Notes: The scaffold listing in the header comment (lines 24-29) is stale. `veinc scaffold samples/entities_template.vein Unit` prints each slot as `base,     // hp: int   from $Health   REQUIRED - give it a value` (and the same for sp from $Shield), not `?`. Run the command live rather than quoting the comment. A bare `Unit` and `&Unit` give the same output. The call-site form `bring X ?` does work: `bring Ward ?` compiles and fills sp with 0. The line `builder Unit { $Health $Shield   mark #Unit }` appears twice (header comment line 7 and code line 45), so it cannot be used as an anchor.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_template.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
unit  e1 hp=11 sp=4
unit  e4 hp=3 sp=0
unit  e5 hp=3 sp=0
  health e1 hp=11
  health e2 hp=12
  health e4 hp=3
  health e5 hp=3
  shield e1 sp=4
  shield e3 sp=6
  shield e4 sp=0
  shield e5 sp=0
unit  e1 hp=10 sp=2
unit  e4 hp=2 sp=-2
unit  e5 hp=2 sp=-2
  health e1 hp=10
  health e2 hp=12
  health e4 hp=2
  health e5 hp=2
  shield e1 sp=2
  shield e3 sp=6
  shield e4 sp=-2
  shield e5 sp=-2
```

```vein
// entities_template.vein — a builder that builds an IDENTITY.
//
// A builder's output field decides what it emits: `markup` an @Html fragment, `css` a @Style, `code` a
// @Script, none at all an event named after it. A `mark` member picks a fifth thing — it builds an
// identity, because only an identity can be marked.
//
//     builder Unit { $Health $Shield   mark #Unit }
//
//     bring Unit(10, 6)          ==          let e = spawn()
//                                            attach $Health to e { hp: 10 }
//                                            attach $Shield to e { sp: 6 }
//                                            mark e #Unit
//
// The two really are the same: `bring` desugars to those statements and to the same IR, so `spawn` is
// immediate and `attach`/`mark` are deferred to the commit point either way. Arguments bind positionally
// across the includes in declaration order — $Health takes hp, then $Shield takes sp — the same rule that
// flattens includes into a fragment builder's parameter list, grouped back into one `attach` per shape.
//
// `bring N Unit(…)` makes N separate identities, since the count wraps the whole sequence.
//
// An include hides the field names one level down, and a `shared` template is consumed from another
// bundle where its declaration is off-screen entirely — so ask, rather than reading the shape:
//
//     $ veinc scaffold samples/entities_template.vein Unit
//     // &Unit builds an identity #Unit
//     bring Unit(
//         ?,     // hp: int   from $Health
//         ?      // sp: int   from $Shield
//     )
//
// `bring Unit ?` is the same list written at the call site — every parameter filled with its type's
// zero, which is how you get a template running before you have decided the values.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_template.vein --ticks 2
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh          (the same program, emitted to C# and diffed against the above)

bundle EntitiesTemplate by you {

    shape $Health { hp: int folds sum }
    shape $Shield { sp: int folds sum }

    // Three templates over two shapes. What an identity CARRIES is the template's whole definition —
    // there is no body and nothing to run, which is what keeps a template a declaration rather than a
    // little constructor with its own rules.
    builder Unit { $Health $Shield   mark #Unit }
    builder Mob  { $Health           mark #Unit }
    builder Ward { $Shield           mark #Unit }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            bring Unit(12, 6)
            bring Mob(12)
            bring Ward(6)
            bring 2 Unit(4, 2)     // the count makes two separate identities, not one shared
        }
    }

    shard Wear {
        each tick {
            target $Health $Shield #Unit as self {
                self.Health.hp -= 1
                self.Shield.sp -= 2
            }
        }
    }

    shard Report {
        settled {
            target $Health $Shield #Unit as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "unit  e" + Entity + " hp=" + self.Health.hp + " sp=" + self.Shield.sp }
            }
            // The templates that attached only one shape are visible to the single-component queries and
            // absent from the paired one above — which is the check that a template attaches exactly what
            // it declares and nothing else.
            target $Health #Unit as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  health e" + Entity + " hp=" + self.Health.hp }
            }
            target $Shield #Unit as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  shield e" + Entity + " sp=" + self.Shield.sp }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_index.vein`

**Index: the loop's counter** — Seeds six rows with two templates. `Index` gives each iteration's 0-based position, which is used for numbering, a top-3 cutoff, striping and a first-item test. A two-shape query shows that Index counts only the matches.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_index.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: Index implicit binding · Entity vs Index · top-N cutoff · striping with % · testing for the first item · Index over a filtered multi-shape query · templates with several includes  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 14  
Notes: STALE: commit 070f82b (the user's own edit) removed the `if Index < 2` filter from the `$Row $Wide` block. The comment at lines 73-77 ('`Index < 2` therefore keeps beta and gamma…') and the printed label '-- first two of the wide ones --' still describe the filter, but the output lists three rows (0 beta, 1 gamma, 2 epsilon). Lines 80 and 84 are leftover whitespace-only lines. Relabel the block or restore the filter before filming. The lexer confirms `count` is a reserved keyword (KwCount), as the header says.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_index.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
-- all six, numbered --
1. alpha
2. beta
3. gamma
4. delta
5. epsilon
6. zeta
-- top 3 --
   alpha
   beta
   gamma
-- even positions --
   0 alpha
   2 gamma
   4 epsilon
first is alpha
-- first two of the wide ones --
   0 beta extra=4
   1 gamma extra=2
   2 epsilon extra=3
```

```vein
// entities_index.vein — `Index`, the nearest loop's 0-based iteration counter, and what it is for.
//
// `Entity` is WHICH identity an activation runs for. `Index` is WHICH ITERATION it is. Same shape of
// thing — a keyword and an implicit binding, no new syntax — and between them they cover what a list
// needs: numbering, a cutoff, striping, the first one.
//
// IT DOES NOT SORT. `Index` counts position in the order the loop already yields, which is spawn order
// (RULES.md 14b). So `if r.rank == Index + 1` compares a key against a position and matches nothing
// except by luck. Ordering BY a key is one pass per key value — samples/rows_in_order.vein.
//
// THE LAST ONE IS NOT EXPRESSIBLE. `Index == 0` is the first; there is no `Count`, so "the last" cannot
// be tested — `count` is a reserved keyword with no implementation. A cutoff from the front (`Index < 3`)
// is the shape that works.
//
// THE CASE THAT COULD HAVE DIVERGED, and why this file is in check-backend: with two components the
// interpreter's `Query` filters both BEFORE iterating, while the emitted C# drives the loop from one
// component and `continue`s on the rest. Count at the top of that loop and the backend numbers entities
// the interpreter never sees. The counter is bumped after the guards instead, so the `$Row $Wide` block
// below counts 0,1,2 over three rows rather than 1,2,4 over six.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_index.vein --ticks 1
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh

bundle EntitiesIndex by you {

    shape $Row  { title: string }
    shape $Wide { extra: int }

    mark #Row

    // Two templates over the same `$Row`, so seeding is one line per thing. `&Wide` includes a second
    // shape, which is what makes half these rows match the two-component query further down; arguments
    // bind positionally across both includes, so it takes (title, extra).
    builder Row  { $Row         mark #Row }
    builder Wide { $Row $Wide   mark #Row }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            bring Row("alpha")
            bring Wide("beta",    4)
            bring Wide("gamma",   2)
            bring Row("delta")
            bring Wide("epsilon", 3)
            bring Row("zeta")
        }
    }

    shard Numbered {
        settled {
            // The everyday use: a numbered list, with no counter the program has to carry itself.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- all six, numbered --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: (Index + 1) + ". " + r.Row.title }
            }

            // A cutoff. `Index` is 0-based, so "the first three" is `< 3`.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- top 3 --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if Index < 3 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.title } }
            }

            // Striping — the every-other-row case a table needs. `%` on the counter, nothing else.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- even positions --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if Index % 2 == 0 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + Index + " " + r.Row.title } }
            }

            // The first one, which is the only end of the list that can be tested. See the header.
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if Index == 0 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "first is " + r.Row.title } }
            }

            // Two components, and the FILTER is the point. Three of the six carry `$Wide`; the other
            // three are not in this query at all, so `Index` counts 0,1,2 over the MATCHES — not 1,2,4,
            // the positions those three occupy in the full list. `Index < 2` therefore keeps beta and
            // gamma. Count positions instead and it would keep only beta, so this filter changes the
            // NUMBER of lines, not just the numbers in them — which is what check-backend pins.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- first two of the wide ones --" }
            target $Row $Wide #Row as r {
                
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                        text: "   " + Index + " " + r.Row.title + " extra=" + r.Wide.extra
                    }
                
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_filter.vein`

**Filtering a query with Vein.Filter** — `need "Vein.Filter"` brings in stdlib fns: the predicates between, outside, firstN, even and divisible, plus sign, which returns -1, 0 or 1. They are used as `if` conditions inside target loops, either on a field value or on Index. The last block orders rows by rank with one `repeat` pass per key value.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_filter.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: need (importing a stdlib bundle) · calling stdlib fns · filtering with an if inside target · predicates on Index · repeat … as · ordering by walking the key range  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Stale wording: header line 19 still says 'A qualified or `use`d call'. `use` is retired (VS0338), and the file itself uses `need`. The predicates live in stdlib/Filter.vein, in publicators Range (between, outside, firstN) and Whole (divisible, even, odd, sign). The full output is 29 lines. The last four lines, cut above, are 'rank 2  beta', 'rank 3  gamma', 'rank 4  delta' and 'rank 5  epsilon'.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_filter.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
-- rank between 2 and 4 --
   beta rank 2
   gamma rank 3
   delta rank 4
-- and the ones outside it --
   alpha rank 1
   epsilon rank 5
   zeta rank -2
-- first three positions --
   0 alpha
   1 beta
   2 gamma
-- striped: even positions --
   0 alpha
   2 gamma
   4 epsilon
-- negative ranks --
   zeta rank -2
-- ranks divisible by 2 --
   beta rank 2
   delta rank 4
   zeta rank -2
-- by rank, ascending --
   rank -2  zeta
   rank 1  alpha
... (truncated; 4 more lines: rank 2 beta, 3 gamma, 4 delta, 5 epsilon)
```

```vein
// entities_filter.vein — narrowing a query with `*Vein.Filter`.
//
// A `target` yields everything that matches its shapes and marks. Keeping only some of that is an `if`
// inside the loop, and `Vein.Filter` is the vocabulary for the condition — there is no
// `filter(collection, predicate)`, because VeinScript has no function values to pass one.
//
// The predicates narrow by VALUE (`between(r.Row.rank, 2, 4)`) or, given the loop counter, by POSITION
// (`firstN(Index, 3)`, `even(Index)`). Same functions either way: `Index` is an int like any other.
//
// The last block ORDERS by rank, which filtering cannot do on its own — a query yields spawn order and
// nothing sorts it BY DEFAULT. `by Shape.field` on the query is the direct way (samples/entities_ordered.vein);
// walking the key space with `repeat` still works when the key is not one component field. Either way,
// zeta (rank -2) ends up first, where spawn order had it last.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_filter.vein --ticks 1
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh          (the same program, emitted to C# and diffed against the above)
//
// This file is in check-backend for a reason beyond the predicates: it is the first sample to call a
// STDLIB `fn` from compiled code. A qualified or `use`d call imports the declaration into the caller's
// module at compile time, so the backend emits it as an ordinary function — proven here rather than
// assumed.

bundle EntitiesFilter by you {

    need "Vein.Filter"

    shape $Row { title: string, rank: int }
    mark #Row

    builder Row { $Row   mark #Row }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            bring Row("alpha",   1)
            bring Row("beta",    2)
            bring Row("gamma",   3)
            bring Row("delta",   4)
            bring Row("epsilon", 5)
            bring Row("zeta",   -2)
        }
    }

    shard Show {
        settled {
            // By VALUE. Both ends included, so 2..4 is beta, gamma, delta.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- rank between 2 and 4 --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if between(r.Row.rank, 2, 4) {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.title + " rank " + r.Row.rank }
                }
            }

            // The complement, which is what `outside` is for — no `not` around the call.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- and the ones outside it --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if outside(r.Row.rank, 2, 4) {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.title + " rank " + r.Row.rank }
                }
            }

            // By POSITION. The same predicates, given `Index` instead of a field.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- first three positions --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if firstN(Index, 3) { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + Index + " " + r.Row.title } }
            }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- striped: even positions --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if even(Index) { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + Index + " " + r.Row.title } }
            }

            // `sign` is the one that is not a predicate, and the one that is genuinely awkward to write
            // inline — an `if` chain returning -1, 0 or 1.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- negative ranks --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if sign(r.Row.rank) < 0 {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.title + " rank " + r.Row.rank }
                }
            }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- ranks divisible by 2 --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if divisible(r.Row.rank, 2) {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.title + " rank " + r.Row.rank }
                }
            }

            // ---- ORDERING, which filtering cannot do on its own ---------------------------------------
            //
            // A query yields spawn order unless asked (RULES.md 14b). `by Row.rank` would sort this
            // directly; the pass below is the older technique, kept because it needs no ordered query and
            // still works when the key is not a single component field: walk the key space outside the
            // query and filter to one value per pass.
            //
            // Ranks here run -2..5, so eight passes with an offset — and the offset is the honest cost.
            // The range has to be known, which is why "order at the source" is the better answer whenever
            // you control the source. See samples/rows_in_order.vein for that side of it.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- by rank, ascending --" }
            repeat 8 as pass {
                target $Row #Row as r {
                    if r.Row.rank == pass - 2 {
                        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   rank " + r.Row.rank + "  " + r.Row.title }
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_ordered.vein`

**Sorting a query: target … by** — Five rows are brought in scrambled order. `target $Row #Row by Row.rank` and `by Row.title` sort them when they are read. The sort is stable, and strings compare ordinally, so "Zeta" comes before "alpha".  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_ordered.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: the default query order is spawn order · sorted query: by Shape.field · stable sort (ties keep spawn order) · ordinal string comparison · sorting at read time leaves entity ids unchanged  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 14  
Notes: The sample is in check-backend to catch a culture-sensitive C# string sort. The observed output matches the header, but the sentence at line 56 is wrong. A culture-aware sort would not put 'Zeta' between bravo and charlie: InvariantCulture orders these titles alpha, bravo, charlie, delta, Zeta, so Zeta comes LAST. Only the ordinal sort puts it first. Say 'a culture-aware sort would put Zeta last' instead of reading line 56.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_ordered.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
-- spawn order --
   4  delta
   1  alpha
   -2  Zeta
   3  charlie
   1  bravo
-- by rank --
   -2  Zeta
   1  alpha
   1  bravo
   3  charlie
   4  delta
-- by title --
   Zeta
   alpha
   bravo
   charlie
   delta
-- ties keep spawn order --
   alpha
   bravo
```

```vein
// entities_ordered.vein — `target … by Shape.field`, ordering where the data is READ.
//
// A query yields spawn order and nothing sorted it, so a `rank` field could be stored and never
// consulted. `by` fixes that:
//
//     target $Row #Row by Row.rank  as r { … }     // ascending by an int field
//     target $Row #Row by Row.title as r { … }     // ascending by a string field
//
// Sorting at READ time rather than at spawn is the whole point: entity ids never move, so nothing
// referring to a row (`$Parent { of: Entity }`, a handler reference) is disturbed, and the SAME rows can
// be shown in several orders in the same frame. Destroying and re-bringing rows to reorder them would
// break every one of those references.
//
// Ties keep spawn order — the sort is stable on both runtimes, which is what makes output repeatable.
//
// STRINGS COMPARE ORDINALLY, deliberately. The interpreter uses `string.CompareOrdinal` and the emitted
// C# is given `StringComparer.Ordinal`, because C#'s DEFAULT string comparison is culture-sensitive and
// would order differently on a machine with a different locale. That is the divergence this file is in
// check-backend to catch.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_ordered.vein --ticks 1
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh

bundle EntitiesOrdered by you {

    shape $Row { title: string, rank: int }
    mark #Row

    builder Row { $Row   mark #Row }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            // Deliberately in neither rank nor title order, so both sorts have work to do.
            bring Row("delta",   4)
            bring Row("alpha",   1)
            bring Row("Zeta",   -2)
            bring Row("charlie", 3)
            bring Row("bravo",   1)
        }
    }

    shard Show {
        settled {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- spawn order --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.rank + "  " + r.Row.title }
            }

            // By an int field. Negatives sort before positives, which a string sort would not do.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- by rank --" }
            target $Row #Row by Row.rank as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.rank + "  " + r.Row.title }
            }

            // By a string field. "Zeta" sorts BEFORE the lowercase names because ordinal comparison puts
            // uppercase first — a culture-aware sort would place it between bravo and charlie, so this
            // line is the one that proves the two runtimes agree on which comparison is used.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- by title --" }
            target $Row #Row by Row.title as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.title }
            }

            // `alpha` and `bravo` both hold rank 1, and they come out in spawn order — alpha was brought
            // first. A sort that was not stable could give either.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- ties keep spawn order --" }
            target $Row #Row by Row.rank as r {
                if r.Row.rank == 1 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.title } }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_bring_order.vein`

**Ordered by: sorting the brings** — Five rows are brought in scrambled order inside an `ordered by &Row.$Row.rank` block, so the brings run sorted by rank and the entity ids come out in rank order. A plain query with no `by` clause then prints them already sorted, and the two rank-1 ties keep the order they were written in.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_bring_order.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: ordered by (sorting brings) · identity template (builder with a mark member) · query order == spawn order · stable sort / ties keep source order · fully-qualified bring key &Builder.$Shape.param  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good  
Notes: In tools/check-backend.sh (2 frames). Header line 6 uses a generic `&Card.rank` example that does not match this file's builder. It pairs with samples/entities_bring_rows.vein (same shape and builder, extended to a loop), so a video probably needs only one of the two, or both back to back.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_bring_order.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
-- spawn order, which is now rank order --
   e1  -2  Zeta
   e2  1  alpha
   e3  1  bravo
   e4  3  charlie
   e5  4  delta
-- ties keep the written order --
   alpha
   bravo
```

```vein
// entities_bring_order.vein — `ordered by &Builder.param { bring … }`, sorting the BRINGS themselves.
//
// There are two places an order can be imposed, and they solve different problems:
//
//   target $Row #Row by Row.rank as r { … }   — sorts a QUERY. Needs identities to query.
//   ordered by &Card.rank { bring Card(…) … }  — sorts the BRINGS. Needs nothing.
//
// The second exists for the case the first cannot reach: a `bring` on a FRAGMENT builder emits its
// @Html the instant it runs and leaves no identity behind, so the call order IS the output order and
// there is nothing to sort later. That is most of a web page.
//
// It works on identity templates too, which is what this file uses so check-backend can diff it —
// the rows come out with ids in rank order, because the brings ran in rank order.
//
// Every key is evaluated BEFORE any body runs. Sorting as it went would let an earlier bring change
// what a later key reads, and the order would depend on itself.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_bring_order.vein --ticks 1
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh

bundle EntitiesBringOrder by you {

    shape $Row { title: string, rank: int }
    mark #Row

    builder Row { $Row   mark #Row }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            // Written in neither rank nor title order. The key is fully qualified: the `rank` parameter
            // of builder `&Row`, contributed by its `$Row` include. Both qualifiers are optional — see
            // the header — but a builder including two shapes that BOTH carry `rank` needs the middle
            // segment, and refuses to guess without it (VS0226).
            ordered by &Row.$Row.rank {
                bring Row("delta",   4)
                bring Row("alpha",   1)
                bring Row("Zeta",   -2)
                bring Row("charlie", 3)
                bring Row("bravo",   1)
            }
        }
    }

    shard Show {
        settled {
            // NOT an ordered query — plain spawn order. It comes out sorted because the brings were
            // sorted, which is the whole point: the order is baked in at creation, so every later
            // reader gets it for free.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- spawn order, which is now rank order --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   e" + Entity + "  " + r.Row.rank + "  " + r.Row.title }
            }

            // alpha and bravo share rank 1 and keep the order they were written in — the sort is
            // stable, so ties fall back to source order rather than to whichever the comparer saw first.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- ties keep the written order --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if r.Row.rank == 1 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.title } }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_bring_rows.vein`

**Ordered by over a data loop** — An `ordered by rank` block holds one literal bring plus a `target` loop over a list of ranks, filtering out -2. The collected brings are replayed sorted, so the rows' entity ids follow rank order, ties keep source order, and the filtered row never exists.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_bring_rows.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: ordered by over a target loop · deferred/replayed brings · Index as source position · iterating a list literal with target · stable sort for ties · filtering before a bring  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good  
Notes: In tools/check-backend.sh (2 frames). This is the data-driven follow-up to samples/entities_bring_order.vein and uses the same shape and builder. RULES 14e points to samples/json_roundtrip.vein for the JSON version. The comment at line 69 ('5 ranks and 4 rows') counts only the rows that came from the list. The output lists 5 rows (e1..e5) because the literal bring adds one, so say '4 of the 5 ranks became rows' on camera.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_bring_rows.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
-- spawn order, which is now rank order --
   e1  rank 0  literal
   e2  rank 1  src1
   e3  rank 1  src4
   e4  rank 3  src3
   e5  rank 4  src0
-- ties keep the order the rows arrived in --
   src1
   src4
-- the filtered row never arrived --
```

```vein
// entities_bring_rows.vein — `ordered by` over a `target` loop: ordering brings you did not write.
//
// samples/entities_bring_order.vein sorts a handful of brings typed out by hand. That is the easy half.
// The half that matters is this one: rows arrive from a database or a JSON body as a LIST, one written
// `bring` inside a loop becomes N brings, and N lives in the data. A block that admitted only literal
// brings could sort a menu you typed and nothing you fetched.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_bring_rows.vein --ticks 1
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh
//
// WHY THE BRING ORDER AND NOT THE QUERY ORDER. `target $Row #Row by Row.rank` sorts what you READ, and
// for a console listing that is enough. It is not enough for a page: a `bring` on a fragment builder
// emits its @Html the instant it runs and leaves no identity behind, so the call order IS the output
// order and there is nothing left to sort. Ordering at the bring is the only place that reaches it.
//
// WHAT IS REORDERED IS `bring`, AND ONLY `bring`. Anything else inside the loop — an `emit`, a `let` —
// runs where it stands, in the order the loop reaches it. The brings are put aside with their keys and
// replayed sorted once the loop has finished.

bundle EntitiesBringRows by you {

    shape $Row { title: string, rank: int }
    mark #Row

    builder Row { $Row   mark #Row }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            // Standing in for a result set: the source's own order, which is nobody's display order.
            let ranks = [4, 1, -2, 3, 1]

            ordered by rank {
                // A literal bring and a loop in ONE block, sorted together. They are the same kind of
                // thing — a bring with a key — so nothing makes them separate.
                bring Row("literal", 0)

                target ranks as r {
                    // The `if` is why the brings are collected rather than counted in advance: how many
                    // there will be is not known until the loop has run. `Index` is the SOURCE position,
                    // and it survives the deferral — the body runs after the loop has finished, so the
                    // counter has to be captured per row rather than read later.
                    if r > -2 {
                        bring Row("src" + Index, r)
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    shard Show {
        settled {
            // A PLAIN query — no `by` — so this is spawn order. It comes out sorted because the brings
            // were sorted, which is the whole point: the order is baked in at creation and every later
            // reader gets it for nothing.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- spawn order, which is now rank order --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   e" + Entity + "  rank " + r.Row.rank + "  " + r.Row.title }
            }

            // src1 and src4 both have rank 1 and keep their source positions, in that order: the sort is
            // stable, so a tie falls back to the order the loop reached them rather than to whichever
            // the comparer happened to see first. Rows tie constantly — any rank column with a repeat —
            // so an unstable sort here would shuffle a page on every run for no visible reason.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- ties keep the order the rows arrived in --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if r.Row.rank == 1 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + r.Row.title } }
            }

            // -2 was filtered out before it ever became a bring, so there are 5 ranks and 4 rows.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- the filtered row never arrived --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if r.Row.rank < 0 { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   should not appear: " + r.Row.title } }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_chance.vein`

**Chance N%: seeded randomness** — Four mobs with 20 hp each lose 1 hp on a 50% chance and 3 hp on a 25% chance every tick. They are reported in `settled`, and because the generator is seeded at 0 the output is identical every run.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_chance.vein --ticks 4`  
Teaches: chance N% blocks · seeded, reproducible randomness · folds sum · spawn / attach / mark by hand · phases: run once, each tick, settled  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 17  
Notes: In tools/check-backend.sh (4 frames). `chance` is a real lexer keyword; `random` is deliberately a built-in function, not a keyword (Lexer.cs comment). See samples/random_draw.vein for random() called directly.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_chance.vein --ticks 4`:
```text
mob e1 hp=20
mob e2 hp=20
mob e3 hp=19
mob e4 hp=20
mob e1 hp=20
mob e2 hp=19
mob e3 hp=19
mob e4 hp=17
mob e1 hp=20
mob e2 hp=19
mob e3 hp=19
mob e4 hp=14
mob e1 hp=20
mob e2 hp=19
mob e3 hp=16
mob e4 hp=13
```

```vein
// entities_chance.vein — `chance N%` under the identity runtime, and the reason it is seeded.
//
// `chance 30% { … }` lowers to `if random() < 0.30`, and `random` is drawn from ONE generator seeded at
// 0 — the interpreter's `Interp._rng`. That is what makes a probabilistic program reproducible: the same
// source and the same frame count print the same thing every run, so it can be diffed.
//
// It is also the whole point of this file as a backend case. A backend that emitted a constant for
// `random` would compile, run, and quietly disagree — with 0.0 every `chance` fires, so the compiled
// program would be strictly harsher than the interpreted one and only a diff would ever show it.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_chance.vein --ticks 4
//   bash tools/check-backend.sh          (the same program, emitted to C# and diffed against the above)

bundle EntitiesChance by you {

    shape $Health { hp: int folds sum }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            repeat 4 {
                let e = spawn()
                attach $Health to e { hp: 20 }
                mark e #Mob
            }
        }
    }

    // Two independent draws per activation, so the ORDER of draws matters as well as their values: a
    // generator that agreed on the sequence but was consulted in a different order would show up here.
    shard Rot {
        each tick {
            target $Health #Mob as self {
                chance 50% { self.Health.hp -= 1 }
                chance 25% { self.Health.hp -= 3 }
            }
        }
    }

    shard Report {
        settled {
            target $Health #Mob as self {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "mob e" + Entity + " hp=" + self.Health.hp }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_chars.vein`

**Characters as ASCII values** — Scans "Vein 42!" one character at a time, bringing an entity per character with its ASCII code and marking it #Letter, #Digit or #Other. Handlers of a follow-up event then list the characters, sum the lowered letter values, turn the digit characters into the number 42, and show chr/code/upper/lower/len/indexing.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_chars.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: a character is a one-character string · ordinal string comparison for range tests · code()/chr() conversions · chars() returns a list that target walks · bring … as name, then mark the bound identity · commit per event: read results in the next event  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: reference  
Notes: In tools/check-backend.sh (1 frame). This is the backend-diffed version of the character rule; samples/characters.vein is the more readable teaching version (classifier fns, identifier check, ROT13) and overlaps heavily. `mark #Scanned` is declared on the line after the builder that uses it, so declaration order does not matter.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_chars.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
scanned 8 characters
  1 'V' = 86
  2 'e' = 101
  3 'i' = 105
  4 'n' = 110
  5 ' ' = 32
  6 '4' = 52
  7 '2' = 50
  8 '!' = 33
lowered letter values sum to 434
digits read as the number 42
  not alphanumeric: ' ' = 32
  not alphanumeric: '!' = 33
chr(code("V")) = V
upper/lower = VEIN vein
len("Vein") = 4, "Vein"[0] = V
```

```vein
// entities_chars.vein — characters by their ASCII value, in the subset the BACKEND compiles.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_chars.vein --ticks 1
//
// WHY THIS ONE IS IN check-backend.sh. Reading text a character at a time is exactly the kind of work
// that wants compiling rather than interpreting, and it was interpreter-only until this sample existed:
// `s[i]`, `code`, `chr`, `chars`, `len`, `upper`/`lower` and — quietly the most important — ORDERING
// between two strings all had no C# emission. The check emits this file, compiles it, runs it, and
// diffs against `veinc run`, so "both runtimes agree about characters" is verified rather than claimed.
//
// A CHARACTER IS A ONE-CHARACTER STRING and its value is `code(c)` — the number in the ASCII table.
// That is the whole vocabulary: no `char` type, no `'a'` literal, and no shape or builder wrapping a
// value that no identity carries.
//
//     "A"            a one-character string
//     code("A")      65        the ASCII value
//     chr(65)        "A"       back again
//     c >= "a"       ordinal, so it agrees with the number
//
// ORDINAL AND NUMERIC AGREE, and that is why both forms are shown side by side below. `"a" < "b"` and
// `code("a") < code("b")` are the same question — comparison is ordinal on the code units, which for
// ASCII is the table order. The number is the one to reach for when the next step is arithmetic; the
// character is the one to reach for when it is a range.

bundle EntitiesChars by you {

    // One character of the input, with its ASCII value alongside. `n` is its 1-based position, so the
    // query reads back in the order the text was written without anything sorting it.
    shape $Ch { n: int, text: string, value: int }

    // The three classes the ASCII table separates by range, decided once while scanning so the queries
    // below do not repeat the test.
    mark #Letter
    mark #Digit
    mark #Other

    builder Ch { $Ch   mark #Scanned }
    mark #Scanned

    event @Scanned { count: int }

    // ---- scan ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Scan {
        var seen: int

        run once {
            seen = 0

            // `chars` hands back a list and `target` walks it — the same statement that walks a query.
            target chars("Vein 42!") as c {
                let position = Index + 1
                seen = seen + 1

                bring Ch(position, c, code(c)) as e

                // RANGE TESTS ON THE CHARACTER. Ordinal comparison means these read as the ASCII table
                // does: 'A'..'Z' is 65..90, 'a'..'z' is 97..122, '0'..'9' is 48..57.
                if (c >= "a" and c <= "z") or (c >= "A" and c <= "Z") { mark e #Letter }
                if c >= "0" and c <= "9" { mark e #Digit }
                if not ((c >= "a" and c <= "z") or (c >= "A" and c <= "Z") or (c >= "0" and c <= "9")) {
                    mark e #Other
                }
            }

            emit @Scanned { count: seen }
        }
    }

    // ---- read it back ----------------------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // A phase boundary: everything brought above is deferred to the commit point, so it becomes real
    // between one event and the next.

    shard Report {
        hear @Scanned as s {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "scanned " + s.count + " characters" }

            target $Ch #Scanned as c {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: "  " + c.Ch.n + " '" + c.Ch.text + "' = " + c.Ch.value
                }
            }
        }
    }

    shard Letters {
        var total: int

        hear @Scanned as s {
            // ARITHMETIC ON THE VALUE, which is what having the number is for. In ASCII a letter's
            // case differs by exactly 32, so lowering is `+ 32` and the sum below is over the lowered
            // values — a thing no range test can compute.
            total = 0
            target $Ch #Letter as c {
                if c.Ch.value >= 65 and c.Ch.value <= 90 { total = total + c.Ch.value + 32 }
                if c.Ch.value >= 97 { total = total + c.Ch.value }
            }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "lowered letter values sum to " + total }
        }
    }

    shard Digits {
        var value: int

        hear @Scanned as s {
            // '7' is not 7. Subtracting the code of '0' is how a digit character becomes its number,
            // and it is the same subtraction every low-level parser does.
            value = 0
            target $Ch #Digit as c {
                value = value * 10 + (c.Ch.value - 48)
            }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "digits read as the number " + value }
        }
    }

    shard Others {
        hear @Scanned as s {
            target $Ch #Other as c {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  not alphanumeric: '" + c.Ch.text + "' = " + c.Ch.value }
            }

            // Round trip, and case folding — both emitted, so both runtimes agree on them.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "chr(code(\"V\")) = " + chr(code("V")) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "upper/lower = " + upper("vein") + " " + lower("VEIN") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "len(\"Vein\") = " + len("Vein") + ", \"Vein\"[0] = " + "Vein"[0] }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_convert.vein`

**int(), float(), bool(): total conversions** — Prints a table of conversions: int/float/string/bool of assorted text, isNumber, and mixed text-number ordering. It then stores two parsed scores on identities and reads them back in a later phase.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_convert.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: type-named conversions int/float/string/bool · total functions (no exceptions) · isNumber as the guard for total conversions · int truncates toward zero and saturates · invariant-culture parsing · ordering rules across text and numbers · structural changes commit at the phase boundary  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: In tools/check-backend.sh (2 frames). Output is 29 lines, shown in full (slightly over ~25). The comment above Report says "This is the second tick"; it means the phase after run once, and Report actually prints on tick 1 and again on tick 2.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_convert.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
int 42      = 42
int -3      = -3
int 4.9     = 4
int abc     = 0
int empty   = 0
int spaced  = 7
float 1.5   = 1.5
float comma = 0
string 7    = [7]
string bool = [true]
string list = [a, b]
int too big = 9223372036854775807
bool 0      = false
bool text   = true
bool 'false'= false
bool 'no'   = false
bool '0'    = false
bool 'cat'  = true
isNumber 42 = true
isNumber ab = false
'5' > 3     = true
'4.5' < 5   = true
'abc' > 3   = true
'10' < '9'  = true
'a' < 'b'   = true
Hero scored 120
Rival scored 95
Hero scored 120
Rival scored 95
```

```vein
// entities_convert.vein — turning text into numbers and back, on both paths.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_convert.vein --ticks 2
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh, so the interpreter and the compiled C# are diffed on every conversion.
// That matters more here than for most built-ins: `float("1.5")` parsed against the machine's locale
// would read as 15 where the decimal separator is a comma, and a difference between the two paths
// would be a program that computed one answer interpreted and another compiled.

bundle Convert by you {

    shape $Score { who: string, points: int }
    mark #Score
    builder Score { $Score   mark #Score }

    shard Read {
        run once {
            // Whole numbers, decimals, and the two ways text fails to be either.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "int 42      = " + int("42") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "int -3      = " + int("-3") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "int 4.9     = " + int("4.9") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "int abc     = " + int("abc") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "int empty   = " + int("") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "int spaced  = " + int("  7  ") }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "float 1.5   = " + float("1.5") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "float comma = " + float("1,5") }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "string 7    = [" + string(7) + "]" }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "string bool = [" + string(true) + "]" }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "string list = " + string(chars("ab")) }

            // A number too large to hold saturates. It does NOT wrap round to a large negative one,
            // which is what the underlying cast does and what a wrong answer here would look like.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "int too big = " + int("999999999999999999999") }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "bool 0      = " + bool(0) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "bool text   = " + bool("x") }

            // `bool` of text READS THE ANSWER rather than asking whether there is text. This is the one
            // conversion that is not truthiness: `if "false"` is true, because there is a string there,
            // but `bool("false")` is false, because that is what the person typed.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "bool 'false'= " + bool("false") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "bool 'no'   = " + bool("no") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "bool '0'    = " + bool("0") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "bool 'cat'  = " + bool("cat") }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "isNumber 42 = " + isNumber("42") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "isNumber ab = " + isNumber("abc") }

            // Ordering across text and a number. Numeric when both read as numbers, ordinal on their
            // text when they do not, and ordinal between two strings — which character comparison
            // depends on and must never change.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "'5' > 3     = " + ("5" > 3) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "'4.5' < 5   = " + ("4.5" < 5) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "'abc' > 3   = " + ("abc" > 3) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "'10' < '9'  = " + ("10" < "9") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "'a' < 'b'   = " + ("a" < "b") }
        }
    }

    // A parsed number reaching an identity's field, which is where the conversion is actually for:
    // console input is text, and a `points: int` will not take it.
    shard Tally {
        run once {
            bring Score("Hero", int("120"))
            bring Score("Rival", int("95.7"))
        }
    }

    // A SEPARATE SCHEDULE, and it has to be. A `bring` is a structural change and structural changes
    // commit at the phase boundary (RULES 11/12b) — so a `target` in the same block queries the world
    // as it was BEFORE the brings and finds nothing. This is the second tick.
    shard Report {
        each tick {
            target $Score #Score as s {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: s.Score.who + " scored " + s.Score.points }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_destroy.vein`

**unmark, unattach, destroy: deferred** — Three mobs (10, 20 and 30 hp, each with a shield) go through three kinds of removal. The one under 15 hp is unmarked #Alive, every mob has its $Shield unattached, and the one over 25 hp is destroyed. A `during` shard counts in the same phase and an `after` shard counts in settled, which shows that the removals only land at the commit point.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_destroy.vein --ticks 3`  
Teaches: unmark (remove a mark) · unattach (remove a shape) · destroy (remove the identity) · structural changes are deferred to the commit point · each tick vs settled · shard order independence  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 3  
Notes: In tools/check-backend.sh (3 frames). The builder is written `builder Mob { $Health, $Shield   mark #Alive   mark #Guarded }`, with a comma between the includes (RULES shows the no-comma form; both parse). The first tick's `during` sees 3 because run once's brings committed before tick 1.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_destroy.vein --ticks 3`:
```text
during   alive 3   shielded 3   entities 3
after    alive 1   shielded 0   entities 2
during   alive 1   shielded 0   entities 2
after    alive 1   shielded 0   entities 2
during   alive 1   shielded 0   entities 2
after    alive 1   shielded 0   entities 2
```

```vein
// entities_destroy.vein — taking things away: `unmark`, `unattach`, `destroy`.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_destroy.vein --ticks 3
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh. `entities_detach.vein` covers `unattach`; `destroy` had call sites in two
// samples and neither was diffed against the compiled backend, so removing an identity was the one
// structural change with no differential coverage at all.
//
// ALL THREE ARE STRUCTURAL, and structural changes commit at the PHASE BOUNDARY (RULES 11/12b). That is
// the single thing worth understanding here, because it makes the obvious reading wrong:
//
//   `unmark m #Alive` does not stop the current frame's later shards from seeing #Alive;
//   `destroy e` does not make `e` vanish mid-frame.
//
// Everything queued during a frame lands together at the end of it, which is what stops two shards from
// disagreeing about the world depending on which ran first. So a shard that removes and a shard that
// counts, in the same frame, both see the world as it was when the frame began — and the frame after
// sees the result.

bundle Destroying by you {

    shape $Health { hp: int }
    shape $Shield { sp: int }
    mark #Alive
    mark #Guarded

    builder Mob { $Health, $Shield   mark #Alive   mark #Guarded }

    shard Spawn {
        run once {
            bring Mob(10, 5)
            bring Mob(20, 5)
            bring Mob(30, 5)
        }
    }

    // ---- take the mark off the weakest -------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // `unmark` removes an identity from a query without touching its data. The entity still exists and
    // still carries $Health; it simply stops being #Alive, so every `target … #Alive` skips it from the
    // next frame on.

    shard Cull {
        each tick {
            target $Health #Alive as m {
                if m.Health.hp < 15 { unmark m #Alive }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- take a component off, keeping the identity ------------------------------------------------
    //
    // `unattach` is the other half: the identity stays, and one shape leaves it. After this the entity
    // no longer matches `target $Shield`, but still matches `target $Health`.

    shard Disarm {
        each tick {
            target $Shield #Guarded as m {
                if m.Shield.sp > 0 { unattach $Shield from m }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- take the whole identity away --------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // `destroy` removes it entirely — every component, every mark. Deferred like the other two, so an
    // entity destroyed in this frame is still counted by anything else running in this frame.

    shard Reap {
        each tick {
            target $Health #Alive as m {
                if m.Health.hp > 25 { destroy m }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- what is there, counted twice per frame ----------------------------------------------------
    //
    // THE TWO COUNTS ARE THE POINT OF THIS FILE. `during` runs in the same phase as the three shards
    // above and sees the world as the frame BEGAN — every removal they queued is still pending. `after`
    // runs once those have committed.
    //
    // The order of the `each tick` shards relative to each other does not matter, and that is the
    // guarantee rather than a caveat: because removals are deferred, `during` reports the same numbers
    // whether it happens to run before or after Cull, Disarm and Reap.
    //
    // Three counts rather than one, because each removal answers a different question: the #Alive count
    // tracks marks, $Shield tracks components, and $Health tracks identities that still exist at all.
    //
    // The mark count names $Health as well, and that is now the RULE rather than a workaround. A
    // mark-only `target #Alive as a` is VS0236: a query's binding reads fields, and the shapes it names
    // are what give it fields to read.
    //
    // It used to compile and was silently skipped by the C# backend — every VeinWorld.Query overload
    // takes a component type — so this file ran one way interpreted and another compiled. Requiring the
    // shape closes that where it can be seen. Every entity here carries $Health, so naming it changes
    // no count.

    shard During {
        each tick {
            var alive = 0
            var shielded = 0
            var living = 0

            target $Health #Alive as a { alive = alive + 1 }
            target $Shield as s { shielded = shielded + 1 }
            target $Health as h { living = living + 1 }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                text: "during   alive " + alive + "   shielded " + shielded + "   entities " + living
            }
        }
    }

    shard After {
        settled {
            var alive = 0
            var shielded = 0
            var living = 0

            target $Health #Alive as a { alive = alive + 1 }
            target $Shield as s { shielded = shielded + 1 }
            target $Health as h { living = living + 1 }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                text: "after    alive " + alive + "   shielded " + shielded + "   entities " + living
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/entities_tree.vein`

**Parent links: decks and cards** — Two decks and five cards are linked by a `$Parent { of: Entity }` shape. An event carries each deck to a handler that lists its cards, each card adds 1 to its parent deck's `folds sum` count, and settled prints the counts and the raw child-to-parent links.  
Run: `veinc run samples/entities_tree.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: relationships as an Entity field in a shape · bring … as to name an identity · passing an Entity as a builder argument · writing a parent's component through a handle · folds sum aggregation read in settled · events carrying an Entity into a later phase  
Difficulty 4/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Interpreter-only per its header, and not in tools/check-backend.sh. Commit 3ae01ea gives the real reason: the sample reads a component off an arbitrary entity, which the backend cannot express yet. Unsupported `hear` is not the reason, because the backend runs emit/hear now. The header's quoted 'hear not emitted' notes are therefore stale: `veinc emit samples/entities_tree.vein` prints no note today, and it writes C# (`c_Parent.of.Deck.count = …` on an int field) that would not compile. `event @Show` and `shard Show` share a word, and the sigil keeps them apart.

Real output of `veinc run samples/entities_tree.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
== Ship
   - nav is a query
   - bring … as
== Backlog
   - packed ECS
   - TLS for peers
   - a JS backend
Ship holds 2 card(s)
Backlog holds 3 card(s)
card e3 'nav is a query' -> parent e1
card e4 'bring … as' -> parent e1
card e5 'packed ECS' -> parent e2
card e6 'TLS for peers' -> parent e2
card e7 'a JS backend' -> parent e2
```

```vein
// entities_tree.vein — parent and child as an identity RELATIONSHIP, not a container.
//
// A mark says what something IS. `#Card` cannot say whose card it is, so the moment there are two decks
// the mark runs out: both decks' cards wear it. A link is what separates them —
//
//     shape $Parent { of: Entity }
//
// and that is an ordinary shape carrying an ordinary Entity. Nothing new was added to the language for
// this; `$OnClick { handler: Entity }` in samples/web_app and `$Edge { to: Entity }` in
// samples/entities_bind.vein are the same mechanism wearing different names.
//
// Two decks of the SAME KIND is the case worth showing. One deck could have been done with a mark.
//
//   veinc run samples/entities_tree.vein --ticks 1
//
// INTERPRETER ONLY, and the compiler says so rather than going quiet — emitting this to C# prints
//
//     note: emit @Show not emitted — the reactive half stays on the interpreter.
//     note: Show.hear_Show: 'hear' not emitted — the backend covers run once / each tick / settled.
//
// so `shard Show` never runs there and the grouped output disappears. That is the deliberate M5 split
// (ROADMAP.md), not a defect. It does mean this sample cannot join tools/check-backend.sh, because the
// only way to carry a parent into a later phase today is an event. `shard Links` below would be
// checkable alone, but samples/entities_bind.vein already covers an Entity stored in a shape.
//
// DEPTH 1, AND WHY. Listing decks generically — "for each deck, list its children" — is a `target`
// inside a `target`, and the outer binding reads EMPTY there today (RULES.md 12c, roadmap item 5). So
// the parent has to reach the query as a VALUE rather than as an enclosing loop variable, which is what
// the @Show event below does. When that bug is fixed, `shard Show` collapses into one nested walk and
// the per-deck emit goes away.

bundle EntitiesTree by you {

    // A deck counts its own children WITHOUT knowing about them: each card contributes 1 to its parent's
    // `count`, and `folds sum` reconciles the five concurrent writes. The deck holds no list of children.
    //
    // `folds sum` ACCUMULATES onto the existing value — it does not recompute one. Run this with
    // `--ticks 3` and the counts read 2,3 then 4,6 then 6,9, because every tick contributes again. A
    // field that should hold "how many right now" has to be zeroed before the contributions land, and
    // `folds sum` will not do that for you. One tick is the honest reading here.
    shape $Deck { title: string, count: int folds sum }
    shape $Card { title: string }

    // Declared locally with EXACTLY the fields of `Vein.Core.Relations.$Parent`, and both halves of that
    // matter. Only a locally declared shape becomes a component — a `use`d one is a compile-time field
    // expansion and cannot be attached (RULES.md 15b) — but components unify by BARE NAME, so at link
    // time this is Core's `$Parent` (publicator `Relations`, not `Meta` — checked). VS0220 stays quiet only because the fields agree; change `of` to
    // anything else and the check fires, which is the point of it.
    shape $Parent { of: Entity }

    mark #Deck
    mark #Card

    builder Deck { $Deck          mark #Deck }
    builder Card { $Card $Parent  mark #Card }

    // Carries the parent ENTITY, which is how a later phase gets hold of it: `run once` commits a single
    // time for every shard, so Seed cannot query what it just spawned, and its locals are gone by the
    // next event.
    event @Show { deck: Entity, title: string }

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            // `as` names what the template built — without it the identity lives in a local the program
            // never sees, and there would be nothing to hand to the cards.
            bring Deck("Ship",    0) as ship
            bring Deck("Backlog", 0) as backlog

            bring Card("nav is a query",   ship)
            bring Card("bring … as",       ship)
            bring Card("packed ECS",       backlog)
            bring Card("TLS for peers",    backlog)
            bring Card("a JS backend",     backlog)

            // One emit per deck. This is the line item 5 removes: with a working nested walk, a single
            // `target $Deck #Deck as d { target $Card … }` would do it for however many decks exist.
            emit @Show { deck: ship,    title: "Ship" }
            emit @Show { deck: backlog, title: "Backlog" }
        }
    }

    shard Show {
        hear @Show as d {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "== " + d.title }

            // ONE flat loop, filtered against `d.deck` — a HEAR binding, which reads correctly inside a
            // `target` loop. An outer TARGET binding would not; that asymmetry is 12c exactly.
            target $Card $Parent as c {
                if c.Parent.of == d.deck {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   - " + c.Card.title }
                }
            }
        }
    }


    // ---- what `settled` is FOR, and why this sample needs it -----------------------------------------
    //
    // Five cards write two counters in the same tick. A field with `folds` collects each write as a
    // CONTRIBUTION from the writer's own snapshot; the reconciled value does not exist until the phase
    // commits. Read it during the tick and every deck still reads 0 — measured, not assumed:
    //
    //     during tick: Ship = 0        Backlog = 0
    //     settled:     Ship = 2        Backlog = 3
    //
    // That is rule 13 with a reason to care. Without folds this shard could have been `each tick` and
    // nothing would have differed, which is why the earlier version of this sample used `settled` for
    // no reason at all.
    //
    // ONE tick, deliberately: see the note on $Deck above — a second tick contributes again.
    shard Count {
        each tick {
            // A child writing its PARENT's component — `c.Parent.of` is an Entity, and an entity plus a
            // component name is a handle, so the write lands on the deck rather than on the card. The
            // deck holds no list of children and needs none.
            target $Card $Parent as c { c.Parent.of.Deck.count += 1 }
        }
    }

    shard Tally {
        settled {
            target $Deck #Deck as d {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: d.Deck.title + " holds " + d.Deck.count + " card(s)" }
            }
        }
    }
    // The same relationship as raw data rather than grouped output, so it is visible that nothing is
    // stored twice: a card knows its parent, and a deck knows nothing about its children.
    shard Links {
        settled {
            target $Card $Parent as c {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: "card e" + Entity + " '" + c.Card.title + "' -> parent e" + c.Parent.of
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/shape_marks.vein`

**Shapes that bring their marks** — Shapes declare marks in their body ($GameCamera brings three, $Shield brings #Shielded, $Ghost brings #Ghost), so attaching or unattaching the shape adds or removes the marks in the same commit. The shield is attached and dropped on alternate frames, and the 'carrying' and 'marked' counts always match.  
Run: `veinc run samples/shape_marks.vein --ticks 4`  
Teaches: marks declared inside a shape body · attach/unattach carry the shape's marks in the same commit · a builder including a mark-bringing shape is an identity template · builders say what a thing HAS · runtime attach with its marks  
Difficulty 4/5 · video value: good  
Notes: In tools/check-backend.sh (4 frames). Off-by-one wording: the comment on line 44 says "Four marks" and the program prints "cameras wearing all four marks", but $GameCamera declares only THREE marks (#CameraFollow, #Rendered, #Cullable); zoom is a field. The presenter should not repeat 'four'.

Real output of `veinc run samples/shape_marks.vein --ticks 4`:
```text
shield: carrying 0, marked 0
cameras wearing all four marks: 1
identities from a builder with no mark line: 1
shield: carrying 1, marked 1
cameras wearing all four marks: 1
identities from a builder with no mark line: 1
shield: carrying 0, marked 0
cameras wearing all four marks: 1
identities from a builder with no mark line: 1
shield: carrying 1, marked 1
cameras wearing all four marks: 1
identities from a builder with no mark line: 1
```

```vein
// shape_marks.vein — a shape that BRINGS its marks, so the two are never a frame apart.
//
//   veinc run samples/shape_marks.vein --ticks 4
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh, because the marks are a LOWERING desugar: one shape declaration turns into
// an `AddTag` beside every `AddComponent` and a `RemoveTag` beside every `RemoveComponent`, in `bring`
// and in a bare `attach` alike. Both runtimes read that same IR, and a diff is what proves the pairing
// survives into generated C#.
//
// THE PROBLEM IT SOLVES. A mark is how other bundles find an identity without knowing its data — a
// capability bundle writes `target #CameraFollow` and never names `$GameCamera`. Before this, the two
// had to be written apart:
//
//     builder Hero { $GameCamera   mark #CameraFollow }      // fine, but only for identities THIS
//                                                            // builder makes
//     shard Adopt { settled { target $GameCamera as c { mark c #CameraFollow } } }   // …and this,
//                                                            // for every other way one arrives
//
// That adoption shard is the trap. A structural change commits at the phase boundary (RULES 11/12b), so
// the shard cannot see the identity until the frame after it was brought, and cannot mark it until the
// frame after that. A `target` merely misses for a frame and catches up. An EVENT does not: a `@KeyDown`
// in that window hits a shard whose target filters on a mark not yet applied, and there is no retry for
// an event — it is dropped, not delayed. The bug looks like input that misses on the first frame.
//
// THE RULE (RULES 15c). A mark written in a shape body means: carrying this shape is what wearing this
// mark means. Attach adds it, `unattach` removes it, and both go into the same phase's command list —
// so they commit together and no window exists at all. Marks stay a set in the store, with no refcount,
// so detaching does remove a mark that was also set by hand; the alternative is a mark left on an
// identity whose data is gone, which every query then matches and none can read.
//
// AND ONE CONSEQUENCE WORTH STATING. `builder Ghost` below has no `mark` line, yet it builds an identity
// — because the shape it includes brings one. Without that, moving `mark #Ghost` out of a builder and
// into its shape would silently turn an identity template into one that emits a fragment, and `bring`
// would stop creating anything with nothing reported.

bundle ShapeMarks by you {

    mark #CameraFollow
    mark #Rendered
    mark #Cullable
    mark #Ghost
    mark #Shielded

    // Four marks, comma-separated among the fields. A shape body still takes fields and nothing else
    // (VS0007) — a mark is not a field, it is what carrying the fields MEANS.
    shape $GameCamera { zoom: float, #CameraFollow, #Rendered, #Cullable }

    // The other spelling, reading like a builder's. One shape, one mark.
    shape $Shield { hp: int
                    mark #Shielded }

    shape $Ghost { alpha: float, #Ghost }

    shape $Actor { n: int }

    // Says what the thing HAS. Never separately what it IS.
    builder Cam { $Actor   $GameCamera }

    // No `mark` member anywhere, and still an identity template.
    builder Ghost { $Ghost }

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            bring Cam(1, 2.5)
            bring Ghost(0.5)
        }
    }

    // Frame 2: a shape attached at RUNTIME, long after any builder ran. Its mark comes with it — the
    // desugar is on the attach, not on the template.
    shard Guard {
        settled {
            target $Actor as b {
                attach $Shield to b { hp: 7 }
            }
        }
    }

    // Frame 3: and off again. `carrying` and `marked` below move together, never one without the other.
    shard Drop {
        settled {
            target $Actor $Shield as b {
                unattach $Shield from b
            }
        }
    }

    // The whole proof is that these two counts are equal on every line. A frame where the shape is on
    // and the mark is not — or the reverse — is the bug this rule exists to remove.
    shard Watch {
        settled {
            var carrying = 0
            target $Actor $Shield as b { carrying = carrying + 1 }
            var marked = 0
            target $Actor #Shielded as b { marked = marked + 1 }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "shield: carrying " + carrying + ", marked " + marked }

            var cams = 0
            target $GameCamera #CameraFollow #Rendered #Cullable as c { cams = cams + 1 }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "cameras wearing all four marks: " + cams }

            var ghosts = 0
            target $Ghost #Ghost as g { ghosts = ghosts + 1 }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "identities from a builder with no mark line: " + ghosts }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/characters.vein`

**Characters without a char type** — Classifies characters with ordinary one-line fns, validates six words as identifiers (a letter or _ first, then letters/digits/_), ROT13-encodes "attack at dawn" with code/chr and a modulo, and shows that comparison is case-sensitive and ordinal.  
Run: `veinc run samples/characters.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: a character is a one-character string · ordinary fn classifiers with range comparisons · string indexing w[0] · words() and chars() return lists for target · code/chr arithmetic (ROT13) · case folding with lower() · shard-level var · one event heard by several shards  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Not in tools/check-backend.sh. The header's "INTERPRETER ONLY. None of these emit to C#" is stale: RULES 28a says s[i], len, code, chr, chars, upper, lower and string ordering all compile (diffed by samples/entities_chars.vein), and samples/text_split_join.vein, which covers split/lines/words, is also in check-backend now. RULES 28 names this file as the whole rule as a running program. Overlaps samples/entities_chars.vein.

Real output of `veinc run samples/characters.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
identifier check
  ok       count
  ok       _x
  rejected  9lives   starts with '9'
  rejected  has-dash   contains '-'
  ok       ok_2
  ok       Zeta
rot13: nggnpx ng qnja
ordinal: Zeta is not zeta
lowered: Zeta is zeta
and every capital sorts before every lowercase
```

```vein
// characters.vein — comparing characters, with no type and no ceremony.
//
//   veinc run samples/characters.vein --ticks 1
//
// A CHARACTER IS A ONE-CHARACTER STRING. `s[0]` yields one, `==` compares it, and `<` orders it — so a
// character needs no `char` type, no `'a'` literal, no `$Char` shape and no `&Char` builder. All of
// that would be ceremony around something the language already has: a shape is for data an identity
// carries, and a character carried by nothing is just a value.
//
// WHAT WAS ACTUALLY MISSING was not a type. `<` `>` `<=` `>=` used to convert both sides with AsDouble,
// which is 0 for any non-numeric string — so `c >= "a" and c <= "z"` was `0 >= 0 and 0 <= 0`, TRUE FOR
// EVERY STRING. An is-a-letter test written the obvious way said yes to "!" and to "5", and said it
// silently. Two strings now compare ORDINALLY, the same way `target … by Shape.field` already sorted
// them, so an operator and the sort cannot disagree.
//
// THE FOUR SPLITS, all handing back a list so `target` walks any of them:
//
//   split(text, sep)   exact separator, keeps empties      a blank CSV column is still a column
//   lines(text)        strips the line ending              a Windows file leaves \r on every line
//   words(text)        runs of whitespace, no empties      "a  b" is two words
//   chars(text)        one entry per character             this file
//
// AND THE THREE THAT ORDERING CANNOT DO:
//
//   code(c) / chr(n)   codepoint out and back              `chr(code(c) + 1)` is the next letter
//   upper / lower      case                                comparison is ordinal, so "A" is not "a"
//
// INTERPRETER ONLY. None of these emit to C# — `veinc emit` says so per call rather than producing a
// file that quietly computes something else. Character work belongs in a shard you run, not one you
// compile to the backend.

bundle Characters by you {

    // ---- classification, written the obvious way ------------------------------------------------
    //
    // These are ordinary `fn`s. That is the whole argument against a `char` keyword: with ordering
    // fixed, every classifier anyone needs is one comparison, and a language that added a type for this
    // would be charging a type for something a range test already answers.

    fn isDigit(c: string) return bool { return c >= "0" and c <= "9" }
    fn isLower(c: string) return bool { return c >= "a" and c <= "z" }
    fn isUpper(c: string) return bool { return c >= "A" and c <= "Z" }
    fn isLetter(c: string) return bool { return isLower(c) or isUpper(c) }

    // `_` is one character and compares like any other. No escape, no special case.
    fn isIdentChar(c: string) return bool { return isLetter(c) or isDigit(c) or c == "_" }
```
