```vein
    // ---- behaviour that belongs to every page --------------------------------------------------------
    // The nav is on every page, so the theme toggle and the clock it contains have to be too. Same
    // shape as Styles: emitted on every request, before any route decides what the page contains.

    shard Behaviour {
        hear @Request as r {
            // The @Script half of the theme. &ThemeToggle is the other end of the palette's `.dark`
            // contract — the CSS defines the class, this puts it on <html> and remembers it.
            bring ThemeToggle()
            bring Clock("clk")
        }
    }

    // ---- pages -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    // &Open and &Close cannot nest — each is its own fragment — but the indentation between them shows
    // the nesting the fragments will produce once the view concatenates them.

    // The home page's content is a WORLD, not a list of literals. `run once` runs before the boot event
    // on every render — `veinc serve` gives each request its own interpreter, so this is re-seeded per
    // request and no page can ever see the last visitor's state.
    //
    // Making them identities is what buys the `target` below: #Featured filters, a second shard could
    // fold a score into them without knowing who else writes it, and a card can be destroyed. Four
    // literal `bring` lines could express none of that.
    shard Catalog {
        run once {
            // The nav, in order. Adding a route is one line here — the SF renders whatever it finds.
            bring NavItem("/",        "home")
            bring NavItem("/docs",    "docs")
            bring NavItem("/counter", "counter")
            bring NavItem("/greet",   "greet")

            // One line per card. Nothing here needs the identity back, so the whole spawn/attach/mark
            // trio collapses into the template — which is what an identity builder is for.
            bring Featured("Shapes",   "Data lives in $Shape — identity components with folds.")
            bring Featured("Shards",   "Behaviour reacts: hear an event, emit more.")
            bring Featured("Builders", "A builder's output field picks its channel: markup, css or code.")
            bring Featured("One page", "These four cards are four separate @Html fragments.")
            bring Featured("where are we ", "LOST IN THE FRAGMENt OF THE WORLD")
        }
    }

    shard Home {
        hear @Request as r {
            if r.path == "/" {
                navBar(r.path)
                bring Hero("VeinScript", "A website assembled entirely from events.")
                bring Open("main")
                    bring OpenAs("div", "grid")
                        // The grid is a query. `target` works inside a `hear` — the same statement the
                        // ECS schedules use — so the page renders whatever the world currently holds,
                        // in spawn order, and adding a card is an `attach` rather than an edit here.
                        target $Card #Featured as self {
                            bring Card(self.Card.title, self.Card.body)
                        }
                    bring Close("div")
                bring Close("main")
                bring Footer("Served by veinc serve")
            }
        }
    }

    // /docs is not here. It lives in shards/Reference.vein, which BundleLoader merges into this bundle:
    // a bundle is its main file PLUS every fragment under `publicators/` and `shards/`. The reference it
    // serves is ~1300 fragments, and putting them in this file would bury the five routes it documents.
    // Nothing about the mechanism changes — `bring` still queues, @Render still fires last.

    // ---- JavaScript, which is where the third channel earns its place -------------------------------

    // The elements and their behaviour, seeded ONCE. Spawning and the default wiring share this block
    // deliberately: `run once` commits a single time for every shard, so a separate wiring shard would
    // find nothing to wire — the entities it wanted do not exist until the phase ends.
    shard CounterSeed {
        run once {
            // Three builders, four brings, no `spawn`. `as out` names the identity so the handler
            // bodies can address it.
            bring Readout("div", "count", "0") as out

            // The bodies are COMPOSED, not quoted. `addNumber(domId(out), 1)` is the same JavaScript
            // the long concatenation used to spell by hand, but the pieces are named functions the
            // editor can complete (`*Vein.Web.Js.`) and hover — and the element id still comes from the
            // identity, so nothing is typed twice.
            bring Btn("button", "", "+1",    addNumber(domId(out),  2))
            bring Btn("button", "", "-1",    addNumber(domId(out), -2))
            bring Btn("button", "", "reset", text(domId(out), "0"))
        }
    }

    // Rendering is a query, exactly as the home grid is: the page shows whatever the world holds, in
    // spawn order. Adding a button is an `attach` in the shard above, not an edit here.
    shard CounterPage {
        hear @Request as r {
            if r.path == "/counter" {
                navBar(r.path)
                bring Hero("Counter", "The page reacts in the browser, not on the server.")
                bring Open("main")
                    // Every element is a `$Element` rather than a shape per kind, because `target` binds
                    // one shape and cannot ask an entity what else it carries — a page rebuilt by query
                    // has to be uniform. The MARK is what separates them: the readout wears #Screen
                    // alone, a button wears #Screen #Button, so each loop below is flat.
                    //
                    // Two loops rather than one because the buttons need a wrapper around them and the
                    // readout must stay outside it. Both are ordered by entity id, which is spawn order.
                    target $Element #Screen as e {
                        if e.Element.tag == "div" {
                            bring Raw("<div class='" + e.Element.cls + "' id='" + domId(Entity) + "'>" +
                                      e.Element.text + "</div>")
                        }
                    }

                    bring OpenAs("div", "row")
                        // A button and its function are ONE identity, so `fnName(Entity)` here and in
                        // the @Script loop below name the same thing by construction — there is no
                        // reference between them that could go stale.
                        target $Element $Handler #Button as b {
                            bring Raw("<button id='" + domId(Entity) + "' onclick='" +
                                      fnName(Entity) + "()'>" + b.Element.text + "</button>")
                        }
                    bring Close("div")

                    bring 2 JumpLine()
                    bring Link("/", "back home")
                bring Close("main")

                // Every handler in the world becomes an @Script fragment. Same shape as a CSS rule or a
                // paragraph, different channel — and now also a thing a shard can find. The name comes
                // from the identity, so it matches the `onclick` above by construction.
                target $Handler as h { bring Define(fnName(Entity), h.Handler.body) }

                bring Footer("Three functions, three @Script fragments")
            }
        }
    }


    // ---- just for fun: the elements change colour every 2 seconds -----------------------------------
    //
    // In the BROWSER, not on the server, and that distinction is the whole lesson. `every 2` is a
    // wall-clock schedule that runs where a real clock lives — `veinc run`, a built .exe. `veinc serve`
    // builds a fresh interpreter per request and starts no timers, so a server-side `every 2` would
    // never fire, and even if it did, the next request would begin from a world that never saw it.
    //
    // What CAN change a page after it is served is the page itself. So this shard walks the same
    // identities the page rendered and ships one interval per element — behaviour composed from
    // `*Vein.Web.Js`, not a hand-written blob.
    shard Tint {
        hear @Request as r {
            if r.path == "/counter" {
                // The palette, once. `raw` is the escape hatch for the bit the vocabulary has no word
                // for — declaring a JavaScript variable.
                bring Js(raw("var tint=['#e2504a','#3fb27f','#4a7fe2','#e0a33c'];"))

                // One interval per element, phase-shifted by the ENTITY ID so they do not all change
                // together — the identity is doing real work here, not just naming a DOM node.
                target $Element #Screen as e {
                    bring Js(everyMs(2000,
                             setStyleExpr(domId(Entity), "color",
                                          "tint[((Date.now()/2000|0)+" + Entity + ")%tint.length]")))
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- a form: the part a path alone cannot express -----------------------------------------------

    shard Greet {
        hear @Request as r {
            if r.path == "/greet" {
                navBar(r.path)

                if r.method == "GET" {
                    bring Hero("Greet", "A form, posted back to the same path.")
                    bring Open("main")
                        // &Form is self-contained because one fragment cannot wrap others: it carries
                        // its own input and submit, and &Controls styles both.
                        bring Form("/greet", "who", "send")
                    bring Close("main")
                }

                if r.method == "POST" {
                    // `who` came from the form body. It is an ordinary payload field by the time it
                    // reaches here — the same place a query string would have put it.
                    bring Hero("Hello " + r.who, "Received by POST.")
                    bring Open("main")
                        bring Card("method", r.method)
                        bring Card("raw body", r.body)
                        bring Link("/greet", "again")
                    bring Close("main")
                }

                bring Footer("method and body arrive on @Request")
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- assembly ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    // The trigger has to be a separate event. `bring` QUEUES a fragment event rather than writing
    // anything, and the queue does not drain until every @Request handler has finished — so a view that
    // answered on @Request would always see an empty page. @Render queues behind the fragments.
    //
    // Which makes Kernel's POSITION load-bearing: it is written below every page shard, so its @Render is
    // queued after their fragments. That still holds with /docs living in shards/Reference.vein, because
    // fragments are merged BEFORE the main file's members — a bundle's closing shard is in its main file,
    // and it keeps the last word. Move this shard up and the page goes blank.
    shard Kernel {
        hear @Request as r { emit @Render { path: r.path } }
    }

    ShardView Page {
        var html: string
        var css: string
        var js: string

        hear @Html as f   { html += f.markup }
        hear @Style as s  { css += s.css }
        hear @Script as c { js += c.code }

        hear @Render as v {
            // No HTML means no route matched, so stay silent and let serve answer 404. The stylesheet
            // arrived regardless, which is exactly why the check is on `html` and not on the page.
            if not (html == "") {
                emit @Response { status: 200, body: "<!doctype html><html><head><meta charset=\"utf-8\"><title>VeinScript</title><style>" + css + "</style></head><body>" + html + "<script>" + js + "</script></body></html>" }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/ui_view.vein`

**HUD layout against the view** — Lays out three HUD widgets (top-left, top-right, centred) against a $View whose width and height the program reads at runtime, using a nested target over the view and the #Hud widgets.  
Run: `veinc run samples/ui_view.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: $View host-provided shape · spawn / attach / mark by hand · nested target (both bindings readable) · component field assignment · float arithmetic for anchoring · settled phase · need Vein.Math / Vein.UI  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Works. In tools/check-backend.sh. A real host attaches $View; this sample attaches it itself because `veinc run` has no window. Widgets are matched by their text content, which the file itself calls a sample-only shortcut (a real HUD would carry a mark per corner).

Real output of `veinc run samples/ui_view.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
view 1280x720
SCORE 0 at 0,0 @24px
01:30 at 0,0 @24px
PAUSED at 0,0 @48px
view 1280x720
SCORE 0 at 16,16 @24px
01:30 at 1168,16 @24px
PAUSED at 520,332 @48px
```

```vein
// ui_view.vein — laying a HUD out against the edges of a view whose size the program does not know
// until it asks.
//
//   veinc run samples/ui_view.vein --ticks 2
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh, because the arithmetic is the whole feature: a widget pinned to the RIGHT
// edge is `view.width - margin - width`, and if the two runtimes disagree about a float the HUD is in a
// different place compiled than interpreted — which is exactly the kind of thing nobody notices until a
// score is half off the screen in a shipped build.
//
// THE PROBLEM. `Vein.Math.$Rect` is absolute pixels from the top-left, and until now nothing in the
// standard library said how wide the surface was. A score in the top-left is fine — it is at (16, 16)
// on every screen. A timer in the top-RIGHT could not be written at all: the best a program could do
// was guess a number that is correct at one window size and wrong at every other.
//
// WHY A SHAPE AND NOT AN EVENT. `@Resized` would announce a change; it would not answer the question.
// A game that never resizes would hear nothing and still not know how wide it is, and any shard wanting
// the width would have had to be listening earlier and stored it. `$View` is an identity the host keeps
// current, so any shard can ask at any time — the same arrangement a camera already has.
//
// `@Print` is reached by its qualified path rather than a `need "Vein.Console"`, because that bundle
// exports a `spawn` and would shadow the built-in one every line below depends on (VS0217).
//
// The host attaches `$View`. This sample attaches it itself, because `veinc run` has no window: that is
// the one thing here a real host does differently, and it is one `attach` either way.

bundle UiView by you {

    need "Vein.Math"
    need "Vein.UI"

    mark #Hud

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            // Standing in for the host. A real one keeps this current as the window changes.
            let screen = spawn()
            attach $View to screen { width: 1280.0, height: 720.0 }

            // Three widgets: one pinned to each top corner, one centred. Their rects are filled in
            // below, once the view is readable — a `bring` here could only guess.
            let score = spawn()
            attach $Text to score { content: "SCORE 0" }
            attach $Font to score { size: 24.0 }
            attach $Rect to score { x: 0.0, y: 0.0, width: 160.0, height: 28.0 }
            mark score #Hud

            let timer = spawn()
            attach $Text to timer { content: "01:30" }
            attach $Font to timer { size: 24.0 }
            attach $Rect to timer { x: 0.0, y: 0.0, width: 96.0, height: 28.0 }
            mark timer #Hud

            let title = spawn()
            attach $Text to title { content: "PAUSED" }
            attach $Font to title { size: 48.0 }
            attach $Rect to title { x: 0.0, y: 0.0, width: 240.0, height: 56.0 }
            mark title #Hud
        }
    }

    // A structural change commits at the phase boundary (RULES 11/12b), so the layout runs a frame after
    // the spawn — which is what a real HUD does too: the host attaches `$View` before the first frame,
    // and the layout reads it on every frame after.
    shard Layout {
        settled {
            target $View as v {
                target $Text $Rect $Font #Hud as w {
                    // Pinned by what the widget SAYS it is. `content` is the only thing distinguishing
                    // these three, which is honest for a sample and is not how a real HUD would do it —
                    // a real one carries a mark per corner, and that is a kit's job rather than the
                    // standard library's.
                    var margin = 16.0

                    if w.Text.content == "SCORE 0" {
                        w.Rect.x = margin
                        w.Rect.y = margin
                    }
                    if w.Text.content == "01:30" {
                        // THE LINE THAT COULD NOT BE WRITTEN. Right edge, minus the widget, minus the
                        // margin — and it is correct at 1280 wide and at any other width.
                        w.Rect.x = v.View.width - w.Rect.width - margin
                        w.Rect.y = margin
                    }
                    if w.Text.content == "PAUSED" {
                        w.Rect.x = (v.View.width - w.Rect.width) / 2.0
                        w.Rect.y = (v.View.height - w.Rect.height) / 2.0
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    shard Report {
        settled {
            target $View as v {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "view " + v.View.width + "x" + v.View.height }
            }
            target $Text $Rect $Font #Hud as w {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: w.Text.content + " at " + w.Rect.x + "," + w.Rect.y + " @" + w.Font.size + "px"
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/ui_widgets.vein`

**UI widgets as identities** — Attaches Vein.UI widget shapes ($Button, $Field, $Text) to spawned identities, relying on declared defaults for optional button fields, mutates the focused field each tick, and walks one query per widget shape the way a renderer would.  
Run: `veinc run samples/ui_widgets.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: widgets are identities, not markup · attach with shape defaults · several marks on one identity · component state changed by assignment · one query per shape for a renderer · need Vein.UI  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Works. In tools/check-backend.sh. Output order is query order: all $Text, then $Button, then $Field. STALE HEADER (lines 20-23): it says the C# backend does not emit a `hear` for an event declared in another bundle, and that samples/stdlib_events.vein is interpreter-only. Both are out of date. Commit 25642f0 ("Cross-bundle events compile, unless their meaning is transport") made pure-data events such as *Vein.UI.Interaction.@Clicked compile. stdlib_events.vein, which hears @Clicked, is now in tools/check-backend.sh (3 frames). The backend still refuses only transport events such as @Fetch and @ReadFile. This file simply does not use @Clicked, so do not repeat the header's reason on camera.

Real output of `veinc run samples/ui_widgets.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
text    Preferences
button  Save  (no id)
button  Cancel  id=btn-cancel class=muted onclick=dismiss
field   [Ada]  placeholder=your name
text    Preferences
button  Save  (no id)
button  Cancel  id=btn-cancel class=muted onclick=dismiss
field   [Ada]  placeholder=your name
```

```vein
// ui_widgets.vein — Vein.UI's widget shapes, as identities.
//
//   veinc run samples/ui_widgets.vein --ticks 2
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh. stdlib/UI.vein had no sample at all, so nothing checked that its shapes
// still resolve into something a program can attach and read back.
//
// WIDGETS ARE IDENTITIES, NOT MARKUP. That is the whole design of Vein.UI and the reason it is separate
// from Vein.Web: a `$Button` is state — a label, an id, a class, a handler name — and a RENDERER turns
// it into pixels or into DOM. stdlib/Web.vein's `Button` builder is one such renderer, and it includes
// `*Vein.UI.Widgets.$Button` rather than declaring a second one, so a button attached for a native
// backend and a button rendered to HTML are one identity and not two.
//
// `use UI` AND A BARE `attach`, which is the readable form and, until recently, the broken one: the
// component attached and every read of it came back empty, because a shape reaching a bundle through
// `use` was never imported into the module. The other route — a builder including
// `*Vein.UI.Widgets.$Button` — is what samples/motion.vein uses. Both work; this file exercises the
// one that reads like ordinary code.
//
// NO @Clicked HERE. Vein.UI's interaction events are the natural other half, and the C# backend does
// not emit a `hear` for an event declared in another bundle — so a sample using them cannot be diffed,
// and a sample in check-backend.sh that only one runtime can run is not a differential test.
// samples/stdlib_events.vein covers them, interpreter-only and marked as such.

bundle UiWidgets by you {

    need "Vein.UI"

    mark #Widget
    mark #Focused

    shard Build {
        run once {
            // A button with only its label. The other three fields carry declared defaults — an id, a
            // class and a handler are all optional in any renderer, exactly as `<button>Go</button>` is
            // a complete button — so this is a one-field attach and the rest arrive as "".
            let save = spawn()
            attach $Button to save { label: "Save" }
            mark save #Widget

            // And one with everything a renderer resolves.
            let cancel = spawn()
            attach $Button to cancel { label: "Cancel", id: "btn-cancel", class: "muted", onclick: "dismiss" }
            mark cancel #Widget

            let name = spawn()
            attach $Field to name { value: "", placeholder: "your name" }
            mark name #Widget
            mark name #Focused

            let title = spawn()
            attach $Text to title { content: "Preferences" }
            mark title #Widget
        }
    }

    // Typing into the focused field. A widget's state is ordinary component data, so changing what a
    // field holds is an assignment and not a message to a toolkit.
    shard Type {
        each tick {
            target $Field #Focused as f {
                if f.Field.value == "" { f.Field.value = "Ada" }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- what a renderer would walk ----------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // Three separate queries rather than one over #Widget, because a renderer needs to know WHICH shape
    // it is drawing — and a query names the shape it reads. That is the same reason `target` takes a
    // shape rather than only a mark: the binding has a type because the query said so.

    shard Render {
        settled {
            target $Text #Widget as t {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "text    " + t.Text.content }
            }

            target $Button #Widget as b {
                // An empty id is the default doing its job, and worth showing rather than hiding.
                if b.Button.id == "" {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "button  " + b.Button.label + "  (no id)" }
                } else {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                        text: "button  " + b.Button.label + "  id=" + b.Button.id +
                              " class=" + b.Button.class + " onclick=" + b.Button.onclick
                    }
                }
            }

            target $Field #Widget as f {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: "field   [" + f.Field.value + "]  placeholder=" + f.Field.placeholder
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

---

# Consoles and networks

## `samples/console.vein`

**Console I/O as events** — The smallest interactive program. On boot it prints a prompt, and for every line typed at stdin it prints "you said <line>" back. It reads and writes the terminal only through stdlib events.  
Run: `veinc run samples/console.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: bundle · shard · start event (program entry) · event declaration · hear handler with `as` payload binding · emit with payload fields · fully qualified stdlib reference (*Author.Bundle.Publicator.@Event) · console I/O as events  
Difficulty 1/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 4  
Notes: Compiles cleanly (`veinc ir` exit 0, no diagnostics). Not run because it waits for typed input. `bundle App` has no `by <author>` clause, unlike most newer samples.

```vein
// console.vein — an interactive console program. It uses the Vein.Console stdlib events explicitly:
//   emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: … }   → writes a line to stdout
//   hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Input as i { … }     → fires for each line typed at stdin
// The runtime bridges these to the terminal. Run it:
//   veinc run samples/console.vein         (type lines; Ctrl+Z on Windows / Ctrl+D on Unix to end)

bundle App {
    start @Boot { }        // this program's entry — fired once when it runs
    event @Boot { }

    shard Main {
        hear @Boot as b {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "Type something (Ctrl+Z / Ctrl+D to end):" }
        }
        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Input as i {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "you said " + i.text }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/console_builder.vein`

**Spawning consoles with a builder** — On boot it opens two new named console windows, #Server and #Client1, each printing its own first line. It does this by calling the stdlib Console builder with `bring`.  
Run: `veinc run samples/console_builder.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: builders and `bring` · the `&` builder sigil · cross-bundle builder reference · a builder with no output field emits @<BuilderName> (rule 5) · marks as console addresses · spawning named console windows  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Compiles cleanly (`veinc ir` exit 0, no diagnostics). Not run because it opens real console windows (second processes). It teaches the same thing as three_consoles.vein, which uses the emit form.

```vein
// samples/console_builder.vein — use a stdlib BUILDER cross-bundle via the `&` sigil.
//
//   veinc run samples/console_builder.vein
//
// `bring *Vein.Console.Io.&Console(name, firsttext)` resolves the stdlib Console builder (the `&` is the
// builder sigil, like @ for events / $ for shapes / # for marks) and desugars to emit @Console { … },
// spawning a named console window. This is the builder equivalent of `emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console`.
//
// NOTE: qualified `bring` resolves the stdlib builder at lower-time, so it works under `veinc run` /
// the Workbench (where stdlib/ is locatable). A `veinc build` standalone .exe can't locate stdlib to
// re-lower, so prefer the event form (emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console) for shipped exes.

bundle ConsoleBuilderDemo by you {

    start @Boot { }
    event @Boot { }

    shard Launcher {
        hear @Boot as b {
            bring *Vein.Console.Io.&Console(#Server,  "Server ready.")
            bring *Vein.Console.Io.&Console(#Client1, "Client 1 ready.")
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/console_broadcast.vein`

**Broadcast: one, random, all** — Opens three console windows (#Alpha, #Beta, #Gamma). Every 2 seconds the launching window sends one message to Alpha only, one to a randomly picked console and one to all three. Every window prints what it receives, prefixed with the sender's name.  
Run: `veinc run samples/console_broadcast.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: run once startup schedule · every N wall-clock timer · shard var state · here() (this console's own address) · pick() random choice from a list · target over a plain collection · @Send / @Message between consoles · list literals  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 24  
Notes: Compiles cleanly (`veinc ir` exit 0, no diagnostics). Not run here because it opens three console windows (second processes). The launching window keeps broadcasting until its own input ends (Ctrl+Z then Enter on Windows, Ctrl+D on Unix) or it is closed. Closing the three spawned windows does not stop it, and they exit by themselves when the launching window does. The header offers both `veinc build`, which writes console_broadcast.exe, and `veinc run`.

```vein
// samples/console_broadcast.vein — spawn three console applications, then write to them on a timer.
//
//   veinc build samples/console_broadcast.vein     →  console_broadcast.exe   (double-click)
//   veinc run   samples/console_broadcast.vein     →  same thing, from a terminal
//
// Every 2 seconds the main window sends three messages, one of each kind:
//   * to ONE fixed console          (#Alpha)
//   * to a RANDOM one               (pick(consoles))
//   * to ALL of them                (target consoles as c)
//
// Stdlib events are fully qualified so their origin is obvious (*Vein.Console.Io.* = stdlib):
//   @Console → spawn a named console   ·  @Send → write a line to console `to`
//   @Message → a line that arrived     ·  @Print → write a line to THIS console
//
// The catch worth understanding: a spawned console re-runs THIS SAME PROGRAM, so its `every 2` block
// fires too. `here()` is this console's own address, so `here() == #Main` means "only the window the
// user actually launched broadcasts". #Main is the reserved address of that root console.

bundle Broadcast by you {

    shard Launcher {
        // `run once` is the startup schedule — it runs before any event is heard.
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print   { text: "main — opening three consoles, broadcasting every 2s." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Alpha, firsttext: "Alpha ready — waiting for messages." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Beta,  firsttext: "Beta ready — waiting for messages." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Gamma, firsttext: "Gamma ready — waiting for messages." }
        }
    }

    shard Broadcaster {
        var n: int                      // shard-private state: which round we are on

        every 2 {
            if here() == #Main {
                n += 1
                let consoles = [#Alpha, #Beta, #Gamma]

                // 1. ONE fixed console.
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: #Alpha, text: "round " + n + " — for Alpha only" }

                // 2. A RANDOM one of them.
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: pick(consoles), text: "round " + n + " — you were picked" }

                // 3. ALL of them: `target` over a plain collection binds each element in turn.
                target consoles as c {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: c, text: "round " + n + " — broadcast to everyone" }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // Every console (including the root) prints what it receives. `from` is the sender's address.
    shard Inbox {
        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Message as m {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.from + ": " + m.text }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/console_chat.vein`

**Chat between spawned consoles** — The launching window opens five named consoles: #Server and #Client1 to #Client4. A line typed in any window is sent to #Server, #Client4 and #Main, and every window prints what it receives as "<sender> says: <text>".  
Run: `veinc build samples/console_chat.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: emit @Console to spawn named consoles · forwarding @Input as @Send · hear @Message with from/text · events crossing process boundaries (named pipes) · #Main root address  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Compiles cleanly (`veinc ir` exit 0, no diagnostics). Not run because it opens five console windows and needs typing. Most of it repeats three_consoles.vein (spawning) and console_broadcast.vein (send/receive).

```vein
// samples/console_chat.vein — messaging BETWEEN spawned console applications.
//
//   veinc build samples/console_chat.vein     →  console_chat.exe   (double-click)
//
// Everything stdlib is FULLY qualified, so origin is obvious (*Vein.Console.Io.* = stdlib; bare = local):
//   *Vein.Console.Io.@Console  → spawn a named console          (stdlib)
//   *Vein.Console.Io.@Input    → a line typed in THIS console   (stdlib)
//   *Vein.Console.Io.@Send     → send a line to console `to`     (stdlib)
//   *Vein.Console.Io.@Message  → a line received from `from`     (stdlib)
//   *Vein.Console.Io.@Print    → write a line to this console    (stdlib)
//
// The main window spawns Server + Client1..4. Type in ANY window → the line is sent to Server (and
// Client4), which prints "<who> says: <line>". That's an event crossing process boundaries (named pipe).

bundle ConsoleChat by you {

    start @Boot { }
    event @Boot { }                                    // the only local event — this bundle's entry

    shard Launcher {
        hear @Boot as b {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print   { text: "Main — type here; it goes to Server." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Server,  firsttext: "Server ready — messages appear here." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Client1, firsttext: "Client1 ready — type to message Server." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Client2, firsttext: "Client2 ready — type to message Server." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Client3, firsttext: "Client3 ready — type to message Server." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Client4, firsttext: "Client4 ready — type to message Server." }
        }
    }

    shard Chat {
        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Input as i {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: #Server,  text: i.text }        // I typed → send to Server
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: #Client4, text: i.text }        //          … and to Client4
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: #Main,    text: i.text }        //          … and back to root
        }
        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Message as m {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.from + " says: " + m.text }   // received → print
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/console_roles.vein`

**One program, four roles** — One file runs in four windows, and each behaves differently depending on its console address. Main launches the others, pings a random worker every 2 seconds, prints replies and reports closed workers. Alpha echoes, Beta counts and Gamma relays to Alpha.  
Run: `veinc build samples/console_roles.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: match on here() as a role switch · when arms and an else arm · @Undelivered for unreachable consoles · shard var state per process · pick() · every N timer · run once  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 24  
Notes: Compiles cleanly (`veinc ir` exit 0, no diagnostics). Not run because it opens three console windows and runs on timers. The Driver's leftover `when #Alpha` test arm has been there since commit 8e6432a, and lines 78-82 use tab indentation; consider cleaning up both before recording.

```vein
// samples/console_roles.vein — ONE program, four behaviours, chosen by which console it is running as.
//
//   veinc build samples/console_roles.vein     →  console_roles.exe   (double-click)
//
// A spawned console re-runs THIS SAME FILE, so every window executes every shard. What differs is
// `here()` — this process's own console address. `match here() { when #Alpha { … } }` is therefore a
// ROLE SWITCH: the window you launched is #Main and behaves as the launcher/driver, and each window it
// spawned takes the role named by its own address.
//
//   #Main   launches the others, then every 2s pings a random one, printing what it sends,
//           every reply it gets back, and any worker that has gone away (@Undelivered)
//   #Alpha  echoes what it is sent back to Main
//   #Beta   counts what it is sent and reports the running total
//   #Gamma  relays what it is sent to Alpha (so a ping to Gamma reaches Main in two hops)
//
// Compare samples/console_broadcast.vein, which uses `if here() == #Main` to keep ONE behaviour on the
// root; this is the general form of the same idea.

bundle Roles by you {

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            match here() {
                // Only the window the user launched opens the others — and a spawned console cannot
                // spawn further ones anyway, so the tree can never run away.
                when #Main {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print   { text: "Main — launching Alpha, Beta and Gamma." }
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Alpha, firsttext: "Alpha — I echo." }
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Beta,  firsttext: "Beta — I count." }
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Gamma, firsttext: "Gamma — I relay to Alpha." }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    shard Driver {
        var round: int

        every 2 {
            match here() {
                // The workers reach this block too — they simply match no arm and do nothing, which is
                // the whole point: one timer, one program, different work per identity.
                when #Main {
                    round += 1
                    let workers = [#Alpha, #Beta, #Gamma]
                    let dest = pick(workers)
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "→ " + dest + ": ping " + round }
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send  { to: dest, text: "ping " + round }
                }
                when #Alpha {
                    round += 1
                    let workers = [#Alpha, #Beta, #Gamma]
                    let dest = pick(workers)
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "arrow → " + #Beta + " : FROM ALPHA TEST " + round }
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send  { to: #Beta, text: " FROM ALPHA TEST " + round }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // A worker window can be CLOSED at any time — that is normal, not an error. The send then finds
    // nothing listening and comes back as @Undelivered, which is the only way a program can tell
    // "delivered" from "shouting into a void". Without this, closing the workers just makes Main go
    // quiet, and a silent window is indistinguishable from a dead one.
    shard Reachability {
        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Undelivered as u {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  (" + u.to + " is not running — closed?)" }
        }
    }

    shard Worker {
        var seen: int

        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Message as m {
            match here() {
                when #Alpha { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: #Main,  text: "Alpha echoes: " + m.text } }
                when #Gamma { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: #Alpha, text: "Gamma relays: " + m.text } }
				when #Beta {
				    seen += 1
				    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.from + " → " + m.text }
				    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send  { to: #Main, text: "Beta has now seen " + seen }
				}
                // #Main has no `when` arm — it is the one that reads the replies.
                else { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.from + " → " + m.text } }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/three_consoles.vein`

**Three consoles from one program** — The simplest spawning demo. On boot the main window prints one line and opens three named console windows (#Server, #Client1, #Client2), each of which prints its own first line.  
Run: `veinc build samples/three_consoles.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console to spawn a window · marks as console names · start/@Boot entry · bare local names vs fully qualified stdlib names  
Difficulty 1/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Compiles cleanly (`veinc ir` exit 0, no diagnostics). Not run as a whole because it opens three console windows. Each spawned window re-runs the program and its `hear @Boot` fires again. The spawns are skipped in a child but the @Print is not, so every child window shows its own first line followed by "main — opening three consoles.". A simulated child (VEIN_CONSOLE and VEIN_CONSOLE_FIRST set) printed "Server console — ready." then that line. It teaches the same thing as console_builder.vein (builder form) and console_chat.vein's Launcher, and it is the smallest of the three.

```vein
// samples/three_consoles.vein — spawn THREE named console applications from one program.
//
//   veinc build samples/three_consoles.vein     →  three_consoles.exe   (double-click)
//
// Everything from the standard library is written with its FULL qualified path, so you can see exactly
// what comes from where:
//   *Vein.Console.Io.@Print     → write a line to this console         (stdlib)
//   *Vein.Console.Io.@Console   → spawn a named console window         (stdlib)
// Bare names (@Boot, Launcher) are declared HERE, by this bundle.
//
// Spawning is just emitting the stdlib @Console event — no local builder, no `bring`. A spawned console
// never spawns again (its firsttext prints and it waits).

bundle ThreeConsoles by you {

    start @Boot { }
    event @Boot { }                                    // the only local event — this bundle's entry

    shard Launcher {
        hear @Boot as b {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print   { text: "main — opening three consoles." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Server,  firsttext: "Server console — ready." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Client1, firsttext: "Client 1 console — ready." }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Console { name: #Client2, firsttext: "Client 2 console — ready." }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/control_center.vein`

**Control centre across three launches** — The same file is started in two or three terminals under different VEIN_CONSOLE names. Control keeps a roster of workers as entities and relays each worker's line to every other worker. Alpha and Beta never address each other.  
Run: `VEIN_CONSOLE=Control veinc run samples/control_center.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: VEIN_CONSOLE sets here() for independently launched processes · match here() role switch with else · entities as a growing roster (spawn/attach/target) · shape declaration · relay to everyone except the sender · inequality as not (a == b) · destroy on @Undelivered · separate memory per process: only messages cross  
Difficulty 4/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Compiles cleanly (`veinc ir` exit 0, no diagnostics). Not run because it needs three terminals and typing. The terminal 2 and 3 commands are `VEIN_CONSOLE=Alpha veinc run samples/control_center.vein` and `VEIN_CONSOLE=Beta veinc run samples/control_center.vein`. The header includes a transcript of a real three-window run. The Relay handler is nearly identical to samples/chat/server.vein's, with console pipes where the chat uses the network.

```vein
// control_center.vein — ONE program, launched two or three times, the instances talking to each other.
//
//   Terminal 1:   VEIN_CONSOLE=Control veinc run samples/control_center.vein     ← start this first
//   Terminal 2:   VEIN_CONSOLE=Alpha   veinc run samples/control_center.vein
//   Terminal 3:   VEIN_CONSOLE=Beta    veinc run samples/control_center.vein
//
//   PowerShell:   $env:VEIN_CONSOLE="Alpha"; .\veinc.cmd run samples\control_center.vein
//
// Type a line in Alpha and it appears in Beta — having gone through Control, which is the whole point.
// Alpha and Beta never address each other; neither knows the other exists.
//
// An actual run of the three — Alpha typed one line, and note what each window did NOT see:
//
//   Control:  CONTROL — waiting for workers.        Alpha:  WORKER Alpha — type a line…
//             * Beta joined                                 (its own line never comes back)
//             Beta: reporting in
//             * Alpha joined                         Beta:  WORKER Beta — type a line…
//             Alpha: reporting in                           <- Alpha: reporting in
//             Alpha: hello from alpha                       <- Alpha: hello from alpha
//
// Beta sees Alpha's join too, because the relay forwards everything rather than inspecting text for
// messages it thinks are administrative. A control centre that reads its traffic to decide what to
// pass on is a control centre with two definitions of "a message" waiting to disagree.
//
// WHAT MAKES SEPARATE LAUNCHES FIND EACH OTHER. A console's address is a NAMED PIPE called
// `vein.console.<name>` (Ir/ConsoleBus.cs), and that name is MACHINE-GLOBAL — no process id, no session
// id, nothing instance-specific in it. So two `veinc run` processes on one machine are already reachable
// to each other; there is no connecting step, no port, and nothing to configure. `@Send { to: #Control }`
// finds whoever is listening as Control, whichever process that happens to be.
//
// This is also why running samples/console_roles.vein TWICE makes its windows talk across the two runs:
// both instances host `vein.console.Alpha`, and `NamedPipeServerStream` is opened with
// MaxAllowedServerInstances, so a send lands on WHICHEVER listener accepts first. Two instances of one
// program pool their consoles rather than isolating them. Deliberate here, surprising there.
//
// WHICH INSTANCE AM I. `here()` is this process's own address, and the runtime reads it from the
// VEIN_CONSOLE environment variable (`ConsoleLauncher.CurrentName` → `Interp._self` → `here()`), falling
// back to #Main. So `match here()` is a ROLE SWITCH across independently launched processes — the same
// switch samples/console_roles.vein uses for windows it spawned itself, minus the spawning.
//
// The distinction that matters: an address here is a NAME, and names are the thing a role switch can
// choose. samples/chat/ stays two files because its addresses are PORTS, which two processes cannot
// share and `here()` cannot invent — a different problem wearing the same clothes.
//
// MEMORY IS NOT SHARED, and it is worth being exact because it is the first thing people assume. Each
// process has its own `EntityStore`: no entity, no component, no shard `var` crosses. What crosses is
// MESSAGES. Control's roster below is not a view of anything — it is what Control has been TOLD, built
// out of the messages it received, and that is the honest shape for a control centre rather than a
// limitation to work around.
//
// STDIN KEEPS A PROCESS ALIVE. `veinc run` pumps stdin until EOF and then exits, so piping /dev/null
// into one of these ends it before anything arrives. Run them in real terminals.

bundle ControlCenter by you {

    // One row of the roster: a worker Control has heard from. `addr` is its console address, which is
    // what `@Send` needs to answer it.
    //
    // An ENTITY per worker, because a shard `var` holds one value and the language has no growable list
    // (samples/chat/server.vein makes the same choice for the same reason). Joining is spawning; the
    // roster is "which entities exist", so there is no second copy of that fact to drift.
    shape $Worker { addr: string }

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            match here() {
                when #Control {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "CONTROL — waiting for workers." }
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "Start others with VEIN_CONSOLE=Alpha / =Beta." }
                }

                // Everything that is not Control is a worker, including a plain `veinc run` with no
                // environment variable at all — that one is #Main, and it works exactly the same.
                else {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "WORKER " + here() + " — type a line and press Enter." }

                    // Announcing is what puts this process on Control's roster. Control is told the
                    // sender by the transport, so there is nothing to introduce ourselves with.
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: #Control, text: "reporting in" }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- the centre ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Relay {
        var known: bool

        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Message as m {
            match here() {
                when #Control {
                    // Is this a worker we already have? `target` is the only way to look through the
                    // roster, so the answer comes back on shard state rather than as a return value.
                    known = false
                    target $Worker as w {
                        if w.Worker.addr == m.from { known = true }
                    }

                    if not known {
                        let e = spawn()
                        attach $Worker to e { addr: m.from }
                        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "* " + m.from + " joined" }
                    }

                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.from + ": " + m.text }

                    // THE RELAY. Everyone except the sender — no `!=` in VeinScript (LANGUAGE.md §7),
                    // so inequality is `not (a == b)`. Without the guard the sender hears their own line
                    // come back, which reads as an echo bug rather than a broadcast.
                    //
                    // This is the line that makes the demo: Alpha addressed Control, and Beta receives.
                    target $Worker as w {
                        if not (w.Worker.addr == m.from) {
                            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: w.Worker.addr, text: m.from + ": " + m.text }
                        }
                    }
                }

                // A worker just shows what arrived. It reached us through Control, from a process that
                // does not know we exist.
                else { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  <- " + m.text } }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- what a worker types ------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Typing {
        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Input as i {
            match here() {
                // Control is a relay, not a participant — a line typed here goes nowhere on purpose.
                when #Control { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "(control relays; it does not chat)" } }
                else { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Send { to: #Control, text: i.text } }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- when the centre is not running -------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // A send that reaches nobody is NORMAL here: start a worker before Control and this is what you get,
    // rather than a hang or a silence. It is the only way a program can tell "delivered" from "shouting
    // into a void" — the same line stdlib/Net.vein draws with @Undelivered for peers.

    shard Reachability {
        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Undelivered as u {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "(no one is listening as " + u.to + " — is Control running?)" }

            // Control drops a worker that has gone away. `destroy` takes the identity AND its component;
            // unmarking would leave the row behind, and a returning worker would then be given a second
            // one for the same address. Safe mid-iteration: a query materialises its ids before the body
            // runs, and the destroy is queued for the commit point.
            target $Worker as w {
                if w.Worker.addr == u.to { destroy w }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/chat`

**Networked chat: server and clients** — A TCP chat. server.vein listens on port 9700 as #Server, keeps its clients as entities and relays each line to everyone except the sender. client.vein asks for your name, uses it as your network identity and forwards what you type. alice.vein and bob.vein are the older version, one file per user with fixed identities and ports.  
Files: `samples/chat/server.vein`, `samples/chat/client.vein`, `samples/chat/alice.vein`, `samples/chat/bob.vein`  
Run: `veinc run samples/chat/server.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: Vein.Net.Peer @Listen / @Link / @Send / @Message / @Undelivered · shared-secret keys and signed frames · audience barrier on a hear · entities as a dynamic roster · destroy when a client leaves · learning return routes from signed frames · fn used as a constant · var state is per shard  
Difficulty 4/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 27  
Notes: All four .vein files compile cleanly (`veinc check` prints ok). Not run because it needs a server and clients in separate terminals, network sockets and typing. As shipped, client.vein cannot reach a local server: ServerAddress() returns the placeholder "999.999.999.999:9700" (commit cbc8b96), so every send prints "(cannot reach 999.999.999.999:9700 — is the server running?)". Change it to "127.0.0.1:9700" before a local demo. The folder also holds client.exe (68 MB), which .gitignore excludes; it is left out of `files`. There is no vein.discovery file and no bundles/ subfolder. alice.vein and bob.vein are the same program apart from identity and port (9701 and 9702), and client.vein replaces them.

### `samples/chat/server.vein`

```vein
// samples/chat/server.vein — the chat SERVER. Start this first, then any number of clients.
//
//   Terminal 1:  veinc run samples/chat/server.vein
//   Terminal 2:  veinc run samples/chat/client.vein     ← type your name, then chat
//   Terminal 3:  veinc run samples/chat/client.vein     ← and again, any number of times
//
// The roster is DYNAMIC, and $Client is how. A shard's `var` holds one value and the language has no
// growable list — but it has entities, and that is the growing collection: `spawn()` makes one,
// `attach $Client` gives it data, and `target $Client` iterates them. Joining is spawning an entity;
// leaving is destroying it.
//
// There is deliberately no `#Online` mark. Existence IS membership — a client that leaves is destroyed,
// so a mark would be a second copy of the same fact, and the copy is what drifts.
//
// The server is told NOTHING about any client. It never `@Link`s one: a signed frame carries the port
// its sender listens on, so hearing from someone teaches the server how to answer them. Every name and
// every route below was learned at runtime.

bundle ChatServer by you {

    // One row of the roster. `addr` is the client's network identity — what @Send needs to reach them.
    shape $Client { addr: string }

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Listen { as: #Server, at: 9700, key: "chat-secret" }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "server listening on 9700 — waiting for clients" }
        }
    }

    shard Relay {
        var known: bool

        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Message as m {
            // 1. Do we already have this sender? `target` is the only way to look through the roster,
            //    so the answer comes back on shard state rather than as a return value.
            known = false
            target $Client as self {
                if self.Client.addr == m.from { known = true }
            }

            // 2. A newcomer becomes an entity. That is the whole "stacking" mechanism.
            if not known {
                let e = spawn()
                attach $Client to e { addr: m.from }
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "* " + m.from + " joined" }
            }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.from + ": " + m.text }

            // 3. Relay to everyone except the sender. No `!=` in VeinScript (LANGUAGE.md §7) —
            //    inequality is `not (a == b)`. Without the guard the sender gets their own line back.
            target $Client as self {
                if not (self.Client.addr == m.from) {
                    emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Send { to: self.Client.addr, text: m.from + ": " + m.text }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // A client that closed its window. `destroy` removes the identity outright — the entity AND the
    // $Client component on it.
    //
    // Unmarking would have looked equivalent and is not. The entity would linger with its component
    // forever, so every disconnect leaks one; and because the roster is "which entities exist", a
    // returning client would not be recognised as already known and would be given a SECOND entity for
    // the same address. Leaving is a lifecycle event, so it gets the lifecycle operation.
    //
    // Destroying mid-iteration is safe: a query materialises its ids before the body runs, and the
    // destroy itself is queued for the commit point — so nothing here observes a half-changed roster.
    shard Reachability {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Undelivered as u {
            target $Client as self {
                if self.Client.addr == u.to {
                    destroy self
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "* " + u.to + " left" }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

### `samples/chat/client.vein`

```vein
// samples/chat/client.vein — ONE chat client, for everybody. Start samples/chat/server.vein first.
//
//   veinc run samples/chat/client.vein
//
//   > your name
//   alice                      ← whatever you like; this becomes your network identity
//   hi everyone                ← every line after that is a message
//
// There is nothing to configure: the server address is baked in (ServerAddress below), so the only
// thing anyone types is who they are. To point this at a different server — a local one for testing —
// change that one function.
//
// Run it in as many terminals, on as many machines, as you like. There used to be an alice.vein and a
// bob.vein because a program had no way to tell which one it was at startup — `here()` answers "which
// console am I" (#Main for both) and the language cannot read an environment variable. The missing
// option was simpler than either: ASK.
//
// This file contains no address. The two things that differ between one client and the next — who you
// are and where the server is — are both typed in, so the same file runs everywhere unchanged.
//
// Note the ASYMMETRY with the server, which names no address at all. `@Listen` binds a port and accepts
// on every interface: a listener does not need an address, it IS one. `@Link` dials, and dialling needs
// a destination. And only the side that speaks FIRST needs one, because a signed frame carries the port
// its sender listens on — so the server learns each client's route and never links anybody.

bundle ChatClient by you {

    // The one place the address lives. A `fn` because the language has no constant — and one place
    // beats two, since the address appears both where we connect and where we report failing to.
    fn ServerAddress() return string { return "999.999.999.999:9700" }

    shard Session {
        var joined: bool

        run once {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "your name:" }
        }

        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Input as i {
            // Read the state ONCE at entry. The branch below flips it, and without this snapshot the
            // very first line would be taken as a name AND immediately resent as a message.
            let first = not joined

            if first {
                joined = true

                // The name you typed IS the network identity — a mark is just a name, so a name typed at
                // the console works exactly as a `#Literal` written in the source would.
                //
                // `at: 0` lets the OS pick a free port. Nothing dials us by name: the server replies down
                // the connection we opened, so two clients on one machine cannot collide, nobody keeps a
                // port list, and a client behind a home router needs no port forwarding.
                emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Listen { as: i.text, at: 0, key: "chat-secret" }
                emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Link { name: #Server, at: ServerAddress(), key: "chat-secret" }

                // Saying hello is what puts us on the roster and teaches the server our route.
                emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Send { to: #Server, text: "is here" }
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "joined as " + i.text + " — type away" }
            }

            if not first {
                emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Send { to: #Server, text: i.text }
            }
        }

        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Undelivered as u {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  (cannot reach " + ServerAddress() + " — is the server running?)" }
        }
    }

    // `audience #Server` admits the server and refuses everyone else. Frames are signed, so that is a
    // guarantee rather than a convention another peer could simply ignore.
    shard Incoming {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Message as m audience #Server {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.text }
        }
    }

    // NOTE: this handler lives inside Session on purpose. `var` state belongs to ONE shard, so a
    // `hear` in a separate shard reading `server` would get that shard's own empty copy — which is
    // exactly what happened here: the warning printed "no server at " with nothing after it.
}
```

### `samples/chat/alice.vein`

```vein
// samples/chat/alice.vein — a chat CLIENT (Alice). Start samples/chat/server.vein first.
//
//   veinc run samples/chat/alice.vein
//
// Type a line and press Enter; it goes to the server, which relays it to the other client. Lines from
// the other client arrive here the same way.
//
// alice.vein and bob.vein are the same program with two lines different — the identity (`as`) and the
// port. They are separate FILES because each side needs its own `@Listen` port: two processes cannot
// bind the same one, and a port is not something a role switch could pick for them.
//
// A role switch itself IS available, and samples/control_center.vein is built on it — the runtime reads
// VEIN_CONSOLE, so `here()` differs between two launches of ONE file and `match here()` picks the role.
// That works for the console transport, whose addresses are names rather than ports. It does not solve
// this file's problem, which is the port.

bundle ChatAlice by you {

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Listen { as: #Alice, at: 9701, key: "chat-secret" }

            // Where the server lives. Registering a route connects nothing — if the server is down the
            // hello below simply comes back as @Undelivered.
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Link { name: #Server, at: "127.0.0.1:9700", key: "chat-secret" }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "Alice connected — type a line and press Enter" }

            // Saying hello is what teaches the SERVER how to reach us: it reads the return port off this
            // signed frame. Without it the server could not relay anything to Alice until Alice spoke.
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Send { to: #Server, text: "joined" }
        }
    }

    // Each line typed at this console becomes an @Input event, which we forward to the server.
    shard Typing {
        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Input as i {
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Send { to: #Server, text: i.text }
        }
    }

    // `audience #Server` admits only the server. The frame is signed, so this excludes anyone else on
    // the network rather than merely asking them politely not to talk to us.
    shard Incoming {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Message as m audience #Server {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.text }
        }
    }

    shard Reachability {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Undelivered as u {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  (server is not running — start samples/chat/server.vein)" }
        }
    }
}
```

### `samples/chat/bob.vein`

```vein
// samples/chat/bob.vein — a chat CLIENT (Bob). Start samples/chat/server.vein first.
//
//   veinc run samples/chat/bob.vein
//
// Type a line and press Enter; it goes to the server, which relays it to the other client. Lines from
// the other client arrive here the same way.
//
// alice.vein and bob.vein are the same program with two lines different — the identity (`as`) and the
// port. They are separate FILES rather than one file choosing a role, because a role switch needs a way
// to tell the two apart at startup: `here()` answers "which console am I", which is #Main for both, and
// the language cannot read an environment variable. Two files is the honest version.

bundle ChatBob by you {

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Listen { as: #Bob, at: 9702, key: "chat-secret" }

            // Where the server lives. Registering a route connects nothing — if the server is down the
            // hello below simply comes back as @Undelivered.
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Link { name: #Server, at: "127.0.0.1:9700", key: "chat-secret" }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "Bob connected — type a line and press Enter" }

            // Saying hello is what teaches the SERVER how to reach us: it reads the return port off this
            // signed frame. Without it the server could not relay anything to Bob until Bob spoke.
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Send { to: #Server, text: "joined" }
        }
    }

    // Each line typed at this console becomes an @Input event, which we forward to the server.
    shard Typing {
        hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Input as i {
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Send { to: #Server, text: i.text }
        }
    }

    // `audience #Server` admits only the server. The frame is signed, so this excludes anyone else on
    // the network rather than merely asking them politely not to talk to us.
    shard Incoming {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Message as m audience #Server {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.text }
        }
    }

    shard Reachability {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Undelivered as u {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  (server is not running — start samples/chat/server.vein)" }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/net_fetch.vein`

**HTTP fetch: request and reply** — Asks httpbin.org for your IP once at startup, then fetches a fresh UUID every 5 seconds and prints each reply's status, URL and body. A request that gets no answer at all is reported separately as @Failed.  
Run: `veinc run samples/net_fetch.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: Vein.Net.Http @Fetch / @Fetched / @Failed · request and reply as events · HTTP status vs transport failure · non-blocking I/O on the event loop · every N timer · run once  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 25  
Notes: Compiles cleanly (`veinc ir` exit 0, no diagnostics). Not run because it needs the network (httpbin.org) and keeps polling until stdin ends.

```vein
// samples/net_fetch.vein — calling a service that has never heard of VeinScript.
//
//   veinc run samples/net_fetch.vein
//
// Vein.Net.Peer addresses an IDENTITY: symmetric, both ends are Vein programs, either may speak first.
// HTTP is the opposite shape and the bundle does not pretend otherwise — a REST endpoint has no mark,
// cannot call you back, and answers exactly once per question. So this is a REQUEST with a reply:
// @Fetch goes out, @Fetched or @Failed comes back.
//
// The distinction worth internalising: a 404 or a 500 is @Fetched, not @Failed. The service ANSWERED,
// and what it answered is the program's business. @Failed means no answer arrived at all — bad host,
// refused connection, timeout. That is the same line @Undelivered draws for a peer.
//
// The request does not block the event loop: it runs on a worker and posts its result back, so the
// console stays responsive and the `every 5` timer below keeps ticking while a slow host hangs.

bundle FetchDemo by you {

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "asking httpbin for an IP…" }
            emit *Vein.Net.Http.@Fetch  { url: "https://httpbin.org/ip", method: "GET", body: "" }
        }
    }

    // Poll something on a timer — the ordinary reason a program fetches at all.
    shard Poller {
        var n: int

        every 5 {
            n += 1
            emit *Vein.Net.Http.@Fetch { url: "https://httpbin.org/uuid", method: "GET", body: "" }
        }
    }

    shard Results {
        hear *Vein.Net.Http.@Fetched as r {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: r.status + " " + r.url }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: r.body }
        }

        // Reached only when nothing came back. Worth handling separately: a program that treats an
        // unreachable host as an empty body will happily parse silence as data.
        hear *Vein.Net.Http.@Failed as f {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "unreachable " + f.url + " — " + f.reason }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/net_peer.vein`

**Network hub with an audience** — The hub half of a two-program demo. It listens on port 9700 as #Hub with a shared-secret key, prints and acknowledges each ping from #Spoke, notes every message from anyone, and reports peers it cannot reach.  
Run: `veinc run samples/net_peer.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: @Listen with identity, port and key · audience barrier on hear · replying to a peer that was never linked · signed and encrypted frames · @Undelivered for peers · the same messaging vocabulary as consoles  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 26  
Notes: Compiles cleanly (`veinc ir` exit 0, no diagnostics). Not run because it listens on a TCP port and needs net_spoke.vein running in a second terminal. It is half of a pair; present it together with net_spoke.vein.

```vein
// samples/net_peer.vein — the HUB half of a two-program network demo. Run it with samples/net_spoke.vein.
//
//   Terminal 1:   veinc run samples/net_peer.vein      ← this file; listens as #Hub on port 9700
//   Terminal 2:   veinc run samples/net_spoke.vein     ← the other file; pings the hub every 2s
//
// Same machine as written (127.0.0.1). For two real machines, change only the `at:` address in
// net_spoke.vein to the hub's IP — nothing in either program changes.
//
// The point of this sample is what ISN'T here. Compare samples/console_roles.vein: the `@Send` and the
// `hear @Message` below are byte for byte the console versions. Nothing about them says "network". A
// mark is an identity; `@Link` tells the runtime where that identity lives; and the send picks the wire
// over the pipe on its own. Delete `@Listen` and this is a local console program again, unchanged.
//
// Notice what the hub does NOT do: it never links the spoke. A verified frame carries the port its
// sender listens on, so receiving one teaches the hub the way back. Only the side that speaks first
// needs configuring.
//
// The key is a SHARED SECRET and it is mandatory — @Listen refuses an empty one. Every frame is signed
// with it, which is what makes `from` a proven identity rather than a claim, and therefore what makes
// the `audience #Spoke` barrier below an actual barrier. A port with no key would take anyone's word.
//
// Frames are encrypted (AES-256-GCM under a key derived from that secret), so the wire carries nothing
// readable but a length. Not TLS though: one static key, so no forward secrecy and no cert identity.

bundle PeerHub by you {

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            // `as` is who this program IS on the network. Without it every peer in the world would
            // answer to #Main — harmless for one machine's pipes, useless across a network.
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Listen { as: #Hub, at: 9700, key: "shared-secret" }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "hub listening on 9700 as #Hub — waiting for #Spoke" }
        }
    }

    // `audience #Spoke` is the whole reason the key exists. It admits the spoke and refuses everyone
    // else — and because the frame was signed, "refuses" is a guarantee, not a convention politely
    // honoured by well-behaved peers.
    shard Trusted {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Message as m audience #Spoke {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "trusted " + m.from + ": " + m.text }
            // Replying to a peer that was never linked — the route was learned when it spoke.
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Send { to: m.from, text: "ack " + m.text }
        }
    }

    // Anything from someone OTHER than the spoke still lands here: the barrier filters one handler, it
    // does not discard the message. That is what you want — unexpected is not the same as unwelcome,
    // and a program that cannot see strangers cannot report them either.
    shard Inbox {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Message as m {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  (inbox saw " + m.from + ")" }
        }
    }

    // A peer that is off, unreachable, or was never linked. Exactly the console model's @Undelivered:
    // "no answer" has to be an event, or a dead machine is indistinguishable from a quiet one.
    shard Reachability {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Undelivered as u {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  (" + u.to + " unreachable)" }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/net_spoke.vein`

**Network spoke: link and ping** — The spoke half. It listens on 9701 as #Spoke, links #Hub at 127.0.0.1:9700, pings the hub every 2 seconds, prints the hub's replies and reports each ping that cannot be delivered.  
Run: `veinc run samples/net_spoke.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: @Link registers a route without connecting · @Listen identity for replies · every N timer with a shard var · audience on replies · @Undelivered turns silence into an event · only the address line knows about the network  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good · used in lesson 26  
Notes: Compiles cleanly (`veinc ir` exit 0, no diagnostics). Not run because it needs net_peer.vein in a second terminal and the TCP ports. It is net_peer.vein's companion; show the two split-screen.

```vein
// samples/net_spoke.vein — the SPOKE half. Start samples/net_peer.vein (the hub) first.
//
//   Terminal 1:   veinc run samples/net_peer.vein      ← the hub; listens as #Hub on 9700
//   Terminal 2:   veinc run samples/net_spoke.vein     ← this file; pings it every 2s
//
// For two real machines, change only the `at:` address below to the hub's IP — "10.0.0.5:9700". That
// address is the ONLY line in either program that knows a network exists.
//
// Try these, in this order, to see what the design actually buys:
//   1. Run the spoke with the hub DOWN  → every send comes back as @Undelivered, so silence is
//      reported rather than merely happening.
//   2. Start the hub                    → pings are answered, and the hub replies without ever having
//      been told where the spoke lives (it learned the route from the first signed frame).
//   3. Change `key` here and rerun      → the hub drops every frame. The sends still "succeed" locally:
//      authentication happens at the RECEIVER, so a bad key looks exactly like a peer that went quiet.

bundle PeerSpoke by you {

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            // This program's own network identity, and the port it accepts replies on.
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Listen { as: #Spoke, at: 9701, key: "shared-secret" }

            // Where the hub LIVES. Registering a route connects nothing — linking a machine that is not
            // up yet is ordinary, and the first send simply comes back as @Undelivered.
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Link { name: #Hub, at: "127.0.0.1:9700", key: "shared-secret" }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "spoke up as #Spoke — pinging #Hub every 2s" }
        }
    }

    shard Pinger {
        var round: int

        every 2 {
            round += 1
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "→ Hub: ping " + round }
            // Identical to a console @Send. The routing table, not this line, decides it goes over TCP.
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Send { to: #Hub, text: "ping " + round }
        }
    }

    shard Replies {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Message as m audience #Hub {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.from + " replied: " + m.text }
        }
    }

    shard Reachability {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Undelivered as u {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  (" + u.to + " is not up — start samples/net_peer.vein)" }
        }
    }
}
```

---

# Apps, bundles and the stdlib

## `samples/app`

**Two authors, one bundle name** — An app manifest loads two bundles that are both named Combat, one by yolan and one by alice, and both declare @Request. The author is part of every qualified name (*yolan.Combat.Api.@Request), and `veinc symbols` lists and flags the collision. The folder also holds RPGConsole.vein, a separate interactive console menu that app.vein does not load.  
Files: `samples/app/app.vein`, `samples/app/yolan_combat.vein`, `samples/app/alice_combat.vein`, `samples/app/RPGConsole.vein`  
Run: `veinc symbols samples/app/app.vein`  
Teaches: app manifest (app/load) · author-qualified names (bundle X by author) · publicator + shared() visibility · bundle-private declarations · cross-bundle emit by * path · load ... start { } payload override · veinc symbols collision report  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good  
Notes: `veinc run samples/app/app.vein --ticks 1` printed only: "app MyGame: principal 'Combat' boots; 2 bundle(s) linked into one runtime [Combat, Combat]". The banner does not show authors, so both bundles appear as 'Combat'. RPGConsole.vein was not run because it is interactive (hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Input). It is a good optional demo of `target … by MenuItem.order` and menu-as-entities. The symbols output was truncated for length.

Real output of `veinc symbols samples/app/app.vein`:
```text
app MyGame  (197 symbols, 8 name collision(s))
  bundle Combat by alice
    *alice.Combat.Combat   (Bundle)   [COLLISION — qualify with author]
    *alice.Combat.Api.@Loot   (Event)
    *alice.Combat.Api.@Request   (Event)   [COLLISION — qualify with author]
  bundle Audio by Vein
  ... [truncated: the 16 Vein stdlib bundles, ~207 lines, including
       *Vein.Web.Http.@Request   (Event)   [COLLISION — qualify with author]] ...
  bundle Combat by yolan
    start @Request { path: string }   (boot — fill via `load … start { … }`)
```
