```text
    *yolan.Combat.Combat   (Bundle)   [COLLISION — qualify with author]
    *yolan.Combat.Api.@Request   (Event)   [COLLISION — qualify with author]
```

### `samples/app/app.vein`

```vein
// app.vein — a project MANIFEST: the set of bundles (across files, from different authors) that
// compose one program. There is no entry point — IOP is reactive, so every loaded bundle participates.
// Discover everything, collision-safe, with:
//   veinc symbols samples/app/app.vein            (human listing; flags name collisions)
//   veinc symbols samples/app/app.vein --json     (same data for tooling)
//
// Both loaded files declare a bundle NAMED Combat but by DIFFERENT authors (yolan, alice), and both
// declare @Request — a collision the author root resolves: `*yolan.Combat.@Request` is unique.

app MyGame {
    // Override yolan's Combat start payload — boot it with our own value (fills only what we change;
    // `veinc symbols` shows the bundle's start signature so you know what fields exist).
    load "yolan_combat.vein" start { path: "/mygame" }
    load "alice_combat.vein" 
}
```

### `samples/app/yolan_combat.vein`

```vein
// yolan's Combat bundle. `by yolan` sets the author — the root of every qualified name here, e.g.
// *yolan.Combat.@Request. It also references alice's colliding event via a `*` path to stay unambiguous.

bundle Combat by yolan {
    start @Request { path: "/" }        // this bundle's own boot event + default payload

    // `shared` lives inside a publicator (annotation-style, above the decl) — that's how a dev chooses
    // what is public ACROSS bundles. A publicator member without `shared` is only bundle-wide.
    publicator Api {
        shared("an inbound request")
        event @Request { path: string }
    }

    event @Damage { amount: int }       // bundle-level -> private to this bundle

    shape $Health { hp: int folds sum }

    shard Kernel {
        hear @Request as req {
            emit @Damage { amount: 5 }
            // Cross-bundle emit: `*alice.Combat.Api.@Loot` names WHO owns @Loot (and which publicator),
            // so its payload types are unambiguous. `veinc symbols` validates it resolves to a shared event.
            emit *alice.Combat.Api.@Loot { gold: 5 }
        }
    }
}
```

### `samples/app/alice_combat.vein`

```vein
// alice's Combat bundle — same bundle NAME and same @Request event name as yolan's, by a different
// author. Qualified as *alice.Combat.@Request. This is the collision the discovery tool flags.

bundle Combat by alice {
    publicator Api {
        shared("an inbound request")
        event @Request { path: string }              // collides by NAME with yolan's; *alice / *yolan disambiguates

        shared("loot dropped by a defeated enemy")
        event @Loot { gold: int }                    // yolan emits *alice.Combat.Api.@Loot
    }

    shard Kernel {
        hear @Request as req { emit @Loot { gold: 10 } }
    }
}
```

### `samples/app/RPGConsole.vein`

```vein
bundle RpgMenu by you {

  // A menu item as an identity: `key` is what you type, `order` is the line it prints on. Both are
  // data on the entity rather than a position in a list, so the menu sorts itself.
  shape $MenuItem { key: string, label: string, order: int }
  mark #MenuItem
  builder MenuItem { $MenuItem   mark #MenuItem }

  shape $Player { name: string, hp: int, gold: int }
  mark #Player
  builder Player { $Player   mark #Player }

  event @ShowMenu { }

  start @ShowMenu { }

  shard Seed {
    run once {
      bring MenuItem("1", "New Game", 1)
      bring MenuItem("2", "Continue", 2)
      bring MenuItem("3", "Inventory", 3)
      bring MenuItem("4", "Quit", 4)

      bring Player("Hero", 100, 50)
    }
  }

  shard Menu {
    hear @ShowMenu as s {
      emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "" }
      emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "===== RPG MAIN MENU =====" }

      // `by MenuItem.order` takes the SHAPE's name, not the binding's — the query is ordered by a
      // field of the shape it selects.
      target $MenuItem #MenuItem by MenuItem.order as m {
        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: m.MenuItem.key + ". " + m.MenuItem.label }
      }

      emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "=========================" }
      emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "Choose (1-4):" }
    }
  }

  shard Choice {
    hear *Vein.Console.Io.@Input as i {
      // COMPARED AS A STRING, deliberately. There is no string-to-number built-in, so the typed line
      // stays text — and `trim` matters because the line arrives with whatever whitespace you gave it,
      // where `" 3"` would never equal `"3"`.
      let choice = trim(i.text)

      if choice == "1" {
        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "Starting a new adventure..." }
        emit @ShowMenu { }
      } else if choice == "2" {
        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "Loading your saved game..." }
        emit @ShowMenu { }
      } else if choice == "3" {
        target $Player #Player as p {
          emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
            text: p.Player.name + " | HP: " + p.Player.hp + " | Gold: " + p.Player.gold
          }
        }
        emit @ShowMenu { }
      } else if choice == "4" {
        // The one branch that does NOT show the menu again — which is what ending looks like in a
        // program with no loop to break out of.
        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "Farewell, adventurer!" }
      } else {
        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "Invalid choice. Try again." }
        emit @ShowMenu { }
      }
    }
  }
}
```

## `samples/app_capabilities`

**Apps: principal plus capabilities** — Three bundles are linked into one runtime. Store is the principal and its `start` is the app's only boot event. Billing (invoices shipments) and Audit (watches both) boot nothing and only react to events heard across bundles. Removing a `load` line removes that capability.  
Files: `samples/app_capabilities/shop.app.vein`, `samples/app_capabilities/Store.vein`, `samples/app_capabilities/Billing.vein`, `samples/app_capabilities/Audit.vein`  
Run: `veinc run samples/app_capabilities/shop.app.vein`  
Teaches: app manifest · principal bundle vs capability bundle · one shared event queue across bundles · cross-bundle hear by qualified path · publicator + shared events · emit queues (event ordering)  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 8  
Notes: The header command has no --ticks. It exited on its own (exit 0) and printed the output shown.

Real output of `veinc run samples/app_capabilities/shop.app.vein`:
```text
app Shop: principal 'Store' boots; 3 bundle(s) linked into one runtime [Store, Billing, Audit]
[Store] order: 3 x widget
[Audit] saw order of widget
[Billing] invoicing widget
[Audit] saw invoice 42 for widget
```

### `samples/app_capabilities/shop.app.vein`

```vein
// samples/app_capabilities/shop.app.vein — three bundles, one runtime.
//
//   veinc run samples/app_capabilities/shop.app.vein
//
// The first `load` is the PRINCIPAL: its `start` is the app's one boot event. Every other bundle
// contributes its shards to the same runtime and boots nothing — so an `emit` in one bundle reaches a
// `hear` in another, and capabilities compose by being listed here.
//
// Comment out a `load` and that capability disappears with no other edit. That is the whole model.

app Shop {
    load "Store.vein"        // ★ principal — the only bundle that starts anything
    load "Billing.vein"      // capability: invoices what Store ships
    load "Audit.vein"        // capability: watches both of the above
}
```

### `samples/app_capabilities/Store.vein`

```vein
// The PRINCIPAL bundle — the one the app lists first. Its `start` is the app's single boot event, and
// everything else in the app reacts to what it emits. This is the only bundle that begins anything.

bundle Store by you {

    publicator Orders {
        shared("A customer placed an order.")
        event @Ordered { item: string, qty: int }

        shared("An order was accepted and is going out.")
        event @Shipped { item: string }
    }

    // The app's front door. `veinc run app.vein` fires exactly this, once.
    start @Ordered { item: "widget", qty: 3 }

    shard Fulfilment {
        hear @Ordered as o {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "[Store] order: " + o.qty + " x " + o.item }
            emit @Shipped { item: o.item }
        }
    }
}
```

### `samples/app_capabilities/Billing.vein`

```vein
// A CAPABILITY bundle. It declares no `start` and begins nothing — it only reacts. Adding it to the app
// adds invoicing; removing the `load` line removes invoicing, and no other bundle changes either way.

bundle Billing by you {

    publicator Invoices {
        shared("An invoice was raised for a shipment.")
        event @Invoiced { item: string, amount: int }
    }

    shard Invoicer {
        hear *you.Store.Orders.@Shipped as s {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "[Billing] invoicing " + s.item }
            emit @Invoiced { item: s.item, amount: 42 }
        }
    }
}
```

### `samples/app_capabilities/Audit.vein`

```vein
// A second capability, listening to BOTH of the others. It knows nothing about how they are wired: it
// names the events it cares about, and linking puts them all on one queue.

bundle Audit by you {
    shard Ledger {
        hear *you.Store.Orders.@Ordered as o {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "[Audit] saw order of " + o.item }
        }
        hear *you.Billing.Invoices.@Invoiced as i {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "[Audit] saw invoice " + i.amount + " for " + i.item }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/app_net`

**The network as a capability** — The principal Gate boots with a port number and never mentions networking. The capability bundle Wire hears Gate's @Opened, binds a real socket as #Hub with Vein.Net.Peer.@Listen, and acknowledges incoming messages. Because there is one _self per runtime, the whole app answers as #Hub.  
Files: `samples/app_net/hub.app.vein`, `samples/app_net/Gate.vein`, `samples/app_net/Wire.vein`  
Run: `veinc run samples/app_net/hub.app.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: capability bundle · one queue / one _self per app runtime · Vein.Net.Peer @Listen / @Message / @Send / @Undelivered · failure as a message · marks as network identities  
Difficulty 4/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Not run: it needs a network socket and a second process. As a non-network check, `veinc symbols samples/app_net/hub.app.vein` loads cleanly (195 symbols; bundles Gate and Wire by you; Gate's start @Opened { at: int } is listed).

### `samples/app_net/hub.app.vein`

```vein
// samples/app_net/hub.app.vein — the network as a CAPABILITY.
//
//   Terminal 1:   veinc run samples/app_net/hub.app.vein     ← this app; #Hub on 9700
//   Terminal 2:   veinc run samples/net_spoke.vein           ← the unchanged spoke from the pair demo
//
// samples/net_peer.vein is the same hub as ONE bundle. Here it is split: the principal knows nothing
// about networks, and a capability bundle does the listening. Comment out the `Wire.vein` load and the
// program is still a valid app — it just has no network, with no edit to Gate.vein.
//
// What this proves that app_capabilities does not: a capability's `@Listen` binds a real socket and its
// `hear @Message` receives, inside a runtime it did not boot. App link+run gives every loaded bundle ONE
// queue and ONE `_self`, so the network identity a non-principal declares is the identity the whole app
// answers to — the spoke's `audience #Hub` barrier admits the reply, which it could only do if the frame
// were signed as #Hub.

app HubApp {
    load "Gate.vein"     // ★ principal — boots, and never mentions the network
    load "Wire.vein"     // capability: listens, receives, replies
}
```

### `samples/app_net/Gate.vein`

```vein
// The PRINCIPAL. It owns the boot event and the port number as a decision — and nothing else. There is
// no `@Listen` here and no mention of Vein.Net: whether this app is reachable is a capability someone
// else supplies, which is the point of the split.

bundle Gate by you {

    publicator Doors {
        shared("The gate is open; here is the port anyone listening should use.")
        event @Opened { at: int }
    }

    // The app's single boot event — only the principal has one.
    start @Opened { at: 9700 }

    shard Announce {
        hear @Opened as o {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "[Gate] open on " + o.at + " — a listener may now bind" }
        }
    }
}
```

### `samples/app_net/Wire.vein`

```vein
// A CAPABILITY bundle. It declares no `start` and begins nothing; it reacts to the principal's boot
// event and binds the socket. Adding this `load` makes the app reachable, removing it makes it local,
// and Gate.vein is identical either way.

bundle Wire by you {

    shard Listener {
        // `as` is who the WHOLE APP is on the network — there is one `_self` per runtime, not one per
        // bundle, so a capability naming it names it for everyone.
        hear *you.Gate.Doors.@Opened as o {
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Listen { as: #Hub, at: o.at, key: "shared-secret" }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "[Wire] listening as #Hub on " + o.at }
        }
    }

    // Byte for byte the handler in samples/net_peer.vein. Nothing here knows it lives in a capability
    // bundle rather than a principal — one queue means a `hear` is a `hear`.
    shard Inbox {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Message as m {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "[Wire] " + m.from + ": " + m.text }
            emit *Vein.Net.Peer.@Send { to: m.from, text: "ack " + m.text }
        }
    }

    shard Reachability {
        hear *Vein.Net.Peer.@Undelivered as u {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  (" + u.to + " unreachable)" }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/bundle_collisions`

**Two kits, one word: @Dispatch** — Two installed kits, acme.Radio and zenith.Courier, each export an event called @Dispatch with completely different payloads. One bundle needs both, emits each by its qualified name, and hears each with its own handler. Events are identified by Author.Bundle.Name, so both are deliverable.  
Files: `samples/bundle_collisions/bundle_collisions.app.vein`, `samples/bundle_collisions/collisions.vein`, `samples/bundle_collisions/vein.discovery`, `samples/bundle_collisions/bundles/Radio.vein`, `samples/bundle_collisions/bundles/Courier.vein`  
Run: `veinc run samples/bundle_collisions/bundle_collisions.app.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: event identity = Author.Bundle.Name · need "Author.Bundle" (resolve + link) · VS0216 ambiguity and qualifying · need … as alias (qualified import) · shapes unify by bare name, events do not · installed kits under bundles/  
Difficulty 4/5 · video value: good  
Notes: The kits live in bundles/ and are found by name, not by path. Uncommenting line 43 in a scratch copy reproduces the header's VS0216 text word for word, but only where the bundle itself is compiled. `veinc check samples/bundle_collisions/collisions.vein` and `veinc run` of the app each print it three times with no line number (`:0:0`), while `veinc check` on bundle_collisions.app.vein prints 'ok'. It is only a warning: the program still runs, the ambiguous emit reaches neither handler, and the output is unchanged. Replacing the second need with `need "zenith.Courier" as C` (and writing *C.Post.@Dispatch) clears the warning, and the bare emit then goes to Radio: `[radio]   : which one am I?`.

Real output of `veinc run samples/bundle_collisions/bundle_collisions.app.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
app BundleCollisions: principal 'Collisions' boots; 3 bundle(s) linked into one runtime [Collisions, Radio, Courier]; 2 of them by `need` [Radio, Courier]
[radio]   news: broadcast to the channel
[courier] delivered to one person  urgent=true
```

### `samples/bundle_collisions/bundle_collisions.app.vein`

```vein
app BundleCollisions {
    load "collisions.vein"
}
```

### `samples/bundle_collisions/collisions.vein`

```vein
// samples/bundle_collisions/collisions.vein — two kits export `@Dispatch`, and this bundle needs both.
//
//   veinc run samples/bundle_collisions/bundle_collisions.app.vein --ticks 1
//
// A COLLISION IS NOT A MISTAKE. `acme.Radio` broadcasts to a channel, `zenith.Courier` delivers to
// one recipient, and `Dispatch` is the right word for both. Neither author could have avoided it,
// and neither has to give it up: they are two events that share a word, not one event with an
// argument about its fields.
//
// THAT IS RECENT, AND IT IS THE POINT OF THIS SAMPLE. Events used to be identified by their bare
// name, so these two were ONE event in one queue. Matching payloads merged silently; differing ones
// — which is what these two have — were VS0332 and the app would not link at all. "One of you must
// rename" is a workable rule inside one team and no rule at all across a package ecosystem. An event
// is now `Author.Bundle.Name`, so both of these are deliverable and neither is confused for the
// other. Look at the two payloads in bundles/: three fields against two, nothing in common.
//
// SHAPES STILL UNIFY BY NAME, and deliberately. A capability bundle reads the principal's
// `$Position` precisely because components match on the bare name — that is the mechanism the
// language is built on, not an oversight — so two bundles disagreeing about a `$Shape` is still
// VS0332. Events were the half where unification bought nothing and cost a name.
//
// WHAT IS LEFT IS THE NAMING PROBLEM, and it is smaller than it looks: with both bundles in scope a
// BARE `@Dispatch` cannot say which of the two it means. That is VS0216, a warning, and it is about
// this one reference rather than about the bundles.
//
// TO SEE IT: uncomment the `emit @Dispatch` in `Loud` and build —
//
//     VS0216: '@Dispatch' is ambiguous across the bundles in scope — *zenith.Courier.Post.Dispatch
//     and *acme.Radio.Wire.Dispatch. Qualify the reference to choose one.

bundle Collisions by you {

    // BOTH NEEDED PLAINLY, which is what puts both bare names in scope and makes the ambiguity
    // possible at all. See the note at the bottom about `as`, which removes it by removing a name.
    need "acme.Radio"
    need "zenith.Courier"

    shard Loud {
        run once {
            // ── UNCOMMENT FOR THE AMBIGUITY ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
            // Both bundles are in scope and both export `@Dispatch`. Which one is this?
            //
            // emit @Dispatch { text: "which one am I?" }
            // ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

            // SAY WHICH ONE, and each takes its own payload — `channel` exists on one of these and
            // `urgent` on the other, and neither bundle had to know the other existed.
            emit *acme.Radio.Wire.@Dispatch { channel: "news", text: "broadcast to the channel" }
            emit *zenith.Courier.Post.@Dispatch { to: #Main, text: "delivered to one person", urgent: true }
        }
    }

    // TWO HANDLERS, TWO EVENTS. Under the old rule one `hear` caught both emits, because there was
    // only one event after linking; now each hears its own and reads fields the other does not have.
    shard Listen {
        hear *acme.Radio.Wire.@Dispatch as d {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "[radio]   " + d.channel + ": " + d.text }
        }

        hear *zenith.Courier.Post.@Dispatch as d {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "[courier] " + d.text + "  urgent=" + d.urgent }
        }
    }
}

// ── THE OTHER FIX: ALIAS ONE OF THEM (RULES 18b) ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
//
// Replace the second `need` above with
//
//     need "zenith.Courier" as C
//
// and write `*C.Post.@Dispatch`. Two things change, and the second is the point:
//
//   * the reference gets shorter — `C` names the head of a `*` path, with author and bundle folded
//     into one segment, which is worth it for a bundle you reach for often;
//
//   * THE AMBIGUITY DISAPPEARS. An alias imports qualified and widens NOTHING, so bare `@Dispatch`
//     no longer means Courier's — only Radio's contributes a bare name, and the commented `emit`
//     above stops warning. That is why `as` answers VS0216 rather than restating it: if an alias
//     also widened, aliasing both would leave the bare name just as ambiguous and solve nothing.
```

### `samples/bundle_collisions/vein.discovery`

```text
# vein.discovery — what `$`, `#`, `@`, `&` and `*` ENUMERATE in this sample.
#
# `samples/vein.discovery` one directory up says `silent all`, because the samples folder holds
# sixty unrelated programs. This file overrides it for this project — the nearest one wins.
#
# BOTH COLLIDING KITS ARE EXPOSED, which is the whole point: type `@` in collisions.vein and the
# list shows `@Dispatch` twice, once per owner, each row naming the bundle it came from. That is
# the editor showing you the collision before the compiler has to.

silent all

expose Vein.Console        # `@Print`, which the Listen shard emits to
expose acme.Radio          # one `@Dispatch`
expose zenith.Courier      # the other `@Dispatch`
```

### `samples/bundle_collisions/bundles/Radio.vein`

```vein
// samples/bundle_collisions/bundles/Radio.vein — one of two kits that chose the same word.
//
// Nothing is wrong with this file. `@Dispatch` is the right name for what it does, and the author
// had no way of knowing somebody else would think so too — which is the point: a collision is not a
// mistake anybody made, it is what happens when two good libraries meet in one program.
//
// ITS PAYLOAD IS ITS OWN. Note what this does NOT have to do: match the other kit's fields. An event
// is identified by `Author.Bundle.Name`, so `acme.Radio.Dispatch` and `zenith.Courier.Dispatch` are
// two events that merely share a word. They used to be ONE — unified by bare name — and two authors
// who disagreed about the payload could not be in the same program at all.

bundle Radio by acme {

    publicator Wire {
        shared("Broadcast a line to a channel.")
        event @Dispatch { channel: string, text: string }

        shared("A channel an identity is tuned to.")
        shape $Channel { name: string }
    }
}
```

### `samples/bundle_collisions/bundles/Courier.vein`

```vein
// samples/bundle_collisions/bundles/Courier.vein — the other one.
//
// A different author, a different publicator, the same member name, and a DIFFERENT payload — three
// fields where Radio has two, and not one of them shared. That is the case that matters: two vendors
// who have never met, both reaching for the obvious word, disagreeing about what it carries.
//
// A SHAPE would still have to agree. Components unify by bare name on purpose, because that is how a
// capability bundle reads the principal's `$Position` without either knowing about the other — so
// `$Postbox` here and a `$Postbox` elsewhere are one component, and VS0332 still says so.

bundle Courier by zenith {

    publicator Post {
        shared("Deliver a line to one recipient, urgently or not.")
        event @Dispatch { to: Mark, text: string, urgent: bool }

        shared("A recipient's postbox.")
        shape $Postbox { unread: int }
    }
}
```

## `samples/kitdemo`

**A game built on an installed kit** — A game bundle `need`s an installed kit (kit.Movement) that lives in bundles/. That one line lets the game use the kit's shared $Mover and #Moving by bare name, and it links the kit's Drift shard, which moves every #Moving entity each tick. A vein.discovery file shows that what the editor offers is a separate question from what is reachable.  
Files: `samples/kitdemo/kitdemo.app.vein`, `samples/kitdemo/game.vein`, `samples/kitdemo/vein.discovery`, `samples/kitdemo/bundles/Movement.vein`  
Run: `veinc run samples/kitdemo/kitdemo.app.vein --ticks 3`  
Teaches: installed kits in bundles/ · need links transitively · shared public half vs private half · builder with multiple $Shape includes · positional args across includes · each tick vs settled · discovery policy vs shared  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 19  
Notes: Tested on a copy: `target *Vein.Transform.Spatial.$Position as m` compiled and matched 0 entities, while the bare `$Position` matched 1. Unlike motion.vein, the first frame moves here, because $Mover's rate is set once at spawn and already committed when tick 1 runs. motion.vein's velocity starts at 0, and each force's write only becomes readable the next frame. Folding is not the difference: a copy of motion.vein with an unfolded velocity shape shows the same one-frame lag.

Real output of `veinc run samples/kitdemo/kitdemo.app.vein --ticks 3`:
```text
app KitDemo: principal 'KitDemo' boots; 2 bundle(s) linked into one runtime [KitDemo, Movement]; 1 of them by `need` [Movement]
TEST
at 1,0.5 after 1
at 2,1 after 2
at 3,1.5 after 3
```

### `samples/kitdemo/kitdemo.app.vein`

```vein
// One line. The game is the root; the kit it needs is linked from there.
app KitDemo {
    load "game.vein"
}
```

### `samples/kitdemo/game.vein`

```vein
// samples/kitdemo/game.vein — a game built on an installed kit.
//
//   veinc run samples/kitdemo/kitdemo.app.vein --ticks 3
//
// WHAT IT DEMONSTRATES, and why it is a directory rather than one more file in `samples/`:
//
//   * `bundles/` — where an installed kit lives, found by name rather than by path.
//   * `need "kit.Movement"` — one line that both RESOLVES the kit's bare names and LINKS its shards,
//     so `Drift` below runs without the manifest naming it.
//   * a one-line manifest, because everything else arrives transitively.
//
// AND IT IS THE FIXTURE FOR EDITOR COMPLETION. Open this file in the Workbench and type `$`: the list
// shows this bundle's own shapes first, then `$Mover   kit.Movement.Drive`, then everything else
// discoverable. `$Budget` is NOT there — the kit keeps it outside its publicator, so no other bundle
// may name it. Type `#` for the same story in marks.

bundle KitDemo by you {

    need "Vein.Transform"
    need "kit.Movement"

    // This bundle's OWN shape. Unshared, and completion still offers it here — everything in your own
    // bundle is yours whatever its visibility. It is only the boundary that `shared` guards.
    shape $Score { points: int }

    mark #Player

    builder Player {
        $Position
        $Mover                                  // the kit's, by its BARE name — that is what `need` buys
        $Score
        mark #Player   mark #Moving
    }

    shard Boot { run once { 
        *Vein.Console.Io.print("TEST")
        bring Player(0.0, 0.0, 0.0,   1.0, 0.5,   0) 
    } }

    shard Watch {
        
        
        
        settled {
              
            target $Position $Score #Player as p {
                p.Score.points = p.Score.points + 1 
             
                
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: "at " + p.Position.x + "," + p.Position.y + " after " + p.Score.points
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

### `samples/kitdemo/vein.discovery`

```text
# vein.discovery — what the editor's `$`, `#`, `@`, `&` and `*` lists ENUMERATE here.
#
# THE THIRD PIECE OF THE STORY, and the one that surprises people. `shared("…")` decides what MAY be
# consumed across a bundle boundary; this decides what is OFFERED when you type a sigil. They are
# different questions: a silenced bundle is still fully usable by an explicit
# `*kit.Movement.Drive.$Mover` — it simply does not clutter a list you are scrolling.
#
# `samples/vein.discovery` one directory up says `silent all`, because the samples folder holds sixty
# unrelated programs and a completion list spanning all of them would be noise. This file overrides it
# for this project — the nearest policy wins — which is what a real game directory would do.
#
# Delete the `expose kit.Movement` line and type `$` in game.vein: `$Mover` disappears from the list
# while the program still compiles and runs. That is the distinction, in one edit.

# ---- the root default --------------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# Start from nothing. The alternative, `expose all`, is the default when this file does not exist at
# all — fine for a two-bundle project and unusable once the standard library and a few kits are on the
# search path, which is the moment anybody writes one of these.

silent all


# ---- what this project works with ----------------------------------------------------------------
#
# A path is `Author`, `Author.Bundle`, or `Author.Bundle.Publicator`, and these four are all the middle
# form. `expose Vein` would take the whole standard library in one line; naming the bundles is the
# difference between a list you read and a list you scroll.

expose Vein.Console          # `@Print`, which the Watch shard emits to
expose Vein.Transform        # `$Position`, which the game and the kit both target
expose kit.Movement          # the installed kit's `$Mover` / `#Moving`

# Exposed although nothing here uses it yet, and deliberately: a game reaches for `sqrt` and `clamp`
# about ten minutes in, and discovery is how you find out those exist. Enumerating a bundle you have
# not needed is free — `need` is still what makes a bare name resolve, and `$`/`#` will say the row
# came from a bundle you have not needed yet.
expose Vein.Math


# ---- the rule that catches people: the MOST SPECIFIC path wins ------------------------------------
#
# `Vein.Transform` is exposed above, and this silences one publicator inside it. `$Position` and
# `$Velocity` (publicator `Spatial`) stay in the `$` list; `@Moved` (publicator `Motion`) leaves the
# `@` list, because a rule on `Author.Bundle.Publicator` is consulted before the one on
# `Author.Bundle`, which is consulted before `Author`, which is consulted before `silent all`.
#
# It reads backwards — an `expose` line and a `silent` line about the same bundle — and it is the
# normal shape of a real policy: take the bundle, drop the corner of it you never touch. The order of
# the lines does not matter, only how many segments each one names.
#
# This one is honest rather than illustrative: kitdemo never emits or hears `@Moved`. Comment the line
# out and type `@` in game.vein to watch it come back.

silent Vein.Transform.Motion


# ---- what this file has no opinion about ----------------------------------------------------------
#
# `$Budget`, in bundles/Movement.vein. It is missing from every list here and nothing above is why:
# the kit declares it outside its publicator, so it is not `shared`, so it does not exist beyond its
# own bundle at all. No `expose` line can reveal it, and that is the difference between the two
# mechanisms — this file filters what you are SHOWN of the reachable, and `shared` decides what is
# reachable. A missing name is a `shared` question first and a discovery question second.
```

### `samples/kitdemo/bundles/Movement.vein`

```vein
// samples/kitdemo/bundles/Movement.vein — an INSTALLED KIT, as a dev would receive one.
//
// It lives under `bundles/`, which is where `BundleIndex.SearchRoots` looks after the standard
// library. Nothing in the game below reaches into this file; it is found by NAME.
//
// WHAT THIS FILE IS FOR, beyond running. It is the fixture the Workbench's `$`/`#` completion needs
// to have anything to find: a second bundle, with a publicator, some of it shared and some of it not.
// Open `game.vein` beside it and type `$` — `$Mover` appears with `kit.Movement.Drive` next to it, and
// `$Internals` does not appear at all, because only `shared("…")` crosses a bundle boundary (RULES 17).

bundle Movement by kit {

    // The kit needs the standard library too, for the same reason the game does: `$Position` below is
    // a BARE name, and `need` is what makes a bare name mean something from another bundle.
    need "Vein.Transform"

    // THE PUBLIC HALF. These two are the whole API, and the `shared("…")` on each is what puts them in
    // `BundleIndex` — a completion list, `veinc symbols`, and a `*kit.Movement.Drive.$Mover` reference
    // all read the same gate.
    publicator Drive {
        shared("How fast a thing moves, per tick, on each axis.")
        shape $Mover { dx: float, dy: float }

        shared("Wear this to be moved by the kit.")
        mark #Moving
    }

    // THE PRIVATE HALF, deliberately outside the publicator. The kit uses it; nothing else can see it,
    // and completion in another bundle must never offer it — `ScopeIndexTests` asserts exactly that.
    shape $Budget { remaining: int }

    // The kit's own behaviour. It arrives because the game `need`s the bundle: a kit is linked, not
    // merely readable (RULES 15c/18), so this shard runs in the game's runtime without the manifest
    // mentioning it.
    shard Drift {
        each tick {
            // BARE, and not by preference: a `target` takes bare shapes only — the qualified form does
            // not even parse here (RULES 17b). This is the rule the Workbench's completion encodes, and
            // writing this sample is how it was confirmed rather than assumed.
            target $Position $Mover #Moving as m {
                m.Position.x = m.Position.x + m.Mover.dx
                m.Position.y = m.Position.y + m.Mover.dy
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/vein.discovery`

**vein.discovery: what completion offers** — A plain-text policy file, not a program. It controls what the editor's `*`/sigil completion lists for everything under samples/: `silent all`, then only Vein.Console and Vein.Math exposed, with commented examples of per-publicator and transitive rules.  
Teaches: discovery policy · discovery vs consumption (shared) · most-specific path wins · nearest file wins · silent transitive  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: reference  
Notes: A config file, so nothing to run. samples/kitdemo/vein.discovery is a better on-screen version of the same idea: it demonstrates most-specific-wins and explains shared vs discovery. Parser: src/Vein.Compiler/Project/DiscoveryPolicy.cs.

```text
# vein.discovery — controls what `*` wildcard discovery ENUMERATES (autocomplete / browsing).
#
#   silent  = hidden from `*` discovery      expose = shown in `*` discovery
#   `silent` controls DISCOVERY; `shared` still controls CONSUMPTION — an explicit
#   *Author.Bundle.Publicator.@member always resolves and runs even if silenced here.
#
# Most-specific path wins; a path is Author | Author.Bundle | Author.Bundle.Publicator.
# `silent all` / `expose all` set the default (no file, or neither line → everything is discoverable).

silent all                    # nuclear: nothing shows in `*` unless exposed below

expose Vein.Console           # this whole bundle is discoverable
expose Vein.Math

# expose a specific publicator only:
# expose acme.Combat.Damage

# re-silence a child under an exposed parent:
# silent acme.Combat.Internal

# --- transitive dependencies ---------------------------------------------------
# Importing a big dependency should NOT dump its whole tree into `*`. Name the front door and its
# transitive tree is silenced in ONE line; the principal stays visible. Then expose what you consume.
#
#   silent transitive *MegaApp.PrincipalBundle   # principal visible; everything else silent
#   expose *MegaApp.Physics                       # + one dependency you actually use
#   # MegaApp.Networking / Audio / Rendering stay silent (still resolvable if referenced explicitly)
#
# (`silent transitive` with no target = silent-by-default root, like `silent all`.)
```

## `samples/stdlib_events.vein`

**Stdlib events: input, UI, combat** — The program emits standard-library events itself (a key press, typed text, a mouse click, a UI click and focus on a spawned button, a collision and damage) and hears each one with its own shard, printing what arrived. There is no device: these events are ordinary data occurrences.  
Run: `veinc run samples/stdlib_events.vein --ticks 3`  
Teaches: stdlib events as data occurrences · spawn is immediate, attach/mark deferred · events carrying an Entity target · qualified hear on stdlib events · host transport events vs occurrences  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Listed in tools/check-backend.sh (stdlib_events.vein:3). Output order follows shard declaration order: Setup's click/focus print before Device's key events.

Real output of `veinc run samples/stdlib_events.vein --ticks 3`:
```text
clicked entity 1
focused entity 1
key down: a
typed: hello
mouse 0 at 12,40
collision between 2 and 2
damaged 2 for 7
```

```vein
// stdlib_events.vein — the standard library's OCCURRENCES: Input, UI interaction, Game combat.
//
//   veinc run samples/stdlib_events.vein --ticks 3
//
// IN tools/check-backend.sh — and it was not, until the language learned to tell a data occurrence
// from one carrying host transport.
//
// The C# backend used to emit neither a cross-bundle `emit` nor a `hear` for ANY event declared in
// another bundle. Half of that was right: `*Vein.Net.Http.@Fetch` performs an HTTP request and
// `*Vein.Files.Io.@ReadFile` reads a file, and the INTERPRETER is what implements them. Emitting a
// payload class and a queue for those would make `emit @Fetch` a program that compiles, runs, and
// silently never fetches.
//
// The other half was never about them. Every event below is pure data — it queues, it dispatches to a
// `hear`, and nothing outside the program happens. Refusing them cost a compiled game its input, its
// collisions and its clock.
//
// `Interp.HostEvents` is the distinction, and it lives in the interpreter because `Drain` is what
// decides it: the nine names it consumes and `continue`s are transport, everything else is an
// occurrence. So this file is now diffed frame for frame like every other sample, and `@Fetch` is
// still refused with the note it always had.
//
// THE PROGRAM EMITS ITS OWN INPUT. There is no device here, and `Vein.Input`'s events are ordinary
// occurrences — a runtime emits them when a key is pressed, and a program can emit them to describe a
// key having been pressed. That is what makes this runnable at all, and it is the same contract
// samples/motion.vein relies on for time.

bundle StdlibEvents by you {

    need "Vein.UI"

    shape $Log { line: string }
    mark #Logged

    mark #Button

    // `spawn()` is IMMEDIATE while `attach` and `mark` are deferred to the phase boundary, so the id is
    // usable in the same block that created it — which is what lets the interaction events name their
    // target here rather than needing a `target` in a later frame to go looking for it.
    shard Setup {
        run once {
            let ok = spawn()
            attach $Button to ok { label: "OK", id: "ok" }
            mark ok #Button

            emit *Vein.UI.Interaction.@Clicked { target: ok }
            emit *Vein.UI.Interaction.@Focused { target: ok }
        }
    }

    // ---- Vein.Input ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Device {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Input.Keyboard.@KeyDown { key: "a" }
            emit *Vein.Input.Keyboard.@TextInput { text: "hello" }
            emit *Vein.Input.Mouse.@MouseDown { x: 12.0, y: 40.0, button: 0 }
        }
    }

    shard Keys {
        hear *Vein.Input.Keyboard.@KeyDown as k {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "key down: " + k.key }
        }
    }

    shard Typing {
        hear *Vein.Input.Keyboard.@TextInput as t {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "typed: " + t.text }
        }
    }

    shard Pointer {
        hear *Vein.Input.Mouse.@MouseDown as m {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "mouse " + m.button + " at " + m.x + "," + m.y }
        }
    }

    // ---- Vein.UI interaction ------------------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // The interaction events carry the target Entity, which is what lets any bundle react to a specific
    // widget rather than to "a button somewhere".

    shard Reacting {
        hear *Vein.UI.Interaction.@Clicked as c {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "clicked entity " + c.target }
        }
    }

    shard Focusing {
        hear *Vein.UI.Interaction.@Focused as f {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "focused entity " + f.target }
        }
    }

    // ---- Vein.Game combat ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Fight {
        run once {
            let hero = spawn()
            emit *Vein.Game.Collision.@Collided { a: hero, b: hero }
            emit *Vein.Game.Combat.@Damaged { target: hero, amount: 7 }
        }
    }

    shard Wounds {
        hear *Vein.Game.Combat.@Damaged as d {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "damaged " + d.target + " for " + d.amount }
        }
    }

    shard Contacts {
        hear *Vein.Game.Collision.@Collided as c {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "collision between " + c.a + " and " + c.b }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/math_round.vein`

**Math stdlib: rounding and roots** — This sample calls Vein.Math's Round and Roots functions by their full qualified path and prints the results: truncate vs floor vs ceil, round half away from zero, roundTo for 0.1 + 0.2, pow10, sqrt and distance. The standard library itself is ordinary VeinScript.  
Run: `veinc run samples/math_round.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: calling stdlib fn by qualified path · stdlib written in VeinScript · trunc vs floor on negatives · round half away from zero · roundTo for float display  
Difficulty 1/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Listed in tools/check-backend.sh (math_round.vein:1). Line 43 wraps `(0.1 + 0.2)` in parentheses on purpose: without them `+` concatenates left to right, and `"raw = " + 0.1 + 0.2` prints `raw = 0.10.2`.

Real output of `veinc run samples/math_round.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
trunc  2.7 = 2
trunc -2.7 = -2
floor  2.7 = 2
floor -2.7 = -3
ceil   2.1 = 3
ceil  -2.1 = -2
round  2.5 = 3
round  2.4 = 2
round -2.5 = -3
raw        = 0.30000000000000004
to 2 dp    = 0.3
pi to 2    = 3.14
pi to 4    = 3.1416
pow10 3    = 1000
sqrt 16    = 4
sqrt 2     = 1.4142
dist 3,4   = 5
dist same  = 0
```

```vein
// math_round.vein — rounding and roots, from stdlib/Math.vein.
//
//   veinc run samples/math_round.vein --ticks 1
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh. `Vein.Math`'s Round and Roots publicators had no sample at all, which
// meant nothing diffed them between the interpreter and the compiled program.
//
// THESE ARE WRITTEN IN VEINSCRIPT, not built in — stdlib/Math.vein is ordinary source you can read.
// `sqrt` is twenty Newton steps; `floor` is arithmetic on a truncation. That is worth knowing, because
// it is also why they are `shared fn` in a publicator and reached by their qualified path.
//
// WHY THEY EXIST AT ALL: `int(x)` truncates toward zero, which is the right answer for indexing and
// the wrong one for almost everything else. `int(-2.5)` is -2; `floor(-2.5)` is -3. The difference
// only shows up on negative numbers, which is exactly when nobody is looking.

bundle MathRound by you {

    shard Rounding {
        run once {
            // TRUNCATE versus FLOOR. Identical for positives, different for every negative — the
            // reason there are two of them.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "trunc  2.7 = " + *Vein.Math.Round.trunc(2.7) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "trunc -2.7 = " + *Vein.Math.Round.trunc(-2.7) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "floor  2.7 = " + *Vein.Math.Round.floor(2.7) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "floor -2.7 = " + *Vein.Math.Round.floor(-2.7) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "ceil   2.1 = " + *Vein.Math.Round.ceil(2.1) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "ceil  -2.1 = " + *Vein.Math.Round.ceil(-2.1) }

            // Halves go AWAY FROM ZERO, not up: round(2.5) is 3 and round(-2.5) is -3. That keeps
            // `round(-v)` equal to `-round(v)`, which half-up quietly breaks — and a column of
            // half-up numbers then drifts upward as you total it.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "round  2.5 = " + *Vein.Math.Round.round(2.5) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "round  2.4 = " + *Vein.Math.Round.round(2.4) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "round -2.5 = " + *Vein.Math.Round.round(-2.5) }
        }
    }

    shard Places {
        run once {
            // roundTo is what binary floating point makes necessary. 0.1 + 0.2 is
            // 0.30000000000000004 in any language with doubles, and printing that to somebody is not
            // an answer — it is an implementation detail with a decimal point in it.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "raw        = " + (0.1 + 0.2) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "to 2 dp    = " + *Vein.Math.Round.roundTo(0.1 + 0.2, 2) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "pi to 2    = " + *Vein.Math.Round.roundTo(3.14159, 2) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "pi to 4    = " + *Vein.Math.Round.roundTo(3.14159, 4) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "pow10 3    = " + *Vein.Math.Round.pow10(3) }
        }
    }

    shard Roots {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "sqrt 16    = " + *Vein.Math.Roots.sqrt(16.0) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "sqrt 2     = " + *Vein.Math.Round.roundTo(*Vein.Math.Roots.sqrt(2.0), 4) }

            // The 3-4-5 triangle, which is the one to check a distance function against because the
            // answer is a whole number and any error in the root shows immediately.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "dist 3,4   = " + *Vein.Math.Roots.distance(0.0, 0.0, 3.0, 4.0) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "dist same  = " + *Vein.Math.Roots.distance(2.0, 2.0, 2.0, 2.0) }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/motion.vein`

**Motion: two forces and an Euler step** — A Mover entity built from stdlib $Position and $Velocity is pushed by two independent shards: Gravity subtracts 4.9 from Velocity.y and Wind adds 1.0 to Velocity.x every tick. An app-written Euler step moves the position, and a settled shard prints the rounded state each tick. stdlib's $Velocity folds sum on each axis, but the two forces write different axes, so nothing is reconciled in this run.  
Run: `veinc run samples/motion.vein --ticks 4`  
Teaches: folds sum declared per axis on stdlib $Velocity (unused here: the forces write different axes) · velocity persists across ticks, so a constant push becomes acceleration · builder with qualified stdlib includes · positional binding across includes · phase boundary: each tick vs settled · explicit Euler integration  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 18  
Notes: Listed in tools/check-backend.sh (motion.vein:4). Two header claims are out of step with the code. Lines 64-66 say Gravity and Wind write the same field, but Gravity writes Velocity.y and Wind writes Velocity.x, so `folds sum` never reconciles anything here. A scratch copy using an unfolded `shape $Vel { x: float, y: float, z: float }` printed exactly the same four frames. The fold only matters when both shards write one axis: with Wind changed to `m.Velocity.y -= 1.0`, v.y goes -5.9, -11.8, -17.7 with the stdlib fold and -1, -2, -3 without it, because the last write wins. The 'NO CROSS-BUNDLE EVENTS HERE' paragraph (lines 21-27) is also dated: stdlib_events.vein and clock_ticked.vein, which hears @Ticked, are both in check-backend.sh now.

Real output of `veinc run samples/motion.vein --ticks 4`:
```text
t = 0.5
  at 0, 100   v 1, -4.9
t = 1
  at 0.5, 97.55   v 2, -9.8
t = 1.5
  at 1.5, 92.65   v 3, -14.7
t = 2
  at 3, 85.3   v 4, -19.6
```

```vein
// motion.vein — Vein.Transform and Vein.Time, used by a program that moves things.
//
//   veinc run samples/motion.vein --ticks 4
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh. Two stdlib bundles had no sample at all, so nothing checked that their
// shapes still resolve into something a program can use — and this is also the only sample that does
// `each tick` arithmetic in FLOAT. Everything else ticks in int.
//
// WHY THE INTEGRATION IS HERE AND NOT IN THE STDLIB. stdlib/Transform.vein says it outright: stepping
// $Position from $Velocity is the application's job, because the rules — fixed or variable step,
// clamping, sub-stepping — belong to the application. No bundle in stdlib/ declares a single shard,
// bridge or ShardView; the standard library is vocabulary, not a framework. `$Velocity folds sum` is
// the part stdlib DOES provide, and it is what lets the two force shards below stay unaware of
// each other.
//
// A STDLIB SHAPE REACHES AN ENTITY THROUGH A BUILDER INCLUDE. `*Vein.Transform.Spatial.$Position`
// inside a builder is the supported route, and the one stdlib/Web.vein already uses for
// `*Vein.UI.Widgets.$Button`. The include resolves the qualified path away, so the component is named
// `Position` — which is why `target $Position` matches it and `m.Position.x` reads it.
//
// NO CROSS-BUNDLE EVENTS HERE, DELIBERATELY. `Vein.Time.@Ticked` and `Vein.Transform.@Moved` would be
// the natural way to write this, and the interpreter runs them fine — but the C# backend does not emit
// a `hear` for an event declared in another bundle (it says so: "there is no payload type here and its
// transport lives on the interpreter"). A sample in check-backend.sh has to be a program BOTH runtimes
// can run, or it is not a differential test. So the clock is read directly and the step happens in
// `each tick`. Covering @Ticked and @Moved needs the backend to emit payload types for external
// events first; docs/SAMPLES.md records that.
//
// TIME IS SUPPLIED BY THE PROGRAM, not read from a clock. stdlib/Time.vein's header says a runtime
// emits time "when appropriate", so a program stepping its own clock is the documented contract rather
// than a workaround. It is also what makes this runnable in check-backend.sh at all: a sample whose
// output depends on wall-clock time fails at random, and a test that fails at random gets deleted.

bundle Motion by you {

    mark #Moving

    // Two includes in one builder. Their fields collide by name — both $Position and $Velocity declare
    // x, y and z — and that is fine, because arguments bind POSITIONALLY across the includes in
    // declaration order: the first three are the position, the next three the velocity.
    builder Mover {
        *Vein.Transform.Spatial.$Position
        *Vein.Transform.Spatial.$Velocity
        mark #Moving
    }

    // $Clock is Vein.Time's shape, carried on the identity that owns the step. `delta` is fixed at half
    // a second so four frames are readable by hand.
    builder Ticker {
        *Vein.Time.Clock.$Clock
        mark #Clock
    }
    mark #Clock

    shard Start {
        run once {
            bring Mover(0.0, 100.0, 0.0,  0.0, 0.0, 0.0)
            bring Ticker(0.0, 0.5)
        }
    }

    // ---- two independent forces, neither aware of the other ----------------------------------------
    //
    // THIS IS WHAT `folds sum` IS FOR. Both shards write the same field in the same frame. Neither reads
    // the other, neither runs in a defined order relative to the other, and the runtime reconciles the
    // two writes into one value at the frame boundary. Written any other way, one of them would win.
    //
    // Because contributions accumulate frame after frame, a constant force reads as an ACCELERATION —
    // which is exactly what gravity is.

    shard Gravity {
        each tick { target $Velocity #Moving as m { m.Velocity.y -= 4.9 } }
    }

    shard Wind {
        each tick { target $Velocity #Moving as m { m.Velocity.x += 1.0 } }
    }

    shard Clock {
        each tick { target $Clock #Clock as c { c.Clock.now += c.Clock.delta } }
    }

    // ---- the application's own integration step ----------------------------------------------------
    //
    // THE FIRST FRAME DOES NOT MOVE, and that is the phase boundary showing through rather than a bug.
    // The forces above contribute during frame 1; those contributions are reconciled at the boundary
    // and are what frame 2 reads (RULES 11/12b). So position always steps by the velocity as it stood
    // at the END of the previous frame — an explicit Euler step, which is the ordinary one.

    shard Step {
        each tick {
            target $Position $Velocity #Moving as m {
                m.Position.x += m.Velocity.x * 0.5
                m.Position.y += m.Velocity.y * 0.5
            }
        }
    }

    // Reported from `settled`, after every contribution in the frame has been reconciled — reporting
    // from `each tick` would race the shards above and print a half-built frame.
    //
    // Rounded on the way out because a float printed at full precision is an implementation detail with
    // a decimal point in it: 0.1 + 0.2 is 0.30000000000000004 in any language with doubles.
    shard Report {
        settled {
            target $Clock #Clock as c {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "t = " + *Vein.Math.Round.roundTo(c.Clock.now, 2) }
            }
            target $Position $Velocity #Moving as m {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: "  at " + *Vein.Math.Round.roundTo(m.Position.x, 2) +
                          ", " + *Vein.Math.Round.roundTo(m.Position.y, 2) +
                          "   v " + *Vein.Math.Round.roundTo(m.Velocity.x, 2) +
                          ", " + *Vein.Math.Round.roundTo(m.Velocity.y, 2)
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/game_collision.vein`

**Collision pairs with nested target** — Four boxes carrying Vein.Game's $Collider and $Body are placed on a line. A settled shard nests one target inside another to test every pair once for overlap using half-extents, and a second shard prints each body's mass and bounce.  
Run: `veinc run samples/game_collision.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: nested target (pair queries) · outer binding read in inner loop · stdlib vocabulary, game behaviour · spawn / attach / mark by hand · plain fn over floats · half-extent AABB overlap  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Listed in tools/check-backend.sh (game_collision.vein:2). The hand-written abs uses `0.0 - v`, but unary minus also works (stdlib's abs writes `-v`).

Real output of `veinc run samples/game_collision.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
overlap: box at 0 and box at 3  (gap 3)
  body at 0  mass 1  bounce 0.5
  body at 3  mass 2  bounce 0.1
  body at 7  mass 4  bounce 0.9
  body at 30  mass 1  bounce 0
overlap: box at 0 and box at 3  (gap 3)
  body at 0  mass 1  bounce 0.5
  body at 3  mass 2  bounce 0.1
  body at 7  mass 4  bounce 0.9
  body at 30  mass 1  bounce 0
```

```vein
// game_collision.vein — Vein.Game's collision and body shapes, with the game supplying the behaviour.
//
//   veinc run samples/game_collision.vein --ticks 2
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh. stdlib/Game.vein had no sample, so nothing checked that $Collider and
// $Body still resolve into something a program can attach and read.
//
// THE LIBRARY SUPPLIES THE VOCABULARY; THE GAME SUPPLIES THE BEHAVIOUR. Game.vein says so in its own
// closing comment and gives the shape of this file almost verbatim: a game writes
// `each tick { target $Collider #Collidable as self { … } }` and decides for itself what overlapping
// means. It cannot live in the library because a shard cannot be `shared` — one declared there would
// not be part of the API, just an invisible system installed in every consumer's frame loop because
// they wanted a rectangle.
//
// PAIRS ARE THE PART WORTH A SAMPLE. A collision is between TWO identities and `target` walks one at a
// time, so the inner loop is a second `target` over the same query — and an outer binding read inside
// an inner loop has to still mean the OUTER entity (RULES 12c). This file is the only sample that
// nests two identity queries and reads across them.
//
// NO @Collided HERE. Emitting Vein.Game's event is the natural next line, and the C# backend emits
// neither a cross-bundle event nor a `hear` for one — so a sample using it could not be diffed against
// the interpreter. samples/stdlib_events.vein covers it, interpreter-only and marked as such.

bundle GameCollision by you {

    need "Vein.Game"
    need "Vein.Transform"

    mark #Collidable

    // No `abs` in the standard library, and the pair test needs one on both axes. Small enough to
    // declare, and it doubles as this file's use of a plain `fn` over floats.
    fn abs(v: float) return float {
        if v < 0.0 { return 0.0 - v }
        return v
    }

    shard Arena {
        run once {
            // Four boxes on a line, chosen so the answer is checkable by hand. `width` and `height` are
            // HALF-EXTENTS (Game.vein says so), so two boxes overlap on an axis when the gap between
            // their centres is less than the sum of their half-widths.
            //
            //   a at 0 and b at 3, half-width 2 each  → gap 3 < 4   overlap
            //   b at 3 and c at 7, half-width 2 each  → gap 4 < 4   false, exactly touching
            //   d at 30                               → clear of everything
            let a = spawn()
            attach $Position to a { x: 0.0, y: 0.0, z: 0.0 }
            attach $Collider to a { width: 2.0, height: 2.0 }
            attach $Body to a { mass: 1.0, restitution: 0.5 }
            mark a #Collidable

            let b = spawn()
            attach $Position to b { x: 3.0, y: 0.0, z: 0.0 }
            attach $Collider to b { width: 2.0, height: 2.0 }
            attach $Body to b { mass: 2.0, restitution: 0.1 }
            mark b #Collidable

            let c = spawn()
            attach $Position to c { x: 7.0, y: 0.0, z: 0.0 }
            attach $Collider to c { width: 2.0, height: 2.0 }
            attach $Body to c { mass: 4.0, restitution: 0.9 }
            mark c #Collidable

            let d = spawn()
            attach $Position to d { x: 30.0, y: 0.0, z: 0.0 }
            attach $Collider to d { width: 1.0, height: 1.0 }
            attach $Body to d { mass: 1.0, restitution: 0.0 }
            mark d #Collidable
        }
    }

    // ---- the game's own broad phase ----------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Overlaps {
        settled {
            target $Position $Collider #Collidable as a {
                target $Position $Collider #Collidable as b {
                    // EACH PAIR ONCE, and never with itself. Ordering on x is what does it: for any two
                    // distinct boxes exactly one arrangement satisfies this, and no box is left of
                    // itself. An index comparison would be the usual trick and `Index` names only the
                    // NEAREST loop, so the outer one has no number to compare against.
                    if a.Position.x < b.Position.x {
                        let dx = abs(b.Position.x - a.Position.x)
                        let dy = abs(b.Position.y - a.Position.y)

                        if dx < a.Collider.width + b.Collider.width {
                            if dy < a.Collider.height + b.Collider.height {
                                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                                    text: "overlap: box at " + a.Position.x + " and box at " + b.Position.x +
                                          "  (gap " + dx + ")"
                                }
                            }
                        }
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // $Body is the other half of Vein.Game's vocabulary — what a resolver would need once an overlap is
    // known. Nothing here resolves anything; the sample's job is that the data is reachable.
    shard Bodies {
        settled {
            target $Position $Body #Collidable as e {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: "  body at " + e.Position.x + "  mass " + e.Body.mass +
                          "  bounce " + e.Body.restitution
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

---

# Text, JSON, files and databases

## `samples/text_search.vein`

**Text search: find, slice, replace** — Uses the built-in string functions (trim, contains, startsWith, endsWith, indexOf, substring, replace, upper, lower) on a log line and prints each result. A second shard walks a few lines of text and keeps only the ones that start with ERROR.  
Run: `veinc run samples/text_search.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: string built-ins are plain functions, not methods · total functions: indexOf answers -1, substring clamps, nothing throws · ordinal (culture-free) string comparison · case-insensitive comparison via lower(a) == lower(b) · walking a list with target lines(...) as line  
Difficulty 1/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Header comment is accurate: the file is listed in tools/check-backend.sh. Ran cleanly with the prebuilt Debug veinc.exe.

Real output of `veinc run samples/text_search.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
trimmed  = [ERROR: disk full on /dev/sda1]
contains = true
starts   = true
ends     = true
empty in = true
at       = 7
missing  = -1
level    = ERROR
rest     = disk full on /dev/sda1
past end = []
too long = [bc]
replaced = WARN: disk full on /dev/sda1
absent   = abc
upper    = MIXED
lower    = mixed
same?    = true
! no disk
! no net
```

```vein
// text_search.vein — looking inside a string, and slicing it.
//
//   veinc run samples/text_search.vein --ticks 1
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh. Before this file, `indexOf`, `substring` and `endsWith` had ZERO call
// sites in any sample — they appeared only inside comments — so the interpreter and the C# backend
// were never diffed on one of them.
//
// EVERY ONE OF THESE IS ORDINAL, never culture-sensitive, so they agree with `<` and with how the
// store sorts. A case-insensitive test is `lower(a) == lower(b)` rather than a second comparison
// operator that quietly means something else.
//
// They are all TOTAL. Nothing here throws: `indexOf` answers -1 for absent, `substring` clamps to the
// string, and an out-of-range read is "" rather than a crash. A language with no `catch` has nowhere
// to put a guard, so the operations do not need one.

bundle TextSearch by you {

    shard Look {
        run once {
            let line = "  ERROR: disk full on /dev/sda1  "
            let trimmed = trim(line)

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "trimmed  = [" + trimmed + "]" }

            // ---- is it in there ----------------------------------------------------------------

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "contains = " + contains(trimmed, "disk full") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "starts   = " + startsWith(trimmed, "ERROR") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "ends     = " + endsWith(trimmed, "sda1") }

            // The empty needle is TRUE — every string contains the empty string. Worth stating,
            // because the opposite reading silently drops every line when a filter box is blank.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "empty in = " + contains(trimmed, "") }

            // ---- where is it -------------------------------------------------------------------
            //
            // -1 for absent is the one value a real position can never be, so `indexOf(s, x) >= 0`
            // reads as "is in there" without a second call.

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "at       = " + indexOf(trimmed, "disk") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "missing  = " + indexOf(trimmed, "network") }

            // ---- cut it up ---------------------------------------------------------------------
            //
            // Two forms: to the end, or a given length. Both CLAMP rather than failing.

            let colon = indexOf(trimmed, ":")
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "level    = " + substring(trimmed, 0, colon) }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "rest     = " + substring(trimmed, colon + 2) }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "past end = [" + substring(trimmed, 900) + "]" }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "too long = [" + substring("abc", 1, 99) + "]" }

            // ---- change it ---------------------------------------------------------------------

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "replaced = " + replace(trimmed, "ERROR", "WARN") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "absent   = " + replace("abc", "zz", "!") }

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "upper    = " + upper("MiXeD") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "lower    = " + lower("MiXeD") }

            // Case-insensitive comparison, the only way there is one.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "same?    = " + (lower("HELLO") == lower("hello")) }
        }
    }

    // A REAL USE, and the reason the family exists at all: deciding something about a line of text
    // that a program read rather than wrote. `if the line mentions ERROR` was unwritable before these.
    shard Filter {
        run once {
            target lines("ok: started\nERROR: no disk\nok: ready\nERROR: no net") as line {
                if startsWith(line, "ERROR") {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "! " + substring(line, indexOf(line, " ") + 1) }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/text_split_join.vein`

**Split, lines, words, chars, join** — Breaks strings into lists four ways (split, lines, words, chars) and puts them back together with join, including a round trip that returns the original string. It ends by parsing a tiny CSV table and printing each row in a new format.  
Run: `veinc run samples/text_split_join.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: four splitters with different rules: split keeps empties, lines strips \r, words drops empties, chars gives one-character strings · join is the only way back to a string · every splitter returns a list, which target walks · list indexing f[0] · string(list) versus a deliberate join  
Difficulty 2/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Stale doc: RULES.md 28a ('CHARACTER WORK COMPILES; the splits do not') and the doc comment above IsPrebuilt in src/Vein.Compiler/Backends/CSharpBackend.cs (~line 1203) both say split/lines/words/trim/join are absent from the backend, but IsPrebuilt and RuntimeCall now include them and this sample is in check-backend.sh. The comment on line 34 ('a program that cannot inspect characters') is also dated, since chars() and s[0] exist (RULES 28).

Real output of `veinc run samples/text_split_join.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
cells    = 4 -> north||south|east
lines    = 3 -> first/second/third
words    = 3 -> the.quick.brown
chars    = 3 -> a-b-c
dashes   = 2026-09-06
slashes  = 2026/09/06
nothing  = 20260906
round    = true
as text  = [2026, 09, 06]
Ada is 36
Grace is 45
Alan is 41
```

```vein
// text_split_join.vein — text into pieces, and pieces back into text.
//
//   veinc run samples/text_split_join.vein --ticks 1
//
// In tools/check-backend.sh. `split` and `join` are the two most ordinary text operations there are,
// and before this file NEITHER had a single call site in any sample.
//
// FOUR WAYS APART, ONE WAY BACK. `split`, `lines`, `words` and `chars` all hand back a list, and
// `join` is the only way back — which is why `target <list> as x` walks all four with one statement.
//
// The four are not interchangeable, and choosing wrongly is quiet:
//
//   split(s, sep)   exact separator, exact pieces. Nothing is discarded.
//   lines(s)        by line, with the ENDING removed whichever it was.
//   words(s)        runs of whitespace, no empties.
//   chars(s)        one-character strings.

bundle TextSplitJoin by you {

    shard Apart {
        run once {
            // SPLIT keeps everything. "a,,b" is three pieces and the middle one is empty — a caller
            // counting columns in a CSV needs the empty column to still be there.
            let cells = split("north,,south,east", ",")
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "cells    = " + len(cells) + " -> " + join(cells, "|") }

            // LINES, not split(text, "\n"). They look equivalent and are not: a file written on
            // Windows leaves a `\r` on every line, and `line == "end"` then fails against "end\r"
            // with both sides looking identical in any output. That trap is what this closes.
            let rows = lines("first\r\nsecond\nthird")
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "lines    = " + len(rows) + " -> " + join(rows, "/") }

            // WORDS collapses runs of whitespace and drops empties. `split(line, " ")` on "a  b"
            // gives three pieces with an empty middle, and a program that cannot inspect characters
            // has no good way to tell an empty word from a real one after the fact.
            let ws = words("  the   quick  brown   ")
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "words    = " + len(ws) + " -> " + join(ws, ".") }

            // CHARS completes the family. A character is a one-character string — there is no `char`
            // type and no 'a' literal, because `==` already compares one and ordering already works.
            let cs = chars("abc")
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "chars    = " + len(cs) + " -> " + join(cs, "-") }
        }
    }

    shard Together {
        run once {
            let parts = split("2026-09-06", "-")

            // JOIN with any separator, including none.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "dashes   = " + join(parts, "-") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "slashes  = " + join(parts, "/") }
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "nothing  = " + join(parts, "") }

            // A ROUND TRIP. Apart and back together is the original, which is the property that makes
            // `split` safe to reach for — nothing is silently dropped on the way through.
            let original = "a,b,,c"
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "round    = " + (join(split(original, ","), ",") == original) }

            // Rendering a list without asking. `join` is how you do it deliberately; this is what a
            // list looks like when it simply lands in a string.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "as text  = " + string(parts) }
        }
    }

    // A REAL USE: a table of records, split apart, walked, and rebuilt in a different shape.
    shard Table {
        run once {
            target lines("ada,36\ngrace,45\nalan,41") as row {
                let f = split(row, ",")
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: upper(substring(f[0], 0, 1)) + substring(f[0], 1) + " is " + f[1] }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/json_roundtrip.vein`

**JSON in, identities, JSON out** — Parses a JSON payload with fromJson, brings one $Row identity per record sorted by rank, then queries them and serialises each back with toJson(r.Row). The output shows the records keep their types: int ranks and bool flags.  
Run: `veinc run samples/json_roundtrip.vein --ticks 1`  
Teaches: fromJson: objects become dictionaries, arrays become lists · collection target with a type ascription (as row: $Row) · ordered by sorts the brings, so spawn order becomes rank order · toJson takes a shape on an identity, not the identity · settled phase for reporting · query order == spawn order  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 28  
Notes: Not in tools/check-backend.sh (interpreter-only by design, as the header says). Ran cleanly.

Real output of `veinc run samples/json_roundtrip.vein --ticks 1`:
```text
parsed 3 rows
-- as identities, in the order they were brought --
   -2  Zeta  done=false
   1  alpha  done=true
   4  delta  done=false
-- serialised back --
   {"title":"Zeta","rank":-2,"done":false}
   {"title":"alpha","rank":1,"done":true}
   {"title":"delta","rank":4,"done":false}
-- values are typed, not text --
   rank+1 = 2, and done is true
```

```vein
// json_roundtrip.vein — JSON in, identities out; identities in, JSON out.
//
// Two builtins, `fromJson` and `toJson`, and between them a payload that arrived over the wire becomes
// a world you can query, and a world you queried becomes a payload you can send back.
//
//   veinc run samples/json_roundtrip.vein --ticks 1
//
// WHY THEY ARE BUILTINS AND NOT A STDLIB BUNDLE. Not because character work is impossible — `s[0]`
// yields a character and `==` compares it (RULES 28) — but because a parser written that way would walk
// every character through the interpreter, building the result one concatenation at a time, to arrive
// at what the host already has. `toJson` also has to escape a tab or a newline inside every value,
// which doubles the work for something no program should be spending its ticks on.
//
// THE READ DIRECTION NEEDED NO LANGUAGE CHANGE AT ALL. An object parses to a dictionary, and field
// access already resolves against a dictionary, so `row.title` below is the ordinary `.` — the same one
// that reads an event payload. An array parses to a list, which `target … as x` already iterates and
// `len` already measures. Both were true before this file existed.
//
// WHAT toJson TAKES IS A SHAPE, NOT AN ENTITY. `toJson(r.Row)` serialises the fields `$Row` declares.
// `toJson(someEntity)` cannot work and is not offered: nothing can ask an identity which shapes it
// carries (RULES.md 14), so there would be no way to know what to write.
//
// INTERPRETER ONLY, and it fails LOUDLY rather than quietly. Neither builtin has a C# emission, so
// `veinc emit` on this file prints
//
//     note: built-in fromJson() has no C# emission — it stays on the interpreter, so this file will
//           not compile.
//
// and the generated code then does not compile. That is deliberate: substituting a placeholder value
// would produce C# that compiles and computes something DIFFERENT, which is the one failure the backend
// contract forbids. A compile error with a note beats a program that quietly disagrees with itself.

bundle JsonRoundtrip by you {

    shape $Row { title: string, rank: int, done: bool }
    mark #Row

    builder Row { $Row   mark #Row }

    shard Ingest {
        run once {
            // Standing in for a request body or a file. One string, three records, unordered.
            let payload = "{\"rows\":[" +
                          "{\"title\":\"delta\",\"rank\":4,\"done\":false}," +
                          "{\"title\":\"alpha\",\"rank\":1,\"done\":true}," +
                          "{\"title\":\"Zeta\",\"rank\":-2,\"done\":false}]}"

            let doc = fromJson(payload)

            // `.rows` is a plain field access on the parsed object, and the result is a list — so the
            // collection form of `target` walks it with no conversion step.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "parsed " + len(doc.rows) + " rows" }

            // ORDERED AT THE BRING, not just at the read. The payload arrived 4, 1, -2 and the brings
            // run -2, 1, 4, so the identities are SPAWNED in rank order and every later reader gets that
            // for free — including one that cannot sort, which is the case this exists for: a `bring` on
            // a fragment builder emits its @Html the moment it runs and leaves nothing to query.
            //
            // The block holds the loop rather than the brings, because how many brings there are lives
            // in the data: one written `bring` inside `target` becomes one per row.
            //
            // `as row: $Row` is the ASCRIPTION, and it is the only thing that can speak for data that
            // arrived from outside. `fromJson` returns whatever the payload held — the compiler cannot
            // know a row has a title — so without it `row.titel` is a silent nothing rather than a
            // mistake, which in code talking to a schema someone else controls is the bug that costs
            // most. The author knows the shape: they are about to `bring Row(…)` with it.
            //
            // It describes a RECORD, not a component. Nothing is attached here, so fields are read
            // directly as `row.title` — unlike `target $Row #Row as r`, where the shape names a
            // component ON an identity and the read is `r.Row.title`.
            //
            // Optional, always: drop it and the loop still runs, untyped, which is what you want while
            // exploring a payload whose shape you do not know yet.
            ordered by rank {
                target doc.rows as row: $Row {
                    bring Row(row.title, row.rank, row.done)
                }
            }
        }
    }

    shard Report {
        settled {
            // A PLAIN query — no `by` — and it still comes out in rank order, because `ordered by`
            // above spawned them that way. That is the difference between the two places an order can
            // be imposed: sorting the read fixes one reader, sorting the brings fixes every reader,
            // including the ones that cannot sort at all.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- as identities, in the order they were brought --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: "   " + r.Row.rank + "  " + r.Row.title + "  done=" + r.Row.done
                }
            }

            // And back out. `toJson(r.Row)` writes every field `$Row` declares, in declaration order,
            // so this is the same record that arrived — a genuine round trip rather than a re-print.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- serialised back --" }
            target $Row #Row by Row.rank as r {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + toJson(r.Row) }
            }

            // Types survive the trip: 4 comes back as 4 and not "4" or 4.0, and false stays a bool.
            // An integral number parses as an int precisely so `rank` stays orderable and printable.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- values are typed, not text --" }
            target $Row #Row as r {
                if r.Row.rank == 1 {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                        text: "   rank+1 = " + (r.Row.rank + 1) + ", and done is " + r.Row.done
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/file_edit.vein`

**Editing a config, scanning a log** — Writes a config file and a log, then reads them back through events. It flips one setting in the config with replace (writing only when something changed) and scans the log for ERROR/FATAL lines, turning each into a $Problem identity with a #Fatal mark where it applies.  
Run: `veinc run samples/file_edit.vein --ticks 2`  
Teaches: a file is an event: @ReadFile -> @FileLoaded / @FileMissing, @WriteFile -> @FileWritten · no open/close, no handle · idempotent edit guard to break a write -> re-read -> write loop · filtering lines with contains/startsWith and `and not` · bring ... as e plus mark to remember a decision · routing by f.path (and why it stops scaling)  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good
