Notes: Running it writes samples/config.txt and samples/server.log next to the source whatever the working directory, because relative paths resolve against the .vein file's folder. Neither file is gitignored, so delete them after a demo. There is a possible logic slip in shard Scan: a line that starts with FATAL and also contains ERROR would be brought twice, once per `if`. The sample data has no such line, so the output is unaffected.

Real output of `veinc run samples/file_edit.vein --ticks 2`:
```text
config: fullscreen switched on
log: 2 line(s) worth keeping
  line 2: ERROR disk full
  line 4: FATAL out of memory
  FATAL at line 4
```

```vein
// file_edit.vein — editing a config file, and scanning a log. The two jobs files are actually for.
//
//   veinc run samples/file_edit.vein --ticks 2
//
// THE SHAPE THIS IS ANSWERING. A sketch for file handling might read:
//
//     open "config.txt" as file            read file as text
//     text replace "a" with "b"            write text to file      close file
//     text lines where contains "ERROR" emit @ErrorLine
//
// The GOAL there is right and this file delivers it. The mechanism is different, and the difference is
// worth stating once because it is the same judgement D12 makes about C syntax: a construct earns its
// place on its own merits, not on reading nicely in the one example that inspired it.
//
// NO `open` / `close`. A file here is an EVENT — `@ReadFile` answers `@FileLoaded` or `@FileMissing`,
// the way `@Fetch` answers `@Fetched` or `@Failed`. A handle you must remember to close would be a
// SECOND I/O model contradicting the first, in a language with no resource lifetimes, no destructors
// and no scope that ends. And the failure would go back to being an error to check rather than a
// message to hear.
//
// NO `where … emit` SUBLANGUAGE. `target` + `if` + `emit` already say it, and they compose:
//
//     text lines where contains "ERROR" emit @ErrorLine      // reads well, and stops there
//
//     target lines(f.text) as l {                            // one line longer, and keeps going:
//         if contains(l, "ERROR") and not contains(l, "test") {
//             bring Problem(Index + 1, l) as e
//             if contains(l, "FATAL") { mark e #Fatal }
//         }
//     }
//
// The second condition, the position, the identity, the mark — none of those fit the first form, and a
// reader who learned it would have to unlearn it at the first real filter. Three known keywords beat one
// new one that only works for the example that motivated it.
//
// WHAT WAS ACTUALLY MISSING was neither: `contains`, `replace`, `startsWith`, `endsWith`, `indexOf` and
// `substring` did not exist. That — not syntax — is why `if the line mentions ERROR` could not be
// written. They are ordinary functions, they compile to C# like the character ones, and with them both
// scenarios above are ordinary VeinScript.
//
// TWO FILES IS WHERE `if f.path == "…"` STILL READS FINE, and this sample keeps it so the guard and the
// idempotence lesson below stay in one frame. It does not scale: at nineteen files every handler is a
// wall of path comparisons, none of which fail loudly when they are wrong. `samples/file_tags.vein` is
// the same job routed by `tag` instead — one handler, no path spelled twice (RULES 28c).

bundle FileEdit by you {

    // One log line worth keeping. `n` is its 1-based number, so the report reads back in file order.
    shape $Problem { n: int, text: string }
    mark #Fatal
    builder Problem { $Problem   mark #Problem }
    mark #Problem

    event @Seeded { }
    event @Scanned { count: int }

    // ---- 1. write the two files this sample edits -------------------------------------------------

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Files.Io.@WriteFile {
                path: "config.txt",
                append: false,
                text: "# demo config\n" +
                      "fullscreen = false\n" +
                      "vsync = true\n" +
                      "volume = 80\n"
            }

            emit *Vein.Files.Io.@WriteFile {
                path: "server.log",
                append: false,
                text: "INFO  started\n" +
                      "ERROR disk full\n" +
                      "INFO  retrying\n" +
                      "FATAL out of memory\n" +
                      "DEBUG ERROR appears in this test line\n"
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- 2. edit a config file --------------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // `open` / `read` / `replace` / `write` / `close` as one statement each, in the shape the language
    // already has: a request, an answer, and a write.

    shard Config {
        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileLoaded as f {
            if f.path == "config.txt" {
                // The whole of `text replace "a" with "b"`.
                let updated = replace(f.text, "fullscreen = false", "fullscreen = true")

                // ONLY WRITE IF SOMETHING CHANGED, and this is the trap a file API invites rather than
                // a detail. Writing answers @FileWritten, and anything that reads on @FileWritten reads
                // this file again, edits it again and writes it again — a loop that never settles and
                // that no single line of it looks wrong.
                //
                // The guard is what makes the edit IDEMPOTENT: the second pass finds nothing to replace,
                // `updated` equals what was read, and the cycle stops on its own. A handle-and-close API
                // would not have helped here; the loop is in the events, not the file.
                if not (updated == f.text) {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "config: fullscreen switched on" }

                    // The whole of `write text to file`. There is no `close` because there is no handle.
                    emit *Vein.Files.Io.@WriteFile { path: f.path, text: updated, append: false }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- 3. scan a log ----------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Scan {
        var seen: int

        var scanned: bool

        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileLoaded as f {
            // Scan the log ONCE. Same reasoning as the config guard: without it a re-read would bring a
            // second copy of every problem, and the report would double with nothing looking wrong.
            if f.path == "server.log" and not scanned {
                scanned = true
                seen = 0

                target lines(f.text) as l {
                    // The condition the sketch could not have grown into: mentions ERROR, but is not
                    // the test line. A second clause costs a word here and a new keyword there.
                    if contains(l, "ERROR") and not contains(l, "test") {
                        seen = seen + 1
                        bring Problem(Index + 1, l) as e

                        // And a decision made once, remembered as a mark rather than re-tested later.
                        if startsWith(l, "FATAL") { mark e #Fatal }
                    }

                    // `startsWith` on a whole word, which `l[0] == "F"` cannot do.
                    if startsWith(l, "FATAL") {
                        seen = seen + 1
                        bring Problem(Index + 1, l) as fatal
                        mark fatal #Fatal
                    }
                }

                emit @Scanned { count: seen }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- 4. read the findings back ----------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Report {
        hear @Scanned as s {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "log: " + s.count + " line(s) worth keeping" }

            target $Problem #Problem as p {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  line " + p.Problem.n + ": " + p.Problem.text }
            }

            // The reason marking beat re-testing: this query repeats no condition.
            target $Problem #Fatal as p {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  FATAL at line " + p.Problem.n }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- 5. reading the edited file back, to prove the write --------------------------------------

    shard Verify {
        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileWritten as w {
            if w.path == "config.txt" { emit *Vein.Files.Io.@ReadFile { path: w.path } }
            if w.path == "server.log" { emit *Vein.Files.Io.@ReadFile { path: w.path } }
        }

        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileMissing as m {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "could not use " + m.path + ": " + m.reason }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/file_tags.vein`

**Route files by tag, not path** — Writes five files (a config, a log and three computed save slots), each carrying a tag. One @FileLoaded handler routes on `match f.tag` instead of comparing paths. Save slots with computed tags land in the else arm, become #Save identities and are reported once all three are in.  
Run: `veinc run samples/file_tags.vein --ticks 6`  
Teaches: the tag field on file events, echoed back untouched · a mark in value position evaluates to its own name · match / when / else checked against declared marks · computed string tags ("slot" + n) · the failure path (@FileMissing) routes through the same match  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 29  
Notes: Running it writes samples/out/conf/app.conf, samples/out/logs/server.log and samples/out/saves/slot1-3.dat. Relative paths resolve against the .vein file's folder, and samples/out/ is gitignored. Everything is event-driven, so the output is the same with or without `--ticks 6`. Matches RULES.md 28c.

Real output of `veinc run samples/file_tags.vein --ticks 6`:
```text
config: fullscreen = false
log line 2: ERROR disk full
log line 3: FATAL out of memory
save 1: level=3
save 2: level=6
save 3: level=9
```

```vein
// file_tags.vein — many files, two handlers. The `tag` field, and why routing by path does not scale.
//
//   veinc run samples/file_tags.vein --ticks 6
//
// THE PROBLEM THIS SOLVES. `hear @FileLoaded` fires for EVERY file the program ever reads. With one
// file that is invisible. With nineteen it is the whole design:
//
//     hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileLoaded as f {
//         if f.path == "conf/app.conf"    { … }        // nineteen of these
//         if f.path == "logs/server.log"  { … }        // each one a string that must be spelled right
//         if f.path == "saves/slot1.dat"  { … }        // in two places, forever
//         …
//     }
//
// Nothing there fails loudly. A typo means the block never runs; a path that changes on Windows means it
// never runs on Windows; a file renamed means every handler that mentioned it has to be found. And the
// handler is keyed on WHERE the file lives, when what it actually wants to know is WHAT the read was for.
//
// THE FIX IS ONE FIELD. `@ReadFile` carries a `tag`, and the runtime hands it straight back on
// @FileLoaded / @FileMissing / @FileWritten without looking at it. So the request says what it is for:
//
//     emit @ReadFile { path: settingsPath, tag: #Config }
//
// and the handler routes on that instead of on a path:
//
//     match f.tag { when #Config { … } when #Log { … } }
//
// A mark in value position evaluates to its own name, so `#Config` at the emit and `when #Config` at the
// handler are the same string with no quoting at either end — and `when` is checked against declared
// marks, so a misspelled tag is a compile error rather than a block that silently never runs.
//
// WHY THE FIELD IS A STRING AND NOT A MARK TYPE. Because tags are often computed. Three save slots — or
// nineteen — want `"slot" + n`, which no mark can spell. A string takes both, and `#Config` still works,
// since that is exactly what a mark evaluates to.
//
// WHAT THE TAG IS NOT. It is not a handle and it is not an identity: nothing is allocated, nothing has to
// be closed, and two reads may share a tag on purpose (that is how "all nineteen configs" is written).
// `f.path` is still there when a handler wants to know which file it actually got.

bundle FileTags by you {

    // The tags. Declaring them is what makes `when #Config` checkable — an undeclared mark is VS0210,
    // so the compiler catches the typo that a path comparison would have swallowed.
    mark #Config
    mark #Log
    mark #Save

    // One save slot read back, so the report can be sorted and counted like anything else.
    shape $Slot { n: int, text: string }
    mark #Slot
    builder Slot { $Slot   mark #Slot }

    // Fired when the last slot has been read back, so the report runs once and at the right moment.
    event @SavesLoaded { count: int }

    // ---- 1. write the five files ------------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // The write carries a tag too, so step 2 can route the confirmations without a second set of path
    // comparisons.

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Files.Io.@WriteFile {
                path: "out/conf/app.conf", append: false, tag: #Config,
                text: "fullscreen = false\nvolume = 80\n"
            }

            emit *Vein.Files.Io.@WriteFile {
                path: "out/logs/server.log", append: false, tag: #Log,
                text: "INFO  started\nERROR disk full\nFATAL out of memory\n"
            }

            // THE COMPUTED TAG, and the reason the field is a string. Three here; the same three lines
            // write nineteen, and not one handler below changes.
            repeat 3 as i {
                emit *Vein.Files.Io.@WriteFile {
                    path: "out/saves/slot" + (i + 1) + ".dat",
                    append: false,
                    tag: "slot" + (i + 1),
                    text: "level=" + ((i + 1) * 3) + "\n"
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- 2. ask for them back ---------------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // ONE handler for five files. It does not name a single path: the tag it hands to @ReadFile is the
    // one that came back on @FileWritten, so a file whose location changes changes only in Seed.

    shard Ask {
        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileWritten as w {
            emit *Vein.Files.Io.@ReadFile { path: w.path, tag: w.tag }
        }
    }

    // ---- 3. one handler, routed by what the read was for ------------------------------------------

    shard Route {
        var slots: int

        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileLoaded as f {
            match f.tag {

                when #Config {
                    target lines(f.text) as l {
                        if startsWith(l, "fullscreen") {
                            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "config: " + l }
                        }
                    }
                }

                when #Log {
                    target lines(f.text) as l {
                        if contains(l, "ERROR") or startsWith(l, "FATAL") {
                            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "log line " + (Index + 1) + ": " + l }
                        }
                    }
                }

                // The slots land here, because their tags are computed and no `when` can name them all.
                // `else` is the arm a nineteen-`if` handler never had: a file that arrives tagged with
                // something nobody wrote a case for is visible instead of silently ignored.
                else {
                    if startsWith(f.tag, "slot") {
                        slots = slots + 1
                        bring Slot(slots, trim(f.text)) as s
                        mark s #Save
                        if slots == 3 { emit @SavesLoaded { count: slots } }
                    } else {
                        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "untagged read: " + f.path }
                    }
                }
            }
        }

        // AND THE FAILURE ROUTES THE SAME WAY. This is the half a path comparison makes worst: the
        // error path needs its own nineteen guards, and it is the path nobody tests.
        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileMissing as m {
            match m.tag {
                when #Config { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "no config; using defaults" } }
                when #Log    { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "no log to scan" } }
                else         { emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "lost " + m.tag + ": " + m.reason } }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- 4. what came back ------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Report {
        hear @SavesLoaded as a {
            target $Slot #Save as s {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "save " + s.Slot.n + ": " + s.Slot.text }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/file_words.vein`

**A text file becomes identities** — Writes a small document, reads it back, and turns every line into a $Line identity and every word into a $Word identity. Headings get a #Heading mark and blank lines a #Blank mark. A later phase queries those identities to report headings, blank-line count, word count and the longest word, then writes the report to a file.  
Run: `veinc run samples/file_words.vein --ticks 3`  
Teaches: text into identities with lines() and words() · bring ... as e binds what the template built (RULES 7b) · marking a decision once instead of re-testing it · structural changes are deferred, so phases chain through events · Index in nested loops; capture the outer one in a let · query order == bring order; Entity ids  
Difficulty 4/5 · video value: core · used in lesson 30  
Notes: Running it writes samples/sample-doc.txt and samples/sample-doc.report.txt next to the source whatever the working directory, because relative paths resolve against the .vein file's folder. Neither is gitignored. The comment at lines 141-143 ('a program that cannot inspect characters') is dated: RULES 28 and lines 149-150 of the same file say line[0] works. The header's `if lines[i].startsWith("#")` remark is about method syntax, which does not exist.

Real output of `veinc run samples/file_words.vein --ticks 3`:
```text
writing sample-doc.txt…
wrote 200 bytes; reading it back…
parsed 9 line(s) of sample-doc.txt
  heading at line 1: # VeinScript
  heading at line 6: # Why identities
  40 words; longest is 'identities.'
report written to sample-doc.report.txt (284 bytes)
```

```vein
// file_words.vein — writing a file, reading it back, and turning it into identities.
//
//   veinc run samples/file_words.vein --ticks 3
//
// WHAT THIS IS FOR. A file arrives as one string, and a string is the least useful shape a document can
// have in this language. `lines` and `words` turn it into pieces, and after them a document is entities
// — which is what this language is actually good at. See samples/characters.vein for going the other
// way, down to individual characters.
//
// `target $Line #Heading` is a sentence about a document. `if lines[i].startsWith("#")` is a sentence
// about a string, and VeinScript cannot write it. That is the whole trade this sample demonstrates.
//
// LINES AND WORDS ARE BROUGHT, NOT SPAWNED BY HAND. `bring Line(n, text, words) as e` is the whole of
// `spawn` + `attach $Line` + `mark #Line` (rule 7), and each one gets an identity in the order it was
// brought — e1, e2, e3 — so the document reads back in the order it was written with nothing sorting
// it (rule 14b). The report prints the first few ids to make that visible.
//
// THE PHASES ARE NOT DECORATION. Structural changes — spawn, attach, mark — are deferred to the commit
// point at the end of the unit that made them (RULES 11), and Drain commits per EVENT (RULES 12b). So a
// handler that spawns `$Line` entities cannot then `target` them: they do not exist yet. Each phase
// therefore ends by emitting the next one, and the boundary between two events is what makes the
// previous phase's entities real:
//
//   @FileWritten  →  read it back
//   @FileLoaded   →  spawn a $Line per line and a $Word per word
//   @Parsed       →  now they exist: count, mark, and report
//   @Reported     →  write the report out
//
// Trying to do it in one handler is the mistake this ordering exists to prevent, and it fails quietly:
// the `target` simply matches nothing and you get an empty report with no error.

bundle FileWords by you {

    // ---- the document, as identities ----------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // One entity per line and one per word. That is a lot of entities for a big file, and it is the
    // point: once a line IS an identity it can be marked, folded over, and queried alongside anything
    // else — `target $Line #Heading` needs no parsing pass of its own.

    shape $Line { n: int, text: string, words: int }
    shape $Word { n: int, line: int, text: string }

    // What a line and a word ARE, as identity tags. `$Line` and `#Line` are different things sharing a
    // name — different keyword, different sigil (rule 14e) — and both are wanted: the shape is the data,
    // the mark is the membership. `target $Line #Line` reads as the sentence it is.
    mark #Line
    mark #Word

    // The templates. `bring Line(3, "…", 7)` IS `spawn` + `attach $Line` + `mark #Line` — rule 7 spells
    // that equivalence out — and writing the shorthand is the point: a document is made of lines and
    // words, so making one should be one statement naming the thing being made, not three describing
    // how it is assembled.
    //
    // THE `mark` MEMBER IS WHAT MAKES THESE IDENTITY TEMPLATES. A builder with only an include builds a
    // FRAGMENT — the include is a compile-time field expansion and attaches nothing (rule 8) — so
    // `bring Line(…) as e` is VS0221 against it, which is precisely the diagnostic that says the
    // template is not making the thing you meant.
    //
    // Arguments bind POSITIONALLY across the include, in the shape's declaration order (rule 9): `&Line`
    // takes (n, text, words), `&Word` takes (n, line, text). `veinc scaffold samples/file_words.vein
    // '&Line'` prints each slot with the shape it came from, which beats counting fields by eye.
    builder Line { $Line   mark #Line }
    builder Word { $Word   mark #Word }

    // A line that looks like a heading. Marking is the cheap way to remember a decision made once, so
    // the test that produced it does not have to be repeated at every later query.
    mark #Heading

    // A line with nothing on it. Worth marking rather than deleting: "how many blank lines" is a real
    // question about a document, and an entity that is not there cannot answer it.
    mark #Blank

    event @Parsed { path: string, lines: int }
    event @Reported { text: string }

    // ---- 1. write something to read -------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Seed {
        run once {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "writing sample-doc.txt…" }

            // `\n` is a real escape in a VeinScript string literal, so a whole document is one literal.
            // The trailing `+` continuation is the rule: the operator ends the line, never starts it.
            emit *Vein.Files.Io.@WriteFile {
                path: "sample-doc.txt",
                append: false,
                text: "# VeinScript\n" +
                      "\n" +
                      "A file is one string until you split it.\n" +
                      "Then it is identities.\n" +
                      "\n" +
                      "# Why identities\n" +
                      "A line that is an identity can be marked and queried.\n" +
                      "A line that is a string cannot even be looked at.\n"
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- 2. read it back --------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Loader {
        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileWritten as w {
            // Only the document. The report is written later by this same program, and hearing our own
            // write would read it back and parse the report as if it were the source.
            if w.path == "sample-doc.txt" {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "wrote " + w.bytes + " bytes; reading it back…" }
                emit *Vein.Files.Io.@ReadFile { path: w.path }
            }
        }

        // The failure is a MESSAGE, like @Undelivered for a send or @Failed for a request. There is no
        // block to encircle and no `catch` to write — this is what VeinScript has instead.
        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileMissing as m {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "could not use " + m.path + ": " + m.reason }
        }
    }

    // ---- 3. text into identities ------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Parser {
        var seen: int

        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileLoaded as f {
            seen = 0

            // `lines` rather than `split(f.text, "\n")`. They look equivalent and are not: a file
            // written on Windows ends every line with `\r\n`, and `split` would leave the `\r` attached
            // — so `line.text == ""` would be false for a blank line, and both sides would look
            // identical in any output printed to check. `lines` strips the ending whichever it was.
            target lines(f.text) as line {

                // `Index` is the nearest loop's 0-based counter, so this is the line number. Captured
                // into a `let` because the nested loop below rebinds `Index` to its own position.
                let number = Index + 1
                seen = seen + 1

                // `as e` binds what the template built (rule 7b), which is what lets the marks below
                // refer to it. Without `as` the identity lives in a local the program never sees, and
                // `spawn`/`attach` by hand was the only way to name it.
                //
                // `words` rather than `split(line, " ")`: on "a  b" split gives three pieces with an
                // empty middle, and a program that cannot inspect characters has no honest way to tell
                // that empty apart from a real word afterwards.
                bring Line(number, line, len(words(line))) as e

                if line == "" { mark e #Blank }

                // A heading is recognised by its first WORD being exactly "#", which is why the sample
                // document writes `# VeinScript` with a space. That is a CHOICE, not a limit: `line[0]
                // == "#"` works and would catch `#VeinScript` too (RULES 28). Matching the word keeps
                // this loop working in `words` alone, and a `#` with no space after it is a different
                // convention worth not silently accepting.
                target words(line) as w {
                    let position = Index + 1
                    bring Word(position, number, w)

                    // No `as` here: nothing needs to refer to the word afterwards. The mark goes on
                    // `e`, the LINE from the enclosing loop — a heading is a property of the line, and
                    // its first word is only the evidence.
                    if position == 1 {
                        if w == "#" { mark e #Heading }
                    }
                }
            }

            // The phase boundary. Everything spawned above is still deferred; emitting hands control
            // back to Drain, which commits before the next event is handled.
            emit @Parsed { path: f.path, lines: seen }
        }
    }

    // ---- 4. now they exist ------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Report {
        var body: string
        var wordCount: int
        var longest: string
        var longestLen: int

        hear @Parsed as p {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "parsed " + p.lines + " line(s) of " + p.path }

            body = "report for " + p.path + "\n" +
                   "lines: " + p.lines + "\n"

            // Headings. The mark was decided once while parsing; this query does not repeat the test.
            body = body + "\nheadings:\n"
            target $Line #Heading as l {
                body = body + "  line " + l.Line.n + ": " + l.Line.text + "\n"
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  heading at line " + l.Line.n + ": " + l.Line.text }
            }

            // The identities themselves. `Entity` is the nearest entity's id, and the ids are in BRING
            // ORDER: a query yields ascending by id, ids are handed out by spawn, and `bring` spawns —
            // so query order == spawn order == the order the lines were brought (rule 14b). The document
            // reads back in the order it was written without anything sorting it.
            //
            // THE IDS ARE NOT CONSECUTIVE PER LINE, and the gaps are the point: `e1  e4  e5` for the
            // first three lines, because line 1's two words took e2 and e3 between them. One counter
            // hands out ids across every template, so the sequence records the order things were MADE —
            // a line, then its words, then the next line. Sorting is still by id and still correct;
            // nothing here needs the numbers to be dense.
            body = body + "\nline identities, in bring order:\n"
            target $Line #Line as l {
                if l.Line.n < 4 {
                    body = body + "  e" + Entity + "  line " + l.Line.n + ": " + l.Line.text + "\n"
                }
            }

            // Blank lines counted rather than listed — the count is the interesting part, and it is only
            // answerable because a blank line is still an entity.
            var blanks: int
            blanks = 0
            target $Line #Blank as b { blanks = blanks + 1 }
            body = body + "\nblank lines: " + blanks + "\n"

            // Every word, for the totals. One entity per word is a lot of entities and it is what makes
            // "the longest word" a query rather than a parsing pass.
            wordCount = 0
            longest = ""
            longestLen = 0
            target $Word as w {
                wordCount = wordCount + 1
                if len(w.Word.text) > longestLen {
                    longestLen = len(w.Word.text)
                    longest = w.Word.text
                }
            }

            body = body + "words: " + wordCount + "\n" +
                          "longest word: " + longest + " (" + longestLen + ")\n"

            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "  " + wordCount + " words; longest is '" + longest + "'" }
            emit @Reported { text: body }
        }
    }

    // ---- 5. write the report back out -------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Writer {
        hear @Reported as r {
            emit *Vein.Files.Io.@WriteFile { path: "sample-doc.report.txt", text: r.text, append: false }
        }

        hear *Vein.Files.Io.@FileWritten as w {
            if w.path == "sample-doc.report.txt" {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "report written to " + w.path + " (" + w.bytes + " bytes)" }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/db_query.vein`

**PostgREST queries against Supabase** — Builds six Supabase/PostgREST query URLs (basic select, filters, set membership, OR, paging, and an embedded join), fires them all at once through the stdlib *Vein.Rest.Db.get, and prints the rows from each reply. The joined query's nested comments are walked with a nested target.  
Run: `veinc run samples/db_query.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: a REST call is emit @Fetch / hear @Fetched (via the stdlib SF Db.get) · announcing a connection once with bring *Vein.Rest.Db.&Connect · telling concurrent replies apart by f.url · 4xx/5xx arrive on @Fetched; @Failed means no answer at all · fromJson lists and a nested target over row.comments · fn helpers returning strings  
Difficulty 3/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Not run: needs a network and real Supabase credentials, and `veinc run` keeps pumping stdin until EOF. Stdlib verified: stdlib/Rest.vein defines builder Connect { $Connection } (emits @Connect), SF get(root, key, path) and Status.ok/clientError. The SF parameter is named `root` because `base` is a keyword in expression position.

```vein
// db_query.vein — the PostgREST query format, which is how you ask Supabase for a subset.
//
// samples/db_supabase.vein reads a whole table and inserts a row. This one is about the URL: filtering,
// set membership, OR across columns, paging, and pulling a related table in with the parent. In
// PostgREST there is no query language and no request body — the whole question is the query string, so
// learning the format IS learning the API.
//
//   1. Put your project URL and anon key in site() and key() below.
//   2. Two tables:
//        create table tasks (id bigserial primary key, title text, rank int, done bool);
//        create table comments (id bigserial primary key, task_id bigint references tasks, body text);
//   3. veinc run samples/db_query.vein
//
// THE CONNECTION IS DECLARED ONCE. `bring *Vein.Rest.Db.&Connect(site(), key())` announces it, and every
// shard that cares hears @Connect and reads `c.base`/`c.key` off the event. That is the point of the
// builder: the two values are written in one place, and a shard added later needs no argument threaded
// to it. Nothing forces this — Db.get takes root and key directly — but a program with several query
// shards would otherwise repeat them in each.
//
// FILTER VALUES HERE ARE ALL LITERALS, and that is a rule. Percent-encoding is defined over BYTES, and
// `code(c)` gives a UTF-16 code unit — so `é` would be encoded as `%E9` rather than `%C3%A9`, and a
// value from a search box containing a space, an `&` or a `#` would silently produce a different query
// than the one you meant. Interpolate only values you control; filter anything user-supplied in the `if`
// inside the target loop instead, where no encoding is involved.
//
// WHAT YOU CANNOT READ BACK. `Prefer: count=exact` makes PostgREST report the total row count in a
// `Content-Range` RESPONSE header — and @Fetched carries url, status and body, not headers. So the
// count is unreachable from here, and paging below is "give me this window" without a total to page
// against. That is a real limit of the event, not of the service.
//
// INTERPRETER ONLY, like every emit/hear program: `veinc emit` prints a note per skipped construct and
// this file is not in tools/check-backend.sh. It is covered by NetHttpTests, which answers each query
// through NetHttp.Hook and asserts the exact paths this file asks for.

bundle DbQuery by you {

    fn site() return string { return "https://YOUR-PROJECT.supabase.co" }
    fn key() return string { return "PASTE-YOUR-ANON-KEY" }

    fn tasks() return string { return "/rest/v1/tasks" }

    // ---- the query format, one idea per function -------------------------------------------------
    //
    // Every path is `?column=operator.value`, joined with `&`. The operator is part of the VALUE, not a
    // separate symbol — `rank=gte.3`, never `rank>=3` — which is what lets the whole query survive as an
    // ordinary URL with no escaping of its own.

    // `select` picks columns. Asking for `*` when you want three of them is the most common waste in a
    // PostgREST app: the row still crosses the network in full. `order` is the cheapest place to sort,
    // because Postgres has an index and this program does not.
    fn qBasic() return string {
        return tasks() + "?select=id,title,rank&order=rank.asc"
    }

    // Comparison operators: eq, neq, gt, gte, lt, lte. Several filters on one URL are ANDed.
    fn qFilter() return string {
        return tasks() + "?select=id,title,rank&rank=gte.3&done=is.false&order=rank.asc"
    }

    // `in.(…)` is set membership, and `not.` negates any operator — so `rank=not.in.(1,2)` is the
    // complement of this. Note the parentheses are literal and need no encoding.
    fn qInSet() return string {
        return tasks() + "?select=id,title,rank&rank=in.(1,3)&order=rank.asc"
    }

    // OR is not a filter on a column, so it does not follow the `column=` shape: it is its own parameter
    // holding a comma-separated list of conditions. `and=(…)` exists too, for nesting inside an or.
    fn qOr() return string {
        return tasks() + "?select=id,title,rank&or=(rank.eq.1,done.is.true)&order=rank.asc"
    }

    // Paging. ORDER IS NOT OPTIONAL HERE, even though the syntax allows leaving it out: without it
    // Postgres may return rows in any order it likes, so page 2 can repeat or skip rows from page 1.
    // A limit without an order is a bug that only shows up under load.
    fn qPage() return string {
        return tasks() + "?select=id,title,rank&order=rank.asc&limit=2&offset=1"
    }

    // The join. A related table goes INSIDE the select as `table(columns)`, and PostgREST answers with
    // the child rows nested in each parent as an array — which `fromJson` gives back as a list, so
    // `target row.comments as cm` walks it with no unpacking step. The foreign key is what makes this
    // work; there is no `on` clause to write.
    fn qEmbed() return string {
        return tasks() + "?select=id,title,comments(id,body)&order=rank.asc"
    }

    fn embedUrl() return string { return site() + qEmbed() }

    // ---- announce the connection -----------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            bring *Vein.Rest.Db.&Connect(site(), key())
        }
    }

    // ---- ask ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Queries {
        hear *Vein.Rest.Db.@Connect as c {
            // Six requests, all in flight at once. They complete in whatever order the service answers,
            // which is why every reply carries the url that caused it — see below.
            *Vein.Rest.Db.get(c.base, c.key, qBasic())
            *Vein.Rest.Db.get(c.base, c.key, qFilter())
            *Vein.Rest.Db.get(c.base, c.key, qInSet())
            *Vein.Rest.Db.get(c.base, c.key, qOr())
            *Vein.Rest.Db.get(c.base, c.key, qPage())
            *Vein.Rest.Db.get(c.base, c.key, qEmbed())
        }
    }

    // ---- read the flat answers ---------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Rows {
        hear *Vein.Net.Http.@Fetched as f {

            // A 4xx or 5xx arrives HERE, not on @Failed — the service answered, and what it answered is
            // this program's business. PostgREST error bodies are unusually good: a bad column name
            // comes back as a message naming the column, so printing the body is worth more than
            // printing the status.
            if not *Vein.Rest.Status.ok(f.status) {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "" }
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: f.status + " on " + f.url }
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + f.body }
            } else {
                // The embedded query has nested rows and gets its own reader below. `f.url` is how one
                // handler tells six requests apart — the reply names the question, because several are
                // outstanding and they do not come back in the order they were sent.
                if not (f.url == embedUrl()) {
                    let rows = fromJson(f.body)
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "" }
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: f.url }
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + len(rows) + " rows" }
                    target rows as row {
                        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                            text: "     " + row.id + "  rank " + row.rank + "  " + row.title
                        }
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- read the joined answer --------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Embedded {
        hear *Vein.Net.Http.@Fetched as f {
            if f.url == embedUrl() {
                if *Vein.Rest.Status.ok(f.status) {
                    let rows = fromJson(f.body)
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "" }
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- tasks with their comments --" }
                    target rows as row {
                        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + row.id + "  " + row.title }
                        // `comments` is a list on the parent row, nested by the service. A task with
                        // none gets an empty array rather than a missing field, so this loop simply
                        // runs zero times — no null check to write.
                        target row.comments as cm {
                            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "        " + cm.id + ": " + cm.body }
                        }
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- and when there was no answer at all -------------------------------------------------------

    shard Trouble {
        hear *Vein.Net.Http.@Failed as f {
            // A different thing from a 4xx: nothing replied. Bad host, refused connection, timeout.
            // There is no body to read and no status to branch on — retrying is the only response.
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "no answer from " + f.url + ": " + f.reason }
        }
    }
}
```

## `samples/db_supabase.vein`

**Supabase read and write** — Holds Supabase credentials as a $Connection identity, GETs a tasks table, and turns each returned row into a $Task identity. It then lists the tasks by rank and POSTs a new $Draft row serialised with toJson. All of it is @Fetch/@Fetched events; there's no database driver.  
Run: `veinc run samples/db_supabase.vein` _(interactive — needs input, a second process, a network or a browser)_  
Teaches: credentials as an identity (&Connection) versus an announcement event (&Connect) · fromJson list plus ascription `target rows as row: $Task` · emit a follow-up event (@Loaded) because brings are not yet committed · an event that includes a stdlib shape (*Vein.Rest.Db.$Connection) · a separate $Draft shape for rows without a server-assigned id · toJson(d.Draft) for a POST body; Status.ok / clientError  
Difficulty 4/5 · video value: good  
Notes: Not run: needs a network and a real Supabase project, and `veinc run` pumps stdin. The comment at lines 101-103 contradicts the current RULES.md 12c (nested target bindings are now both readable), so the stated reason for carrying credentials on @Loaded is outdated, though the design still works. Line 217 ('VeinScript cannot find a character to escape') is also dated given RULES 28 (s[0], chars()).

```vein
// db_supabase.vein — a database read and write, against a real Supabase project.
//
// Supabase's REST API is PostgREST: every table is a URL, a row is a JSON object, and a filter is a
// query parameter. Nothing here is Supabase-shaped except the two auth headers and the `?select=` —
// point the Connection in shard Boot at any PostgREST service, or at any JSON API at all, and the rest
// is the same program.
//
//   1. Put your project URL and anon key in the `bring Connection(…)` in shard Boot below.
//   2. Make a table:  create table tasks (id bigserial primary key, title text, rank int, done bool);
//   3. veinc run samples/db_supabase.vein
//
// It stays open after printing — `veinc run` pumps stdin until EOF, and the reply arrives on that loop.
// Ctrl-C, or Ctrl-Z then Enter, ends it. `--ticks` does nothing useful here: ticks run back to back with
// no delay, so no number of them waits for a network reply.
//
// THE KEY IN THIS FILE IS A PLACEHOLDER AND SHOULD STAY ONE. An anon key is public by design — it is
// safe in a browser — but it is public only in combination with the row-level security policies you set
// on the table. Committing a real one to a repo alongside a table with RLS off publishes the table. The
// service_role key is never safe to commit anywhere.
//
// WHY THIS IS ONE PROGRAM AND NOT A DRIVER. There is no database connection here, no pool, no
// transaction: the whole conversation is `@Fetch` and `@Fetched`, the events stdlib/Net.vein already
// declares. That is the shape a REST service actually has, and it is why the same three shards below
// would talk to Stripe or GitHub with a different URL and a different header.
//
// INTERPRETER ONLY. `emit`/`hear` is the half the C# backend deliberately skips, so `veinc emit` on this
// file prints a note per skipped construct. It is not in tools/check-backend.sh, for the same reason
// samples/rows_in_order.vein is not. What IS checked is NetHttpTests, which drives this exact request
// shape through NetHttp.Hook with no network — see `Vein_Rest_composes_the_header_string`.

bundle DbSupabase by you {

    // ---- configuration ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // The credentials are an IDENTITY, not two functions returning strings. `bring Connection(…)` in
    // shard Boot below attaches *Vein.Rest.Db's `$Connection` and marks it #Connection, so any shard in
    // any later phase reaches them with a query:
    //
    //     target $Connection #Connection as c { … c.Connection.base … }
    //
    // That is docs/RULES.md rule 7, and it is worth the two extra lines: the credentials become
    // something the program HAS rather than a value threaded through every call. A shard added later
    // asks for them instead of having them passed in, and swapping projects is one `bring`.
    //
    // The paths below stay plain functions, because they are LITERALS rather than data — nothing
    // attaches them, nothing queries them, and there is no second copy to keep in step.

    fn tablePath() return string { return "/rest/v1/tasks" }

    // `order=rank.asc` is the FIRST place to impose an order, and the cheapest: Postgres has an index and
    // this program does not. `by Task.rank` on the query below is the second place, and it is here as
    // well — deliberately, because the two guard different things. The URL decides which rows arrive; the
    // query decides what this program does with them, and it stays right if the URL is ever edited.
    //
    // There is a third, for when the source cannot sort — a fixed JSON body, a service with no `order`
    // parameter, or a key the database does not have a column for. Wrap the ingest loop:
    //
    //     ordered by rank { target rows as row: $Task { bring Task(row.id, row.title, row.rank, row.done) } }
    //
    // That sorts the BRINGS, so the identities are spawned in order and even a reader that cannot sort
    // — a fragment builder emitting @Html — comes out right. See samples/entities_bring_rows.vein.
    // It is not used here because `order=rank.asc` already did the work, on a machine with an index.
    // See docs/SYNTAX-DECISIONS.md D14.
    fn selectPath() return string { return tablePath() + "?select=id,title,rank,done&order=rank.asc" }

    // NO QUERY PARAMETER IS BUILT FROM DATA HERE, and that is a rule rather than a convenience. There is
    // no percent-encoding in VeinScript: encoding is defined over BYTES and `code(c)` gives a UTF-16
    // code unit, so anything non-ASCII would be encoded wrongly and a filter built from a title, a
    // search box or a name would break on the first space or `&`. Filter in the `if` inside the target
    // loop, or add the encoded parameter from the host. stdlib/Rest.vein says the same thing, and
    // refuses to offer a `query(url, key, value)` for exactly this reason.

    // ---- talking to it -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // *Vein.Rest.Db.get/post carry the double-header auth (apikey + Authorization: Bearer, the classic
    // "sent only one, got a 401 that names neither" mistake) and, for post, `Prefer: return=representation`
    // — see stdlib/Rest.vein for why both exist. The connection identity is the only thing this program
    // supplies; everything past the colon is the same for any PostgREST-style API.

    // ---- what a row is ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shape $Task { id: int, title: string, rank: int, done: bool }
    mark #Task

    builder Task { $Task   mark #Task }

    // A DRAFT IS A DIFFERENT SHAPE, and the missing `id` is the whole reason. Postgres assigns the id, so
    // a row on its way IN does not have one — and `toJson` writes every field its shape declares, so
    // reusing $Task would post `"id": 0` and either collide with an existing row or be rejected.
    shape $Draft { title: string, rank: int, done: bool }
    mark #Draft

    builder Draft { $Draft   mark #Draft }

    // Raised once the rows from the SELECT are committed, so anything that needs to QUERY them has an
    // event to hang off. Not decoration: `bring` inside a `hear` commits at the END of that event, so a
    // target loop in the same handler would see nothing (docs/RULES.md 12b).
    //
    // It carries the connection by INCLUDING the stdlib's shape rather than re-declaring `base`/`key`,
    // which is what `$Include` is for — one declaration, expanded into this event's fields at compile
    // time, so a change there reaches here. That also solves a real constraint: shard Write below needs
    // the credentials AND a `target $Draft` loop, and a `target` nested inside another silently reads
    // the OUTER binding as empty (docs/RULES.md 12c). Handing them along on the event means Write needs
    // only one loop.
    event @Loaded { count: int, *Vein.Rest.Db.$Connection }

    // ---- the connection --------------------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // BOTH of *Vein.Rest.Db's builders, because they answer different questions and this program asks
    // both. `Connection` is the identity: it persists, so the insert phase further down can look the
    // credentials up long after this shard has finished. `Connect` is the event: it fans out once and
    // is gone, which is exactly right for "start the first query now".
    //
    // The kickoff is an EVENT and not a `settled` block on purpose. `veinc run` defaults to `--ticks 0`,
    // so `settled` would never run at all and the program would sit there having asked nothing.

    shard Boot {
        run once {
            bring *Vein.Rest.Db.&Connection("https://YOUR-PROJECT.supabase.co", "PASTE-YOUR-ANON-KEY")
            bring *Vein.Rest.Db.&Connect("https://YOUR-PROJECT.supabase.co", "PASTE-YOUR-ANON-KEY")
        }
    }

    // One draft, brought at boot so it is committed long before the insert wants to read it.
    shard Compose {
        run once { bring Draft("write the docs", 9, false) }
    }

    // ---- read ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Load {
        // The event carries the credentials in its own payload, so this reads `c.base` — a plain field
        // on the event — rather than querying for them. No `target` needed when the announcement itself
        // is what woke you up.
        hear *Vein.Rest.Db.@Connect as c {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "GET " + c.base + selectPath() }
            *Vein.Rest.Db.get(c.base, c.key, selectPath())
        }
    }

    // ---- the replies -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
    //
    // @Fetched fires for EVERY request this program makes, so the handler has to say which one it is
    // looking at. `f.url` is on the reply for exactly this — several requests can be in flight and they
    // complete out of order.

    shard Replies {
        hear *Vein.Net.Http.@Fetched as f {

            // The guard that is easy to forget: a 401, a 404 and a 500 all arrive here as @Fetched,
            // because the service ANSWERED. Parsing without checking would hand `fromJson` an error
            // object and quietly produce zero rows.
            if not *Vein.Rest.Status.ok(f.status) {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- " + f.status + " --" }
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "   " + f.body }
                if *Vein.Rest.Status.clientError(f.status) {
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                        text: "   a 401 here almost always means the Connection in shard Boot is still"
                    }
                    emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                        text: "   the placeholder, and a 404 means the table is not named `tasks`."
                    }
                }
            } else {
                // WHICH request was this? The select and the insert both land here, so the handler asks
                // the connection identity what the select's URL was. This is the payoff of holding the
                // credentials rather than passing them: a shard that was never handed them can still
                // reconstruct the URL. A `hear` binding stays readable inside a `target` loop, so `f`
                // means what it says in here.
                target $Connection #Connection as c {
                    if f.url == c.Connection.base + selectPath() {
                        // PostgREST answers a select with a bare JSON ARRAY, which `fromJson` returns as
                        // a list — the same thing `target … as x` already walks and `len` already
                        // measures. No conversion step, and no shape declared for the wire format.
                        let rows = fromJson(f.body)
                        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "read " + len(rows) + " rows" }

                        target rows as row: $Task {
                            bring Task(row.id, row.title, row.rank, row.done)
                        }

                        // Handed on rather than listed here: the brings above are not committed yet.
                        emit @Loaded { count: len(rows), base: c.Connection.base, key: c.Connection.key }
                    } else {
                        emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "wrote: " + f.body }
                    }
                }
            }
        }

        // @Failed is the OTHER outcome, and it is a different thing entirely: no reply at all — bad host,
        // refused connection, timeout. Nothing to parse, and retrying is the only sensible response.
        hear *Vein.Net.Http.@Failed as f {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "no answer from " + f.url + ": " + f.reason }
        }
    }

    // ---- what arrived ----------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Listing {
        hear @Loaded as l {
            emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "-- " + l.count + " tasks, by rank --" }
            target $Task #Task by Task.rank as t {
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print {
                    text: "   " + t.Task.rank + "  " + t.Task.title + "  done=" + t.Task.done
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // ---- write -----------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    shard Write {
        hear @Loaded as l {
            // `toJson` of a COMPONENT, not of hand-written JSON. It escapes what JSON requires — a
            // quote, a newline, a tab inside a title — and a program concatenating its own braces cannot,
            // because VeinScript cannot find a character to escape. PostgREST takes an array of rows, so
            // the object goes inside brackets.
            target $Draft #Draft as d {
                let payload = "[" + toJson(d.Draft) + "]"
                emit *Vein.Console.Io.@Print { text: "POST " + l.base + tablePath() + " " + payload }
                *Vein.Rest.Db.post(l.base, l.key, tablePath(), payload)
            }
        }
    }
}
```
